# Changelog

Vilan is a fast-moving alpha. Minor versions (`0.X`) may change the
language, the standard library, and the wire protocol without a
deprecation period; patch versions are fixes. Each release below links
the highlights — the [book](https://vilan-lang.org/docs/) always
tracks the latest state.

below show the spelling). `scripts/cut-release.sh` orders the section by it at

proposal/releases.md §7.2 step 3 defines the four.

ONE BOLD HEAD PER ENTRY. The script reads every line that STARTS with `**` as

carrying no family marker, and the whole section is refused. Sub-heads read

## Unreleased

<!-- family: tooling -->
**An element head's closing `>` keeps its highlight when it lands on a line of its own: the parse now records the element's angle brackets and the language server paints them, so the shape a head is written in cannot change what it looks like.** Spread a wide head's attributes one per line and the `>` that closes it ends up on a line with no `<div` in front of it — where it lost its color entirely, or rather gained the wrong one. Both suspects the report named were real and only one of them was the bug. The TextMate grammar's element rule is a single-line `match` (`(</?)(tag)\s*(/?>)?`), and a TextMate `match` is applied one line at a time, so a `>` that has left the tag's line is out of its reach by construction — it fell through to the operator list and painted as a comparison. The semantic-token pass could not compensate because it never covered element punctuation at all: `ElementBody` carried the tag NAME's span and the close tag's, and nothing said where the brackets were, so `<`, `>`, `</` and `/>` were the grammar's alone. **The parser now records them** — the opening `<`, the `>` or `/>` that ends the head, and a close tag's `</` and `>` — at the four places it consumes them, which is the only place their positions are known; the editor paints all four as the tag they belong to. That is a property of the parse rather than of the line, so a one-line head and a multi-line head produce the same tokens, in the same order, with the same kinds, which is exactly what the new pin asserts (proven red by dropping the emission). The grammar gets the narrow companion rule for the case it can still see — a `>` or `/>` alone on its line is a head terminator, and the formatter breaks after a binary operator and never before one, so no formatted line begins with a bare comparison `>` — because the grammar is what colors the file when the server is not running. (tracker E115, the owner's report from kolt's multi-line heads)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Writing the `import pkg::…` that reaches a file now re-colors that file in the editor, no restart: a `pkg::` graph edit sweeps every open document in the package, and a saved `vilan.toml` sweeps them again — which it had silently stopped doing.** E113 gave an unreferenced file the designated entry's platform, which is the right fallback and is where the owner's report starts: open a browser-only module nothing imports yet, watch it analyze as process (`struct 'View' has no field 'element'` — the process `std::ui` twin), add the import in the entry, and the error stayed until the language server was restarted. Reachability is computed per analysis and is not cached, so the color was never stale — the file was simply never re-analyzed. The sweep after an edit is gated on the DEPENDENCY edge (B39a, which took the largest fixed cost out of every typing pause), and that gate is exactly right for diagnostics and exactly backwards for color: a file's platform is decided by which entry REACHES it, so the edit that re-colors `widget.vl` is written in the file that imports it, and `widget.vl` depends on nothing. The edge found nothing to sweep, forever. An analysis now records which package modules it reached, and when that set MOVES the whole package is re-analyzed and re-published — every open document under the same `pkg::` root, dependency edge or not. The gate stays narrow where it should: a body edit, a rename, a new `std` import all leave the reach identical and take the old path, so the sweep does not widen for the typing that makes up nearly all of it. **The manifest half was worse and is fixed in the same stroke.** `vilan.toml` is in no program's `canonical_sources`, so once the sweep was gated on the edge a manifest SAVE re-analyzed nothing at all — a changed `target`, a new `[entry.…]`, a `default-entry` moved, a dependency finally fetched: none of it reached the editor until a restart, and the code comment promising that a manifest save "re-analyzes every open document" had quietly become false. A saved manifest now sweeps every open document whose package root lies beneath it. Three pins, the two handler-driven ones proven red against the widened sweep removed: the import is added and the error clears, the import is deleted and the process color comes back (a fix that only ever re-colored toward browser passes the first and fails the second), and the decision itself — an edit that does not move the graph re-colors nothing. (tracker E116, the owner's report; E113's follow-up)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A ghost diagnostic no longer flashes back after a comment/uncomment round trip: every analysis now carries the world revision it READ, and a result older than the one already landed is dropped instead of published.** Comment an important line, uncomment it, and an error from the commented state reappeared for a moment — the owner's report, and the class is an ordering hole rather than the slowness that made it visible. The server had two guards against an out-of-order result and both were text comparisons: the debounce generation, checked before an analysis starts, and `land`'s "is this analysis of my own current text", checked after it finishes. Text is exactly what the affected document does not change. An edit in one file re-analyzes every OPEN file that imports it, and a DEPENDENT's own buffer never moves — so its two in-flight analyses, the one that read the module commented out and the one that read it restored, are byte-identical to the live text, both pass the guard, and both land, in whichever order the CPU finishes them. The loser published last. The fix is the standard remedy the report names: a monotonic world revision, bumped by every notification that changes what an analysis would read (an open, an edit, a close, a save), sampled BEFORE an analysis starts and stamped onto its result. `land` then drops a result whose stamp is older than the adopted one, whatever its text says, and the publish planner drops a plan whose stamp is older than the last one it accepted for that owner — neither recorded nor sent, so the superseded view cannot even merge into a shared module's union. Older strictly: an equal stamp is a second look at one world, which is what a dependents' sweep legitimately does, and dropping it would leave the dependent stale. One further seam was closed at the same time — the planner is a mutex, so plan ORDER was always well defined, but the `publish_diagnostics` awaits that follow could still interleave and deliver the older plan last; plan and send now happen under one gate, and the analyses themselves stay fully concurrent. Both pins were proven red against the fix removed: the `land` pin lands two text-identical analyses of one document in the losing order and asserts the older is dropped, and the planner pin publishes a clean analysis and then the superseded broken one and asserts the ghost is not what the editor is left showing. (tracker E117, the owner's report; E106's slowness is what widened the window)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A release whose notes exceed GitHub's 125,000-character body cap publishes anyway: the notes keep whole entries to a safe margin and point at the changelog for the rest.** v0.40.0's section was 221,530 characters at 74 entries and the publish step's `gh release create` refused with HTTP 422 after every build job had succeeded; the section's family order (breaking, then miscompiles, first) means the entries that survive the cut are the ones a reader must see. Never a mid-entry cut.

## v0.40.0 — 2026-09-01

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`std::print` is gone, and so are the fifteen other alias-only re-exports that lived in `std/src/lib.vl`. Each name is now spelled at its real home — `std::io::print` — and the prelude carries the ergonomics the aliases existed for.** Every entry in std's package root was there for one reason: to let a caller write `std::print` instead of `std::io::print`. That is import brevity, and a package root is the wrong place to sell it — a re-export whose only job is to be shorter is a second spelling of a name with no rule for which one is canonical, which is exactly what the estate then split down the middle. With a prelude in the language the ergonomics are served properly, so the alias layer goes. **The census is the whole surface**: `lib.vl` is the only `lib.vl` in std and `export import` appears elsewhere in the package only in the two prelude modules this same order added, so six statements and sixteen names is all of it — `print`, `panic`, `assert`, `Default`, `str`, and the eleven numerics `BigInt`/`f32`/`f64`/`i8`/`i16`/`i32`/`i53`/`u8`/`u16`/`u32`/`u53`. Two findings from counting: **thirteen of the sixteen had ZERO uses in the entire estate** — nobody, in std or the corpus or the docs or the examples or kolt or the website, ever wrote `std::Default`, `std::str` or any of the numerics, and the numerics were doubly dead because they are §4.7 primitives that have been ambient with no prelude at all since before any of this; and **std never used its own aliases**, spelling every internal import `pkg::io::print`, so migrating std itself was a null change and `lib.vl` was the only std file that moved. **Each old spelling gets a curated diagnostic rather than the generic failure**, because `std::print` will be typed from muscle memory for a long time and "cannot find 'print' in the imported path" names neither the removal nor either way forward: `` `std::print` was removed: `print` is in the default prelude (no import needed), and its module path is `std::io::print` ``, with the prelude clause dropped for `panic`/`assert`/`Default` (in neither std set, so claiming no import is needed would be false) and replaced for the primitives with "is a primitive and is always in scope — delete the import". The arm fires only directly under `std`'s root, so a typo inside a real module (`std::io::prnt`) keeps the ordinary message. **The migration took two shapes and the split was deliberate.** The fixture corpora — 200 files of Rust test sources, the `vilan/test` corpus, the benchmarks — were **rewritten** to `std::io::print`, a pure textual substitution that preserves every fixture's meaning exactly and cannot depend on the prelude reaching that fixture's package; the corpus goldens are byte-identical, which is the negative this half is checked against. The teaching corpora — 47 files of doc fences, the examples, `README.md`, the CLI templates — had the import **deleted** instead, 159 lines of it, because a doc fence that imports what the prelude supplies teaches the wrong thing, and the docs gate compiling every one of those fences afterward is the prelude's best end-to-end proof. Hello-world now opens with `fun main`. Two things the sweep exposed and fixed at the root rather than around: the transformer looked `print` up in std's PACKAGE-ROOT scope, which only ever worked because the alias put it there, and now reads a `print_fn_id` the analyzer captures from `std::io` beside `panic`'s — a lang-adjacent function should say where it lives rather than ride a convenience re-export; and `std::number` and `std::string` were being loaded only as a side effect of `lib.vl` re-exporting their members for the `std::i32` / `std::str` short names, so they join `boolean`, `list` and the rest in the always-loaded core list, where the primitives they host say so themselves. **Migrating your own code**: delete `import std::print;` outright if your package uses the default prelude (the compiler will tell you, and Organize Imports now strips it for you); otherwise respell it `std::io::print`. `panic`, `assert` and `Default` are a respelling either way; the numerics and `str` are a deletion, since they were never needed. (backlog B156; proposal/prelude.md §10.2; docs `spec/names.md` §4.7, `appendix/errors.md`)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`to_lowercase_ascii` was never ASCII — the full-Unicode mapping is now spelled `to_lowercase`, and the `_ascii` pair is real. This break draws NO diagnostic: the name `to_lowercase_ascii` keeps compiling and starts meaning something narrower.** The suffix was a promise of a narrower operation than the one performed. `Intrinsic::StrToLowercaseAscii` lowered to the host's `toLowerCase` beside `to_uppercase`'s `toUpperCase`, so `"STRASSE-Ǳ".to_lowercase_ascii()` returned `strasse-ǳ`, case-mapping U+01F1 as no ASCII operation could — and the docs inherited the lie. The full-Unicode half is now named for what it is: **`to_lowercase`, the mirror of `to_uppercase`**, same lowering, same behavior, renamed at every mention including the intrinsic's own (`Intrinsic::StrToLowercase`). That half is an ordinary rename the compiler reports at the call site. **The other half is the unusual one and is worth reading twice: `to_lowercase_ascii` still compiles, still takes no argument, and still returns a `str` — it just does something different now.** A caller who wrote that name wanting the Unicode fold gets the ASCII fold, silently, with nothing to catch it: there is no spelling to flag, so no rename diagnostic can fire and no `[deprecated]` window can steer. That is acceptable here on the census and only on the census — five std call sites, all migrated by intent below, and **zero application callers** (kolt's one use died with its hand-rolled mime table in the 0.38.0 migration; the website has none). If you spelled `to_lowercase_ascii` in your own code and meant the Unicode fold, the fix is to spell `to_lowercase`; nothing will tell you to. What `_ascii` means now is exact: `to_lowercase_ascii` and `to_uppercase_ascii` shift **only** `A`–`Z`/`a`–`z` and pass every other code unit through untouched — `ß`, `Ǳ`, `İ`, `É`, accents, symbols — so the result is always the **same length** as the receiver and an index into one is an index into the other. That is what makes them the right tool for protocol text (file extensions, mime rows, HTTP header names, HTML tag names, hex), where case-insensitivity is defined over ASCII to begin with. Both are plain vilan in `string.vl` — a `code_at` loop over the always-loaded core's own primitives, no new intrinsic, no new helper — which keeps the compiler surface at the rename and makes them work under `const` for free. The full pair keeps the host's mapping and its consequences, now documented rather than discovered: it is locale-independent, non-ASCII letters map, and one-to-many mappings apply, so `"Straße".to_uppercase()` is `"STRASSE"` — seven units from a six-unit receiver, indices not surviving the call. **std's five sites migrated by intent, and two of them were latent bugs.** `markdown.vl`'s `heading_id` is the only site wanting the full fold, and only on its non-ASCII branch — mdBook's own case rule, which `école-été` is the evidence for; its `A`–`Z` branch is guarded and takes the ASCII shift. `process/build.vl`'s mime lookup, `process/http.vl`'s header-name fold and all seven of `process/document.vl`'s markup sites take `_ascii`, all protocol work. The two bugs both lived in `document.vl` and both came from the full fold. `skip_raw_text` carried a comment claiming the lowered copy shares indices with the original — false while the fold could change length (`İ` lowers to two code units), true now. And `is_name_start` — lower the character, then test `a <= c <= z` — answered TRUE for U+212A KELVIN SIGN, which the full fold lowers to a plain `k`, so a character HTML says can start no tag name opened one. Seven behavior pins in the inference suite cover the full pair's Unicode mappings and length changes, the ASCII pair's narrowness (the four letters untouched, the length preserved, the empty string, a full round-trip on mixed input), the Kelvin sign at both folds, and `heading_id`'s two branches; the four ASCII pins were proven red by planting the full-Unicode binding back into the ASCII implementations, and `heading_id`'s by planting the ASCII fold at markdown's Unicode site. No corpus golden moves. (tracker F14, from the owner's question about `to_uppercase`'s missing suffix; docs `std/strings.md`)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`std::reactive`'s `Signal` struct is renamed `SignalCell`; `Signal` is now the writable trait.** `Signal::new(v)` is unchanged and still hands back the canonical cell, so the everyday spelling and all 97 constructor sites in the estate stay exactly as written. What moves is every place a `Signal` was named as a TYPE: a struct field, a return type, a parameter, a generic argument. Those must say `SignalCell<T>`. A `let` annotation may keep saying `Signal<T>` — there a trait reads as a checked constraint (B161), so `let count: Signal<i32> = SignalCell::new(1)` compiles and `count` is still a cell. In this repository the rename moved 56 type positions across 15 corpus, example, benchmark and CLI-fixture programs, 101 in the book, and 464 in the compiler's own test fixtures; the templates needed no source change at all, because they only ever call the constructor. `std::web`'s prelude carries `SignalCell` beside `Signal` for the same reason: a trait cannot name a struct field, and a field is where an application writes the cell. Two compiler-internal readings follow the cell rather than the contract: the HMR transfer classification (a `SignalCell<T>` binding still carries its payload across a swap) and the `[service]` client's mirror element. (backlog A32)

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**A `let` or `mut` REBINDING of a closure parameter waits for the parameter's type the way a mention of it does — so it keeps a container whole, and keeps the copy it owes.** `s.set_with(|values| { mut next = values; next.push(2); next })` refused with "cannot call method 'push' on unknown", and the copy-update-return idiom is precisely what `set_with` is FOR. The cause was one resolver out of step with every other. An unannotated closure parameter reads `Unknown` until the call site infers the closure against the callee's signature and fills its slot (B13's channel); every consulting resolver — a method call, a subscript, a `for`, a `match` — recognises that `Unknown` as a "not yet" and defers on it. The VARIABLE resolver did not: it took the `Unknown` for an answer and ground the binding on it, permanently, because the fill lands afterwards. So the rebinding was `unknown` for the rest of the closure while the parameter itself typed fine — which is why the direct spelling (`values.push(2)`) always gave the right refusal and the rebinding never did. Rebindings now count as mentions: the walk that answers "is this an unfilled closure parameter?" follows a still-ungrounded binding to its initializer, and the variable resolver defers while its value is one. That is the whole fix, and it is general — nothing about `set_with`, about traits, about default bodies, or about `mut`, all of which the discrimination had pointed at. A plain `let` lost the type identically; so did a closure passed to any generic function, a `sub` observer (a trait REQUIREMENT, not a default body), an inherent `SignalCell::update` view, and a `str` parameter — the "scalars are fine" control was an illusion of numeric defaulting grounding `next + 1` by a different route. A chain of rebindings resolves through the same walk, as does one inside a nested closure. The RUNNING half is why this is a miscompile rather than a refusal: an untyped binding reads as a scalar to the emitter, so it dropped the `__clone` that a `mut` rebinding of a List, a Map or a struct owes its caller. `apply(seed, |values| { mut next = values; grow(&mut next); next })` mutated `seed` in place and printed `2 2` where the annotated spelling of the same program printed `1 2` — a clean compile, silently sharing the value it was supposed to copy. Following initializers put the walk on a graph that can cycle (`let a = b; let b = a;` is writable, module bindings resolving in any order), so bindings already stepped through end it; the two module-level-cycle pins hung without that and are green with it. Found by the website migration's clean-build gate, whose four errors were all this one bug.

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**A generic parameter on the right of a native operator is refused now, whatever it is bounded to — the last square of the `+` family, and the same hole in every one of its siblings.** `fun bump<T: Add>(total: i32, value: T): i32 { total + value }` compiled, and `bump(1, Point { x = 1, y = 2 })` printed `11,2`: a string, typed `i32`. B169 had closed the UNBOUNDED parameter in that position and B176 the bounded one on the right of a `str`, and this was the square neither covered — a bound was read as a promise worth keeping, and the pre-ruling message steered the author straight into the program that miscompiled ("Bound it (`<T: Add>`)"). What closes it is a ruling about operand ROLES rather than about generics: the `+` belongs to its LEFT operand, so the right one has to be a MEMBER of what the left's `add` accepts, and a bound can prove membership only where some trait names that set. `str`'s set has one — "everything with a string form" is `Display`, which is exactly why B176's render bound can route a bounded operand through the impl — and a number's does not: `i32`'s `add` accepts `i32`, nothing names that set, and a bound promises a trait's methods rather than a type (`Add`'s `B` defaults to `Self`, which is the parameter, not the left operand). So every generic right operand of a numeric `+` is refused, bounded or not, with a message that names the spellings that work instead of the one that does not; `total += value` rides the same check, reached through the second registration site its desugar builds. The siblings had the identical hole with quieter garbage and close with it: against a `total: i32` and a `value: T`, `total - value` and `total * value` emitted `NaN`, `total & value` emitted `0`, `total << value` emitted `total`, and `total < value` and `total == value` emitted a plausible `false` — each typed as though the operator had been checked. B170 routed every left SHAPE through the check; this routes every right one, and `str` keeps its one exception because `+` on a string is the one native operator whose admitted set a trait can name. Two things deliberately stay open. `f64 * i32` — two grounded numerics that compute a correct answer — is still admitted, because refusing it is the numeric-strictness change with a migration of its own that b148 deferred, not this miscompile fix. And the generic LEFT operand is still pinned rather than closed: a trait default body writes `self.once() + self.once()` over the trait's own parameter today, so refusing it is a bound requirement on every such declaration.

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**A `str` concatenation with a `Display`-bounded generic operand calls the impl instead of handing the value to the host's `+`.** `fun show<T: Display>(value: T): str { "v=" + value }` compiled — correctly, because `T: Display` is exactly the string form the concatenation asks for — and then emitted a raw `+`, so `show(Point { x = 1, y = 2 })` printed `v=1,2` and the `Display` impl was never called: a legal program, a clean compile, a silently wrong string. B169 refused the UNBOUNDED parameter and left the bounded one admitted, which was the right half of the rule; nothing kept the other half, because `grounded` excludes every generic type, so a bounded parameter reached neither the admitted set nor the refusal and fell through to the native emission untouched. The i-string spelling had the identical hole and was quieter about it — `i"v={value}"` *is* that concatenation — and so did `text += value`, whose desugar registers a binary of its own. The concatenation now asks the bound what it promises: a bound providing `to_string(self): str` — `Display`, a trait with `Display` as a supertrait, or a program's own — is the string form, and the site is recorded so codegen routes the operand through that impl at each monomorphization, the same channel `value.to_string()` already used. A bound promising something else (`T: Add`) promises no string form and is refused alongside the unbounded case, in wording that names the missing `to_string` rather than repeating "unbounded" at a parameter that plainly has bounds. Only the operand changes, never the `+`, which is why one rewrite covers the direct form, the hole, the compound append and every non-tail position. A dispatch that cannot be resolved is now a hard compile error rather than a fall-through to the raw `+`: emitting it would be this bug again, from a compile that reported nothing. Ten pins, seven red against the live bug and an eighth (the compound append) red against the fix re-planted — all by RUNNING the emitted JavaScript, since type-checking alone cannot see this class: the typing was right all along. Two more are controls, green throughout: a concrete struct operand is still refused and its `.to_string()` spelling still renders. No corpus golden moves. One square stays open and is pinned `#[ignore]`d: a bounded parameter on the right of a NUMERIC `+` — `fun bump<T: Add>(total: i32, value: T): i32 { total + value }` still prints `11,2` for a `Point`, a string typed as an `i32`. That one cannot be fixed by routing, because a native left operand never dispatches and no bound can promise the right operand IS `i32` (`Add`'s `B` defaults to `Self`, which is the parameter); refusing it is a ruling about what a bound may mean in that position, not a codegen fix. (tracker B176, found by Order 23's operators lane; docs `spec/types.md` and `appendix/errors.md`)

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**An unbounded generic parameter on the right of `+` is refused rather than concatenated.** `fun show<T>(value: T): str { "v=" + value }` compiled, and every instantiation printed the runtime shape — `show(Point { x = 1, y = 2 })` printed `v=1,2`, and an i-string hole spelled the same bug more quietly since `i"v={value}"` *is* that concatenation. `fun add<T>(value: T, total: i32): i32 { total + value }` was worse: it emitted the host's `+` and handed back the string `"11,2"` typed as an `i32`. The operand check b148 shipped judges a GROUNDED right operand, the leniency B24 gave the comparisons, and a parameter is not grounded — and when the check did look, `compare_type` admitted it anyway, because a parameter compares equal to whatever is asked of it. Nothing downstream would have caught it either: a declaration is checked once for all its instantiations, so this was the only place the question could be asked. It is asked now, and answered by the bounds: a parameter admits what its bounds promise, and an unbounded one promises nothing, so it is neither a string form nor the left operand's type. `T: Add` on the right of `a + b` still adds, `T: Display` and an explicit `value.to_string()` still concatenate, and the spec states the rule with both spellings. The mirror case — an unbounded parameter on the LEFT — stays open and pinned; refusing it is a bound requirement on every trait default written over the trait's own parameter, which is a change to generics rather than to `+`.

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**An operator whose left operand is a tuple, an array, a closure, a function reference or `void` is checked like every other operator now, instead of falling through to the host's.** The check b148 shipped keyed off the operand's SHAPE: the dispatch loop `continue`d unless the left operand was a struct or an enum, so those five shapes reached neither `+`'s admitted set nor the "does not implement the `Add` operator" refusal, and the anything-goes emission b148 removed survived intact for exactly them. `(1, 2) + 1` printed `1,21` — b148's own miscompile, entered from the left instead of the right — `nothing() + 1` was `NaN`, a closure concatenated its SOURCE TEXT, and `let v = if false { 1 }; v + 1` printed a plausible wrong `1` rather than anything a reader would notice. The sibling operators shared the hole and their own garbage: `(1, 2) == (1, 2)` was `false` (JS compares the references), `(1, 2) < (1, 3)` was `true` (JS compares `"1,2"` against `"1,3"`), `(1, 2) - 1` was `NaN`. Being non-nominal was never a reason to skip the check — only a reason for the impl lookup to come back empty — and it turns out not to be a reason for even that: `impl (i32, i32) with PartialEq` resolved correctly all along and was simply never reached, so it dispatches now too, and the spec says so. The shapes that still skip are the ones with no verdict to give: `any` absorbs, `never` is unreachable, and an unsettled or still-symbolic type has not decided what it is. Refusals a reader cannot act on were not worth shipping either, so `void` and function values get their reason rather than advice to write an `impl void with PartialEq`, while a tuple and an array — which really can carry one — keep the standard steer. Two shapes stay open and are pinned: an unbounded generic LEFT operand (refusing it is a bound requirement on every trait default written over the trait's own parameter, the breaking generics change b148 deferred), and an operand whose type infers as a trait rather than as the parameter it came from — std's own `List<T: Add + Default>::sum` hits that one, and the wart is in inference, not here.

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**`Option::replace` declared the value it KEEPS as a loan, so a resource moved into a slot was destroyed twice.** `external fun replace(&mut self, value: T)` spelled its new value BARE — a loan under R3, which changes no ownership — and then stored it. The consequence was visible in three lines of accepted code: `mut slot: Option<Res> = None; let r = Res { .. }; slot.replace(r);` printed the destructor twice, once for the slot and once for the caller's binding, which the compiler still believed owned a value it had handed away. The same hole left `r` READABLE after the move — `print(r.tag)` compiled, where the identically-shaped `List::push(own item: T)` has always rejected it — so the move checker was silent on a program with two owners of one resource. The honest declaration is `own value: T`, and that is the whole fix: a declaration that says what the callee does, in the one std surface where an `external` has no body for the move-clean check to read. A sweep of std for the same shape ("a bare parameter the callee stores") cleared two more and left one open: `Shared::new(value: T)` stores, but R10 refuses a `Shared<resource>` at every site, so nothing reaches it at a resource; `Context::run(self, value: T, body)` binds its value for the dynamic extent of `body` and hands ownership straight back, which is exactly what a loan says. The third, the internal `NativeMap::insert`, is genuinely broken — the caller frees the value at the insert statement while the table goes on holding it — and is pinned `#[ignore]`d rather than fixed, because the declaration fix would buy a redundant deep copy on every `Map`/`Set` insert in the language to close a hazard reachable only by importing `std::native_map` directly; the zero-cost fix is adding the internal head to R10's rejecting list, which is a ruling of its own. **With the declaration corrected, C11's temporary predicate goes back to its ruled width.** The statement-temporary rule shipped narrowed to `&` / `&mut` views and a bare `self` receiver *because* of this defect — recording a temporary in a bare parameter would have destroyed a value `replace` had stored — and the narrowing is now lifted to the rule the spec has stated all along: only `own` moves, so a resource temporary in ANY loan position is its statement's to destroy. `peek(Res { .. })` on a bare `handle: Res` parameter used to leak on every path and now releases at the statement, at a vilan function and at an `[extern]` alike. One callee is exempt, and the exemption is new spec text: a `[extern(…, retains)]` host keeps what it is handed past the call, and a temporary has no binding whose scope could hold it open, so such an argument is left to leak rather than freed under a host still reading it — the direction these rules always fail toward. Six pins, each red-first: the double destruction and the readable-after-move, the widened predicate at both callee kinds proven by re-planting the narrowing, the retains exemption proven by removing its guard, and the pre-existing `option_replace_returns_the_old_resource_for_the_caller_to_own` pin, which goes red on the widening WITHOUT the declaration fix — the two had to land together. No corpus golden moves. (backlog B153, found by Order 19's lastuse-s3 lane; docs `spec/memory.md` §6.8 and `std/option-result.md`)

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**A resource held in an `if` arm, a `match` leg, a loop body or any nested block emitted a `try` that trapped a name read after it: accepted vilan that throws `ReferenceError` the moment the scope runs — live in v0.39.0.** S3 moved disposal from scope end to last use by wrapping the statements between an acquisition and its last read in a `try`/`finally`. That region lowers to a JS block, so every `const` a statement inside it declares dies at the closing brace, and the region must therefore be WIDENED until it covers the last read of every name declared within it. That rule shipped, was pinned, and worked — at a function body's top level, and nowhere else. The widening asks the dataflow, once per statement of the region being emitted, *what names does this statement put into the block I am about to wrap?*, and the answer arrives as a map the liveness pass keyed on the OUTERMOST statement enclosing each declaration. At a body's top level a declaration's statement chain has ONE element, so outermost and direct are the same id and the lookup hits; one level down they are different ids — inside an `if` arm the chain reads `[the if, the let]`, the transformer holds the `let` and the map holds the `if` — so every lookup missed, the fixpoint never ran, and the `try` closed over a `const` whose read followed it. `if flag { let r = Res { … }; let value = 7; print(r.tag); print(value); }`, with one ordinary statement after the `if`, emitted `const value` *inside* the `try` and `console.log(value)` after it. Nothing catches this before run time: the program type-checks, the emitted JavaScript parses, and it throws `ReferenceError: value is not defined` when the arm executes. The differential that isolates it is one statement long — the SAME `if` written as the function's TAIL widens correctly, because a tail is never pushed onto the statement chain and the arm's declarations key themselves, and adding a single `print("after")` after the `if` breaks it. The fix is the keying, moved on the map's side: `declared_binding_extents` now keys the INNERMOST enclosing statement, which is the coordinate its one consumer holds at every depth. That is the side that was wrong, not a second lookup bolted beside the first, because only a statement's OWN declarations can put a name into the block the region becomes — a name declared inside a nested construct is scoped to it and cannot be read outside it — so the direct statement is the only key that ever answers, and the outermost key answered at exactly one depth by coincidence of chain length. The three shapes that were already correct stay correct and are pinned or probed as negatives: a closure body is its own walk region, so its chains start at the closure; the function body is the one-element case; and an `own` resource parameter keys nothing at all, declaring at its body's entry with an empty chain. Six pins, five of them red first with a genuine `ReferenceError` — a compile-only pin cannot see this class, since the bad JavaScript is well-formed — covering the `if` arm, the `match` leg, the loop body, a block two deep, and the tail-versus-statement differential with both spellings in one program so neither can be fixed at the other's expense; the sixth is the closure-body negative. No corpus golden moves. (tracker B159, audit run 5's F1; docs `spec/memory.md`)

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**An `if`'s arms are now unified, by the rule a `match`'s legs have always gone through: `let mixed = if c { 1 } else { "two" }` is refused instead of typing as `i32` and printing `two1`.** The arms were never checked against each other. The `if` took its type from the FIRST arm and every other arm's value flowed out unchecked, so a mismatch did not fail — it *ran*, with the binding claiming one type and holding another. `print(mixed + 1)` printed `two1`, `+` having been emitted for the type the checker believed rather than the value that arrived. It escaped through a declared return type just as readily — `fun pick(c: bool): i32 { if c { 1 } else { "two" } }` compiled and returned `"two"` — and through a concrete annotation, `let mixed: Signal<i32> = if c { Signal::new(1) } else { Other::new(2) }`, where the annotation checked the first arm and nothing checked the second. The class is the one a checker exists to refuse: an accepted program computing with the wrong type. `match` legs were already unified and reported `match legs have mismatched types`, which is what made the hole legible — the same program written two ways, one refused and one run — so the fix is not a second implementation but the SAME one: the merge that `resolve_match` performed inline is now `unify_arm_bodies`, and both constructs come through it. Stating the rule once is the point. Every property the match path had is therefore the `if`'s by construction and not by re-derivation: each arm is inferred against the whole expression's own expectation, so a return-position generic call in ANY arm binds from the declared type rather than from whatever the first arm settled on; an arm that definitely LEAVES contributes `Never`, the merge's identity, so a `ret` arm beside a value arm is not a mismatch (B124's rule, which a naive unification would have broken the moment it started reporting); and a mismatch anchors at the OFFENDING arm's tail, not at the whole `if` (E7). An `else if` chain is one construct with one arm per branch, not a nest of two-armed pairs, so the third arm unifies with the first and not merely with its neighbour — both spellings of that are pinned. The check is its own constraint rather than a line inside the inference, and it runs LAST: unlike `match`, whose resolution *types* the expression every dependent then reads, the arm check produces nothing anything consumes — the `if`'s type still comes from inference, which folds the identical merge without a voice to report with — so it can wait until every value-typing kind has landed and read settled arm types instead of deferring on each of them. An estate census ran before the fix was believed: the 124 corpus programs and every entry of every project in the estate — vilan's own examples, kolt, the website, the playground, both platforms where a package builds for two — compile byte-for-byte identically under it. **Not one program in the estate stops compiling**, which is why this is filed as a miscompile rather than a breaking change: what the fix refuses is code that ran wrongly, and no code was relying on it. Eight pins, five proven red first against the constraint removed — the `let` form, the function-tail form, a mismatch in an `else if` chain's middle arm and in its final `else`, the span, the arms-that-unify green case, the diverging-arm negative, and the `match` control that has always failed the same way. Found by the A32 lane's probes on the shipped 0.39.0 compiler, and the rule B161's ruled semantics rest on: "branches must unify to ONE type" now names a rule that exists. (tracker B163, from proposal/reactive-traits.md §8.1; docs `spec/types.md` §5.11)

**A supertrait's type argument is now substituted through a sub-trait bound: with `trait Sig<T> with Src<T>`, `fun bad<S: Sig<u32>>(s: S): str { s.get() }` is refused instead of letting a `u32` out typed as `str`.** `get` is declared by `Src`, and its `T` is `Src`'s parameter. The bound's member set was resolved by walking the trait and its supertraits for the NAME and then substituting the SUB-trait's parameters over whatever came back — parameters a supertrait's signature never mentions — so a member inherited from a supertrait was substituted with nothing at all, and the leaked parameter behaved as a wildcard that unified with whatever the call site claimed. The declared `str` was accepted, the `u32` flowed out under it, and `print(bad(C { v = 7 }) + "!")` printed `7!` with `+` emitted for the type the checker believed. In practice the hole usually surfaced one step later and much less legibly: calling a method ON the result failed with `cannot call method 'to_string' on T`, naming a parameter the author never wrote and cannot see, because that is what the call actually landed on. The boundary was exactly the supertrait path and nothing else, which is what made the diagnosis clean — the sub-trait's OWN members substituted correctly, and a bound on the supertrait DIRECTLY substituted correctly, including against std's `Source<u32>`. The fix is at the walk, not at a lookup: `trait_with_supertraits_at` now pairs every trait it reaches with the arguments it is reached WITH, substituting the sub-trait's arguments into the supertrait's WRITTEN ones, so `Sig<u32>` yields `Src<u32>` and not `Src<T>`; `method_member_in_trait_at` returns the trait that DECLARES the member alongside it; and both call paths that substitute a bound's members — the generic-bounded receiver and the trait-typed `self` inside a default body — build their substitution from the declaring trait's parameters rather than from the trait the bound happened to name. The walk order is unchanged, so which trait a member resolves to is unchanged; only its arguments are now known. Because the substitution is carried rather than reconstructed, the shapes that were never reasoned about fall out: two supertrait levels thread the argument the whole way, parameters match by POSITION so a supertrait written `trait Sig<A> with Src<A>` and used at `Sig<u32>` substitutes identically, a supertrait taking a LATER parameter (`trait Sig<A, B> with Src<B>`) takes that one, and a supertrait named at a concrete argument (`with Src<u32>`) is typed at it whatever the bound says. std is not exposed today — `Source` has no sub-trait — but `trait Signal<T> with Source<T>` is the first one the reactive arc writes, and it walked straight into this. The concrete-receiver path was probed and is NOT affected: an inherited supertrait default called on a value binds through the impl subject and always typed correctly. Eight pins, six proven red first by re-planting the sub-trait substitution — five refusals plus the green case that now resolves and runs (`s.get().to_string()` under a three-level bound, which used to be the confusing error) — and the two controls that must stay green, the direct supertrait bound and the sub-trait's own member. The whole estate compiles unchanged. Found by the A32 lane's probes on the shipped 0.39.0 compiler. (tracker B164, from proposal/reactive-traits.md §8.2; docs `spec/types.md` §5.5)

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**`"here " + point` compiled and printed `here 1,2`: `+` never typed its right operand, and an i-string is that same `+`.** A binary expression takes its type from its LEFT operand, and the native types never dispatch — machine JS *is* their operator semantics — so with `str` on the left the whole expression was `str` no matter what stood on the right, and the emission was the host's own `+` over whatever the right operand lowered to. A struct lowers to a tuple, an enum to a tagged array, a `List` to an array, and the host renders any of those as its comma-joined elements: `Point { x = 1, y = 2 }` printed as `1,2`, `Some(5)` as `0,5`, a `(1, 2)` as `1,2`. B24 closed exactly this hole for the COMPARISONS, and the check it added sits in the same loop — but it was scoped to `==`/`!=`/`<`/`<=`/`>`/`>=`, the operators §5.7 was being written against when it was found, so `+` was left with nothing typing its right side at all. **The reason the garbage turned up far from any `+` a reader wrote is that two desugarings build one**, which is what made this general rather than a curiosity. An interpolated string is not a second mechanism: `lexing::emit_interpolated` lowers `i"here {p} there"` to `("" + "here " + p + " there")` before the parser exists, so `i"here {p} there"` printed `here 1,2 there` through the identical path — the spec's own §2.3 already said each hole "must be valid as a `+` operand with `str`", and nothing was checking it. A `css` block's mixed value is built to that same shape (`css::build_value`), so `padding: calc({space(4)} + 2px);` put a `Length`'s two-field tuple *inside a declaration*, sheet fragment and all — `calc(var(--space-4),:root{--space-4:1rem} + 2px)`. **The rule is now stated rather than inferred**, because the natives have no impl to read it off: `+` on a native left operand admits `str + x` where `x` is a `str`, a numeric primitive, or a `bool`, and `T + T` for a numeric primitive `T`, and nothing else. The concatenation arm is deliberately NOT the trait's `B = Self`, and it is the one place the asymmetry is load-bearing: an i-string hole is this expression, and std's own `impl i32 with Display` is `i"{self}"`, which is `"" + self` — a strict `B = Self` would have refused the language's own rendering primitive. What the arm demands instead is that the operand's host value already *be* its rendering, which the numbers, `bool` and `str` satisfy and nothing else does. The steer names the one fix that works — `to_string()`, with an `impl … with Display` if the type has none — and deliberately does **not** offer "or interpolate it", because the i-string is this same concatenation and would send the reader back into the same garbage. Three more refusals fall out of the same rule and each gets its own words. The MIRROR shape, `count + "!"`, used to type as `i32` while producing the string `1!`; the numeric steer ("suffix the literal") would misread the author there, so it says what is actually true — only a `str` LEFT operand concatenates, because the expression takes its type from the left. Differently-typed numeric operands (`ratio + count` at `f64` and `i32`) now refuse under `+` exactly as they have always refused under `<`, an unsuffixed literal still adapting to its peer. And `bool` and backed enums, which are native for `==`/`<` without being numbers, have no `Add` at all: `true + true` would have added the lowering and typed the `2` as a `bool`, and two backings sum to something that is rarely a variant. A backed enum is refused on the concatenation arm too, and that is a judgement rather than a consequence — `Size::Small` lowers to `"sm"` and *would* have concatenated as one, which reads as a display form the program chose when nothing chose it. **The family marker is `miscompile` on §7.2's own definition** — "wrong code emitted, or a program admitted that it must refuse" — and this is both halves at once: the accepted program rendered a runtime tuple into user-facing text. The numeric arm alone would be a `breaking` entry (`f64 + i32` computes a correct answer today), but it is not a separable change: it is the same `B = Self` sentence, it cost the estate nothing, and the entry's claim is the miscompile. **Scope, stated because the neighbours look identical**: this is `+`'s rule, not the arithmetic family's. `f64 * i32` and `str - str` still skip the check, and closing theirs refuses code that computes correct answers today — a breaking numeric-strictness change with a migration of its own (`as_f64()`), argued separately rather than smuggled in behind a miscompile fix. One residual inside `+` is left open and pinned `#[ignore]`d rather than papered over: an UNBOUNDED GENERIC right operand still escapes, because the rule rejects only a grounded operand — B24's own leniency — so `fun show<T>(value: T): str { "v=" + value }` still prints `v=1,2` at `T := Point`. The declaration is checked once for all instantiations, so the honest fix is a refusal there with a `T: Display` steer rather than a per-monomorphization check, and choosing between those is a design call this operand rule does not settle. A census of the estate at the fixed rule — std, the corpus, every doc fence, the examples, the benchmarks, `macro_std`, every test binary in the workspace, the website, the playground and the dogfood app — turned up THREE reliances, and every one of them was itself an instance of the bug rather than a use the rule took away. **The migration note is those three.** An inference pin asserted that `1 + (2 + "x")` prints `12x` — an `i32 + str` typed as `i32`; its real subject was the JS printer's parenthesization of a right-nested operand, which float addition pins without mixing types at all (`0.1 + (0.2 + 0.3)` is `0.6`; flat, it is `0.6000000000000001`). The const-sweep's diagnostic fixture summed `scale(derived) + started` at `i32 + Instant` purely to keep four bindings read; `started` now gets a read of its own through `to_iso()`. And the css block's order-sensitivity fixture wrote `border: 1px solid {Color::gray(500)}` — a MIXED value, so a concatenation — which was emitting `1px solid var(--gray-500),:root{--gray-500:#6b7280}` into a `border` declaration: invalid CSS a browser drops, invisible to that test because it compares two builds of itself. The shorthand's value is hole-free now; what it proves is the ORDER of `border` against `border-color`, which no value enters. That third one names a real gap the refusal exposes rather than creates: a typed style value used MID-value has no correct spelling today, because `.text` reaches the var reference and drops the `:root` line the one-hole path carries. Refusing is the honest state, and the shape is pinned as such. Nineteen pins plus the `#[ignore]`d residual, each proven non-vacuous in the direction it claims: the fourteen refusals go red with the rule removed — `+=` among them, since the compound-assignment desugar synthesizes its binary at a second registration site and had to be reached there too — the four "still compiles and runs" pins go red under an over-broad rule, and the user-`Add`-impl negative — an `impl Metres with Add<i32>`, whose `B` is not `Self` and must stay that way — goes red when the native check is widened past the natives. No corpus golden moves. (backlog B148, found by Order 18's css-block-s2 lane; completes B24; docs `spec/types.md` §5.7, `tour/values-and-types.md`, `std/strings.md`, `appendix/errors.md` and `appendix/gotchas.md`)

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**Assigning to a struct field checked nothing at all — `b.value = "text"` into an `i32` field compiled, and `b.value + 1` printed `text1`.** The struct LITERAL door has always checked its field values; the ASSIGNMENT door checked none of them, so the same value the compiler refused at construction it accepted one line later at a store. The loud half is the one above — a field typed `i32` holding a `str`, arithmetic done on it, and no diagnostic anywhere. The half that actually cost someone a debugging session is silent: a bare value assigned into an `Option<T>` field lands UNWRAPPED, so the `is Some(let v)` that reads it back never matches and the program takes the other branch without a word. That is how it was found — a kolt builder storing its handler with `self.on_drag_move = handler` where the field is `Option<|DragEvent| void>`, running with the drag callback permanently invisible. Every mutation-through-a-field in the estate was unverified: nested (`a.b.c = v`), indexed (`list[i].f = v`) and compound (`s.f += v`) alike, since none of them is a local and it was only locals that the assignment pass ever wired to a type. The fix is not a second check beside the first — a second check is how the two doors came to disagree in the first place. Both doors now call **one** function, `check_field_value`, which infers the value AGAINST the declared field type and reconciles: the literal path was rewritten to go through it rather than to duplicate it, so a future divergence has nowhere to live. The assignment side is a constraint (`FieldAssignment`) rather than a post-pass, because the target is a *place* whose field type is not known until the field accessor resolves — it defers until the target lands, is vacuous when the target turns out to be a local or a tuple position (those places are checked by the kinds that own them), and reads the field type off the TARGET, which the accessor already recorded with the receiver's type arguments substituted in, so a field reached through `Wrap<Doubler>` is checked against `Doubler` and not against the struct's abstract parameter. Compound assignment needs no second site, and that is the point of registering the constraint on the value that LANDS: `s.f += v` desugars to `s.f = s.f + v`, and it is the sum, not the written `v`, that the field ends up holding — a `str` field `+= 5` is well typed and still runs, where checking the written operand would refuse it. No compound shape can land the wrong type today, because every overloadable operator's trait returns `Self`; the door is covered regardless, so it stays covered when one can. **The estate census came back at zero.** Every entry of every project — vilan's corpus and examples, kolt, the website, the playground, 149 checks, std riding along on each — was checked with the fix on and against a base binary, and not one site newly refuses; the corpus goldens do not move. (The estate had to be shadow-migrated past this cycle's `std::print` removal first, or the census would have been reading eight first-error walls instead of the code behind them.) The kolt site that prompted the report is already written `Some(handler)` in the working tree — the owner reached the same conclusion by hand, which is the refusal this now gives him at compile time. Nine pins, each proven red-first by re-planting the removed check: str-into-numeric, scalar-into-scalar, a bare value into an `Option` field, the owner's closure-into-`Option<closure>` shape, the nested and indexed places, the compound form (red under the wrong-operand registration, which is the mistake it exists to catch), the well-typed store whose `is Some` does match, and the literal-door control, which must and does stay exactly where it was. (backlog B166, the owner's kolt find generalized by probe; docs `spec/types.md` §5.11)

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**`if stored is Some(let f) { f() }` compiled to a call on an `f` nothing ever declared: a `ReferenceError` at run time, from a program the compiler accepted.** An `is` capture has no declaration of its own — that is the lowering's design. It is ALIASED to the subject's payload slot (`$a[1]`) and substituted at each use, which is why a capture costs nothing and why `match`, whose legs really do declare their captures as `const`s, takes a different path. The alias table had one reader: the arm that turns a local into a JS value. There are TWO places a local becomes JS, and the second — the call arm's named-callee fast path, written for functions and reached by every `Expr::Local` subject — never consulted it and emitted the binding's SOURCE NAME as the callee. So the defect selected precisely the payloads whose uses are calls, which is to say closure-typed ones: an `i32` payload was read as a value and had always worked, a struct payload read through its fields had always worked, and the same closure through `match` had always worked. Three green neighbours around one shape that threw. It was found in kolt's `interact.vl`, where a stored drag handler is exactly an `Option<|| DragHandler>` pulled out with `is Some(let …)` and called. The fix is a guard, not a new path: the call arm no longer claims an `is` capture as a named callee, so it falls to the value-call arm it should always have taken — which walks the subject and finds the alias by the same lookup a READ does. One alias table, and now every place a local becomes JS consults it. `$a[1]()` is the emission, which is what reading the capture and then calling it has always meant. Nine pins, every one of them RUNNING the bundle — the program compiled before, so only execution can tell the difference — and every one proven red-first by removing the guard: the direct call in the arm, the call captured by a closure created in the arm, two closures deep, a capture called with arguments whose result is used, an `else if` chain where each arm must alias its own subject, the shadowed spelling that the first (and wrong) diagnosis blamed, and the three controls — `i32`, `match`, struct payload — which stay green under the planting and are pinned so they keep doing that. No corpus golden moves: a program that called an `is` capture could not have had a working golden. **The first diagnosis was wrong and the probes are why.** It was filed as an alpha-rename that lost a shadowed binding inside closure bodies, on the strength of a `drag_handler_2` in the emitted output; the owner could not reproduce the shadowing trigger, and four discriminating probes — the same shape with and without a shadow, with an `i32` payload, and through `match` — put the boundary exactly on the payload's type and nowhere near the name. (backlog B167, the owner's kolt find; diagnosis corrected mid-lane by probe)

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**A call through a bound no longer inherits the context requirements of a same-named member on an unrelated trait.** The context-coverage check refines its candidate set per call, but the set it refines is keyed by member NAME — every trait in the program declaring that name, and every impl of those traits. For a bound call that is far too wide: `v.bind(..)` under `V: MyTrait<str>` can only reach an impl of `MyTrait`, and never some other trait's `bind`. Two traits in one program spelling a member the same way was enough for each to inherit the other's context reads, and the refusal that came out named a `run` the author's own code had nothing to do with. The refinement now narrows a bound site to the impls providing the bound's own traits, with their supertraits, and widens back exactly as before wherever the constraint names no trait or the filter selects nothing. Found building std's `MaybeSignal`, whose blanket subject matches every type: without this, its `bind` was a candidate for every receiver in every program that loaded `std::reactive`. (backlog A32)

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**Two blanket impls separated only by their instantiation picked the wrong body, silently.** `impl type S: Source<type T> with Maybe<T>` beside `impl type T with Maybe<T>`: a call bounded `Maybe<Cell>` wants the static one, because the bounded one provides `Maybe<i32>` on a `Cell` that is a `Source<i32>`. The selection order checks exactly that at its instantiation tier — but the bound's own binder was never grounded. `S` bound from the receiver and `T` stayed a hole, so the arguments the impl provides read as "cannot tell", the leniency clause kept it, and the two blankets reached the specificity tier together, where a bounded binder outranks a bare one. The reactive body then ran against a value that was never what its `self.read()` expected, and the program printed `undefined` rather than failing. The bound's binders now ground from the receiver's OWN implementation of that bound's trait — `Cell: Source<i32>` binds `T = i32` — so the instantiation tier separates the pair as it was written to. Only reachable since a `type` binder could be written inside a bound at all (B165), and std's `MaybeSignal` is the first thing in the tree shaped like it. (backlog A32, B157)

<!-- family: feature -->
**A bound whose argument is a BARE generic parameter now keeps the caller's parameter — and its bounds — inside a generic body, so `View::swap`, `View::swap_split` and `ui::chunk_preload` take a `Source` like every other read-only binding.** A33 swept `std::ui`, classified each concrete-`Signal` parameter read-only or write-back, and widened the read-only ones to a `Source<T>` bound — eight of them. `swap` is read-only by that same classification and it was held anyway, for a defect the widening walked into rather than for anything about `swap`: `fun consume<T: PartialEq, S: Source<T>>(source: S)` called with a `Signal<T>` from inside a generic body refused, naming a parameter the author never wrote. The pair that localizes it is exact. `S: Source<List<T>>` — the bound's argument CONSTRUCTED over the caller's own `T`, which is `bind_each`'s bound — always resolved, and shipped. `S: Source<T>` — the bound's argument the BARE parameter — did not. **The cause is that a unifier is not directed.** A generic mentioned only in a sibling's parameterized bound is bound by recovering the bound's arguments from the implementation the sibling's type provides (`analyzer.rs::trait_args_for`), which reconciles the receiver against that impl's SUBJECT and reads the bindings off. Reconciliation is unification: with one generic side and one concrete side the binding can only run one way, which is precisely why the constructed argument always worked; with generics on BOTH sides it kept the left one's pair, `caller T -> impl Z`. That maps the caller's parameter onto the impl's and says nothing about `Z`, so `impl Signal<type Z> with Source<Z>`'s written argument substituted to nothing and the impl's own unbound parameter came back as the bound's argument. The callee's `T` was then inferred to a parameter carrying no bounds at all, and its declared `T: PartialEq` was checked against that and refused. The bindings are now ORIENTED toward the impl's own binders — kept as written when the key is one, inverted when the value is one, dropped when neither is, since an impl's trait arguments are written in its binders and in concrete types and a binding for anything else cannot ground them. The reconciliation call itself is untouched, so which impl a receiver matches is unchanged; only a binder that was previously left ungrounded now grounds. (`satisfies_trait_bound` solves the same problem by reconciling subject-first; this site could not simply follow it, because swapping the operands there would also change which impls match a bare-trait receiver.) **The refusal that remains is better than the one that went.** With the caller's parameter genuinely unbounded the call is still refused — the check did not go vacuous, it now reaches the right parameter — and the message names the parameter the author has to go and edit rather than the impl's, which through std used to surface as a bound missing from a `U` appearing nowhere in the program. **The three that waited widened together**, keeping their generic-parameter ORDER, because a split build's emitter rebinds the gate call's type arguments from `swap` onto `swap_split` and `chunk_preload` by position (`transformer.rs::rebind_by_position`); the process twin's `swap` moved in lockstep, as A33's eight did. Emission moved once and in the good direction: `swap`'s widened binder puts its body under the enclosing monomorphization's memo key, so a duplicate `Signal::get` instance merges and the split fixture's eager bundle emits 104 functions where it emitted 105 — the same program, one declaration smaller, every other artifact byte-identical and the chunk files and manifest untouched. Twelve pins. The `#[ignore]`d exhibit A33 left behind is un-ignored; four more edge cases join it, each proven red first by re-planting the defect — the caller's member reaching the callee through the bound, two bare-parameter bounds over an impl that writes its binders in the other order, the bound surviving two generic bodies of re-wrapping, and the non-vacuity pin that must still refuse; the constructed-argument control stays green either way. Four compile pins drive the widened signatures with a user `Source` that is deliberately not a `Signal`, one running pin shows the widened `swap` still SUBSCRIBES and still disposes the subtree it replaces (a `swap` that read once would type, build and pass every compile pin), and a concrete-`Signal` control holds the existing call sites. One new `#[ignore]`d pin records what this fix exposed and did not close: `satisfies_trait_bound` answers for a `Type::Generic` value from its DECLARED bounds alone, so a blanket `impl type T with Wrap<T>` cannot satisfy `W: Wrap<T>` when `W` is bound to the caller's own parameter — a concrete caller passes, and B168's own defect is gone from that program. (backlog B168, found by Order 22's a33 lane; docs `spec/types.md` §5.6, `std/browser.md`, `std/reactive.md`, `guide/ui.md`)

<!-- family: feature -->
**A trait may now declare associated functions — a `fun` with no `self`, with a default body — and `Trait::func(..)` calls that body.** A trait's members were all methods: something with a receiver, reached through one. That left the CONSTRUCTOR out. A type whose canonical construction belongs to a capability rather than to one struct — `Signal::new(value)`, where `Signal` is the capability and `SignalCell` the canonical cell — had nowhere to put it, and `Trait::static()` did not resolve at all, with or without a body on the declaration (the state B83 probed and recorded rather than built). It resolves now, and to a ruled answer: `Trait::func(..)` is THE TRAIT'S OWN default body, always — never an impl's override, which is reached through the implementing type's own path, `Type::func(..)`. That is not an arbitrary tie-break. A `self` method's `Trait::method(receiver)` form exists to name the trait a RECEIVER dispatches through; an associated function has no receiver, so there is nothing to select between, and the two spellings name two bodies rather than one dispatch. The trait's own generic parameters bind from the call like any generic function's (`Signal::new(7)` binds `T = i32`), and a supertrait's associated function is reachable through the sub-trait, as its methods are. Two refusals ship with it, from day one rather than as a later tightening: `Trait::func(..)` for a declaration with NO default body names a per-impl requirement with nothing behind it — the shape that used to reach the transformer's "please report this program" guard — and is refused naming both spellings; and `Type::func(..)` where the impl declares no such member is refused with the trait's spelling named, because a body with no receiver is not inherited onto a type's path the way a method's is. `spec/types.md` §5.5 states all of it.

<!-- family: feature -->
**A trait in a `let` binding's annotation is now a checked CONSTRAINT on the binding's inferred type — not an error, and not the binding's type.** `let count: Signal<i32> = SignalCell::new(1)` is the shape: `count`'s type is `SignalCell<i32>`, what its initializer infers, exactly as if nothing had been written — and the annotation asserts that whatever the initializer produces implements `Signal<i32>`, failing at the annotation when it does not. This is the bounded-generic rule in binding position, checked wide and kept narrow: reading `count`'s members reaches `SignalCell`'s own, fields included; a reassignment must still be a `SignalCell<i32>`; nothing is boxed, widened or erased. The refusal it replaces at this one position is NARROWED, not repealed — a parameter, a return type, a struct field and every nested spelling (`&Display`, `List<Display>`) still refuse, which is what keeps a heterogeneous container impossible. The holes that refusal was built to shut stay shut, by the semantics rather than by the ban; the destructor case is the clearest, since `let handle: Named = Handle { .. }` once compiled, ran, and silently never dropped the handle, and today it compiles AND drops it, because the binding is a `Handle`. The `if` case needed no rule of its own: the arms unify first, and the constraint meets the ONE type that unification produced, so two arms of different concrete types are refused at the arms as an ordinary mismatch even when both implement the trait — there is no widening for them to meet in. The reading is universal, for every trait name in the position, std's included.

<!-- family: feature -->
**`impl type T with Trait<T>` is a real blanket impl now: it dispatches through a bound, and any constructor-headed impl outranks it wherever both match.** The form parsed, analyzed and compiled cleanly for as long as nothing called through it; the first dispatch died with an internal error asking the author to report their own program — "a call resolved to `MaybeSignal`'s requirement `bind`, which has no body". The cause was a divergence rather than a missing feature. The analyzer already resolves a blanket beside a specific impl by SPECIFICITY (B73's R3): `"x".show()` reaching `impl type T with Show` has worked for releases. But a call through a BOUND — `fun badge<V: MaybeSignal<str>>(label: V)` — cannot be resolved there at all, because the receiver is a type parameter until monomorphization binds it, so the transformer re-asks the question at emission — and it re-asked with a nominal head comparison that could not see a generic subject at all and ranked nothing. A blanket was therefore invisible to every bounded call, and, less loudly, a pair the analyzer DID rank was answered here in declaration order: `impl Box<type T: Marker>` beside `impl Box<type T>` ran the marked body at a direct call and the plain body at a bounded one, in one program, silently. Both are gone, because the two sides now ask one shared question (`crates/vilan-core/src/impl_select.rs`, following `dispatch_refine`'s precedent of a shared module owning a resolution its consumers merely ask), in three tiers the spec now states: **applicability** — the impl's subject PATTERN matches the concrete type, its binders read as holes and the type as rigid, and every binder's bound holds there; **instantiation** — where the caller knows which instantiation it wants, and a bound-directed call always does, an impl is kept only if the arguments it provides FOR THIS RECEIVER are those arguments; **specificity** — the impl whose subject the others match and which matches none of theirs wins, then, for subjects of equal shape, the one whose binders carry the stronger bounds. A blanket is simply the least specific tier, which is why nothing needed inventing: it is the order B73 already ruled, applied where it had never been asked. The pay-off is `MaybeSignal<T>` whole — one trait, one blanket static impl, one generic `Signal` impl, and a component parameter that takes `badge("static")` and `badge(signal)` with no call-site ceremony, static dispatch, and the signal leg reacting to a later `set`. It also keeps a case a negative bound would have deleted: under a `MaybeSignal<Signal<str>>` bound a bare `Signal<str>` is a legitimate STATIC value through the blanket, while the same value under a `MaybeSignal<str>` bound is the reactive source through the `Signal` impl — the instantiation is decided before specificity is consulted, so both readings of one type live in one program. That is the recorded argument for ruling `impl type T: (not Signal)` OUT of the grammar and not merely deferring it: the two impls provably never overlap at one bound (`T = Signal<T>` has no finite solution), so nothing needed excluding, and a negative bound would have inverted the rule that adding an implementation is additive — implementing a trait for your type would newly EXCLUDE it from every `not`-bound in the ecosystem, making an impl addition a breaking change. **And the accept-then-ICE is closed as a class, not as an instance.** Every call through a bound now reaches one implementation's member, or the trait's default where the winning impl declares none, or the program is refused before it runs. Two impls with the same subject were already refused at the second declaration — so a second blanket for one trait never reaches a call site — and two the order genuinely cannot rank (`impl type T: Alpha` beside `impl type U: Beta`, met by a type that is both) were already reported at a direct call but reached the internal error through a bound; they are now reported at the call that made the binding, naming both subjects and the fix, in the same words the direct spelling uses. Ten pins, each proven red first by re-planting the exact defect it removes — blindness to a generic subject (seven go red, with the original internal error), the ranking itself (four), and the bound-side refusal (one) — plus a corpus program holding the whole recipe. Const evaluation reaches trait dispatch, so it reaches the blanket, and is pinned to select the same body a runtime call does at both tiers. No corpus golden moves. (backlog B158, found probing B157; docs `spec/types.md` §5.4)
