<!-- family: feature -->
**A package now declares its own ambient scope: `print`, `Option`/`Some`/`None` and `Result`/`Ok`/`Err` are in scope with no import, `prelude = "std::web"` swaps in an application set, and the whole thing is the weakest binding in the language.** The argument is one probe. `list.get(1)` hands you an `Option` — a lang item, manufactured by a construct you wrote without naming a type — and the compiler then refused to let you take it apart, because you had not imported names you never wrote. `?.` has the same shape. A language that can *manufacture* a value at a use site should be able to *name* it there, and that is what closes here. This is not a new mechanism: the spec's §4.7 already made the primitives and `List` ambient, `macros.rs` already made every std macro ambient with the shadowing rule written into its own doc comment, and `world_prelude_nodes` already made ten `meta` types ambient in every macro body — ordinary std values and types were the one namespace left out of a pattern the language applies three times. **std ships TWO preludes**, which is the census's own finding turned into the design rather than an observation about it: `print` is not the most-imported name in real vilan code, `Signal` is (21 of 42 application files against `print`'s 15), and nine of the top sixteen application names are the style/UI cluster — so no single fixed set serves both a test corpus and an app. The base set is seven names, each admitted by a rule and not by rank: universal (base layer, every target), language-level, unambiguous, and top-of-census in more than one corpus. The web set adds `Signal`, `view` and `View` as members and `style` and `ui` as **modules**. **Ambient module names are the design's real addition.** A member entry costs one ambient name and buys one; a module entry costs one and buys a namespace — sixteen style enums behind `style::`, for the price of one qualifying segment. It also cannot collide, which is how the census's one self-collision dissolves rather than being adjudicated: `std::display::Display` (the `to_string` trait) and `std::style::Display` (the CSS enum) both exist in the base layer and could not both be bare, so bare `Display` is the trait's alone and the CSS one is `style::Display`. **The enum never renames and nothing in the estate moves.** No new precedence rule was needed for any of it — `std::style::style` is a function whose name equals its module's and the estate calls it bare 60 times, and every one of those files carries `import std::style::style;`, which is an explicit import and therefore outranks the ambient module. The other half of that is pinned too: a file which imports the function no longer reaches the module, so `style::Display` stops resolving *there* — one name, one binding, the ordinary rule rather than anything the prelude adds. **The key is `prelude`, on `[package]` and `[library]` alike**, because std — which must declare `prelude = false` — is a `[library]`. Its value grammar is one rule: a module path, or `false`. `"std::prelude"` and `"std::web"` are not set names the compiler knows, they are real modules in std whose exports ARE the ambient names, written in vilan in the same mechanism a user's custom prelude uses — so there is no built-in list in Rust to drift out of sync with the docs, and a reader can open the file. Omitting the key means the base set, which makes it additive to every existing manifest with no edit. A custom prelude REPLACES rather than extends, and extension is spelled by re-export — one mechanism, no merge rules, and std does not get a mechanism its users lack (`web.vl` re-states the base seven rather than deriving them). `prelude = "std"` is refused by name, because after the alias sweep std's root exports nothing and accepting it would mean a silently EMPTY prelude; `prelude = true` is refused because an untagged string-or-bool accepts it syntactically and the affirmative is spelled by omitting the key. **Per package, never inherited** — not from a consumer to a dependency, not from a dependency to a consumer, not from a `[project]` root (which has no such key), not per platform layer. Two packages that disagree about what `Signal` means both keep compiling in one build, which is the property that makes this safe to ship before a registry rather than after. **The implementation is a resolution scope, and that was the one thing this feature could get catastrophically wrong.** In this compiler an explicit import BEATS a same-file declaration, silently — `world_prelude_nodes` says so in its own doc comment ("imports overwrite hoisted function bindings, so the exclusion is how the prelude yields") and filters every name a macro world declares out of its synthesized text for exactly that reason. So a value prelude spliced in as file-head imports would have *replaced* `vilan/test/match-patterns.vl`'s own `enum Signal` and `vilan/test/default.vl`'s own `trait Default` with std's — turning two non-events into miscompiles. Instead the prelude's bindings are copied into a scope with `or_insert`, in the one window where both halves hold: after the import fixpoint (so a prelude module's re-exports point at real definitions, and an explicit import is already there to yield to) and before any name resolves (so nothing has memoized a lookup the prelude would change). Items overwrite it during the walk, imports overwrite it just before — the weakest scope by ORDERING rather than by a precedence table. `record_reference` is deliberately not called for a prelude binding, or Organize Imports would start pruning real imports because "the prelude already references them". The pin that holds this is not the obvious one: the entry file is seeded before its walk, so ordering alone protects it and every entry-file shadowing pin stayed green with the fix removed — it took a *module* file whose own `print` has a disagreeing signature to turn the miscompile into something an analyze-only harness can see. **Tooling was half the feature and mostly fell out.** Completion and go-to-definition needed no code at all: prelude names are ordinary entries in `name_to_id_map`, so `scope_completions` offers them undecorated, `auto_import_completions` stops offering them by its existing "already in scope" filter, and go-to-definition lands in the std source exactly as an import does. Organize Imports gained one line and now **strips an import the prelude covers**, matched on the DEFINITION rather than the name — so `import std::io::print;` goes and `import my_lib::print;` beside an ambient `std::print` stays. `vilan fmt` is untouched, by its own standing rule that pruning is the editor's job. The unresolved-name diagnostic gained the arm the two-set design makes necessary: a base-prelude package reaching for `Signal` is told `` `Signal` is in the prelude of the web set — set `prelude = "std::web"` `` rather than being steered toward an import, and that index is read out of `std/src/web.vl` itself on a cold path so the steer can never drift from the module. The base seven are excluded from that arm, since both sets carry them. One defect was found and fixed by a pin rather than by review: the base-cache key did not include the prelude, so a stored `std::web` world was served to a base-prelude program — the key and the workspace fingerprint now carry the entry package's and every dependency's. Twenty-nine pins across the manifest, resolution and editor surfaces, the shadowing ones proven non-vacuous by planting the bug they remove. (backlog B156; proposal/prelude.md; docs `spec/names.md` §4.4/§4.7, `spec/appendix.md` §A.4, `tour/hello-vilan.md`, `tour/projects.md`, `appendix/errors.md`, `appendix/editor.md`, `appendix/glossary.md`)

<!-- family: feature -->
**A reactive binding now asks for what it actually uses: `bind_text`, `bind_class`, `bind_attr`, `bind_styled`, `style_var`, `bind_each`, `when` and `show` take any `Source<T>`, not the concrete `Signal<T>`.** Every binding in `std::ui` demanded a `Signal` — including the seven that never write one. The trait that exists precisely to say "readable and subscribable" was declared, implemented by `Signal`, used by `ReactiveServer::expose`, and then ignored by the entire view layer, so a user type that implements `Source<T>` could not drive a binding whose whole body is `source.effect(..)`. The exhibit is kolt's `StorageSignal` — a signal with a place to hang persistence, `set` kept off the trait — which could subscribe, derive, and cross the wire, and could not fill an attribute. The widening is a bound on an ordinary generic parameter (`fun bind_attr<S: Source<str>>(self, name: str, source: S): View`), the spelling `expose<T: Wire, S: Source<T>>` has shipped with since the RPC arc, and it moves in **lockstep across both `ui` twins**: the same component source is compiled through the browser layer and the SSR twin, so a widening on one side alone would have made every custom-source component client-only. What stays concrete is what WRITES. `bind_value` and `bind_draft` write back through `set` and `push`, which `Source` does not declare — pinned as refusals rather than left to be noticed, so a later blanket widening cannot take the write side by accident — and `optimistic`, `Optimistic::over` and `Signal::update` are the same class in `std::reactive`. Three more are read-only and still held, each for a reason worth stating. **`swap`** (and `swap_split`, and `chunk_preload`) reads exactly as `when` does and would have widened with it, but a `Source<T>` bound whose argument is a BARE generic parameter does not resolve inside a generic body: the callee's `T` binds to the impl's own unbound parameter instead of the caller's, so the callee's `T: PartialEq` is checked against something carrying no bound and refused — and `swap_split` calls `swap` from exactly such a body, so widening it makes std uncompilable. The gap is narrow and now pinned from both sides: `S: Source<List<T>>`, the bound's argument CONSTRUCTED over the caller's `T`, resolves fine — that is `bind_each`, and it shipped — while the bare-parameter twin is `#[ignore]`d until the solver closes it, with the note that the three signatures then widen together *in their generic-parameter order*, because the split gate rebinds their type arguments by position. **`attr` and `child`** dispatch through the `AttrValue` and `Slot` traits, whose reactive arms are `impl Signal<str> with …`; widening a trait ARM is a blanket impl and not a bound, so `<div href(source)>` still wants a signal and the element-syntax desugar — which builds `.attr(name, value)`, never `.bind_attr` — is byte-for-byte where it was. **`combine`** only reads too, and is held by the grammar rather than the semantics: its mapped-tuple parameter would need each element to be *some* `Source<U>`, a per-element existential the form has no place to bind (`(U in T: Source<U>)` is refused because vilan has no trait objects, and a single `<S: Source<U>>` names one type where the tuple needs a different one per element). **The negative this was checked against is emission, and it is worth reporting precisely, because "byte-identical" turned out to be the wrong claim by a hair in the right direction.** A generic function is monomorphized, so the widened binders moved out of the plain-named emissions into the generated-name pool — which renames them and shifts every generated name after them. Nothing else moved: a checker that reduces two bundles to a canonical form (duplicate declarations unified, generated names renumbered by a canonical traversal, statements compared in order and declarations as a set) reports every affected artifact as the SAME PROGRAM up to naming — one corpus golden, four split goldens, and six example bundles — and the process corpus program's stdout is byte-identical before and after. Exactly one corpus program's golden moves, out of a hundred, and every `.css` asset is untouched. The bundles are also *smaller*: the widening puts the nested `effect`/`observe` instances under the enclosing monomorphization's memo key, so two instances that used to be emitted twice now hit it once — 285 bytes off the reactive-ui example, 274 off the walkthrough client. Pinned red-first from three directions: a browser app built by the real CLI and run against a DOM stub, where a user `Source` drives all eight widened bindings and then a write through it reaches the DOM (reverting the signature reddens it; keeping the signature and making the binding read ONCE reddens it too, which is the half a compile-only pin cannot see); compile pins for both twins, including the SSR twin's rendered markup; and the refusals for the write-back pair and the two trait arms. (backlog A33; docs `std/reactive.md`, `std/browser.md`, `guide/ui.md`, `guide/ssr.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`window` is a listen target now, `listen` hands back the `Subscription` that unhooks a DOM listener, and an `Event` will tell you where the pointer is.** `std::dom` bound element listeners and nothing else, so anything needing a window-level event hand-rolled its own `external fun` — `std::router` had one for `popstate`, the website had a third, and a `message`, `resize` or `storage` event was a thing std simply could not deliver. The live exhibit is a sidebar-resize drag in the dogfood app, which had to hand-roll all three of the gaps at once, and a drag is the canonical case for why element-local `on_event` cannot carry it: the pointer leaves the element the moment the drag starts moving. **`window()` is a handle, not a suffix.** The obvious sketch was an `on_window` free function, but taken as written it grows a parallel vocabulary — `on_window`, `on_window_event`, then a `listen_window` beside `listen` — where the only difference from the element forms is *which object* is being listened to. That is a target, and vilan already spells targets as handles, so `external struct Window` carries exactly the three verbs `Element` does and the surface never has to answer why one of them is spelled differently. It also gives the next asks somewhere to land: window `resize` plus `innerWidth`/`devicePixelRatio` is what the canvas proposal's DPR path wants, and those are accessors on a handle rather than three more free functions. Nothing beyond the listen verbs ships, deliberately — an accessor no delivered event has a caller for is dead surface, which is the trap this gap was filed with. Acquiring the handle costs one runtime helper, because a global property has no function form; that is the same reason `location.pathname` has needed one since routing shipped. **Removal is a `Subscription`, and it is a new verb.** `on_event`'s signature does not change: it is the chaining form, it is what `on:click` lowers to, and it is fire-and-forget by design, because an element listener dies with its element and that is usually the whole answer. Nothing about the window ever dies, so removal has to be expressible — and the vocabulary already existed, since `Source::sub` has always handed back a handle whose `dispose` unhooks it. So `listen` is that shape on both targets, `[must_use]` exactly as `sub` is, over a new `Subscription::teardown(release)` constructor: a subscription over no signal, whose `dispose` runs the hook once and nothing else, riding the same one-shot release cell a remote source's lease decrement already uses — which is what makes disposing twice safe, and a drag disposes twice by construction. Two tiers, one rule: `on`/`on_event` are fire-and-forget, `listen` is the removable form. Making `on_event` return the handle instead would have turned every existing zero-ceremony call site into a `must_use` warning, and `View.on_event`'s chaining return has no room for it at all. Ownership stays the caller's, as `sub`'s is and unlike `effect`'s, and that asymmetry is the point: a drag arms two window listeners per `pointerdown`, so auto-registering with the ambient owner would push two dead cleanups onto the enclosing boundary on every drag, forever — the leak the owner model exists to prevent, entered from the other end. `get_owner().take(window().listen(…))` is the one-line idiom, and an owner-tied twin is recorded rather than built. The whole design rests on one host fact worth stating: `removeEventListener` is **identity-matched**, so `listen`'s teardown has to hand back the same closure the host was given, and that was probed against the real emitter before anything was built — a closure bound once, passed to a std function, used as an argument and captured by the returned teardown, emits as a single JS `const` referenced twice. **`retains` marks registration only.** `addEventListener` retains, because the host stores a vilan closure and calls it later; `removeEventListener` keeps nothing past the call and is left unmarked. The exhibit's hand-roll marks both, which is the over-marking a golden caught on `appendChild` during the retention audit, and this surface deliberately does not inherit it. **The coordinates are named for the pointer, not for the DOM's coordinate space.** `clientX`'s "client" means *viewport* for reasons that are pure history, and the mechanical transliteration would teach that history to every reader forever, so the accessors are `pointer_x`/`pointer_y`. The family objection is real and answered: if `pageX` lands later its name is `page_x` — where the pointer is *in the document* — which is self-explanatory on its own terms rather than as the second row of a coordinate-space table, and two good names beat four uniform ones. Page, offset and screen variants wait for a caller, as does `setPointerCapture`, which is worth designing alongside and is not a substitute: it retargets a drag's events to the capturing element, but `resize`, `popstate`, `storage` and `message` still have no element to hang on, and capture is its own surface with its own disposal question. `std::router` folds onto the new verbs and deletes its own hand-roll, which is one of the three the gap was filed over and the only one that lives in std; the split fixture's golden moves by exactly the two lines that says — the helper, and `window.addEventListener` becoming `__dom_window().addEventListener` — with every chunk byte-identical. Its `popstate` handler keeps its explicit `turn` wrapper, because the window verbs are raw exactly as `element.on` is. The acceptance test is that the exhibit rewrites onto std surface **alone** — its `impl Event` block and both window externs deleted, its `mut dispose` closure becoming two `Subscription::dispose` calls — and that rewrite is pinned end to end under a DOM stub whose `removeEventListener` matches by identity like a browser's, so a teardown that reconstructs its handler removes nothing and every negative assertion fails. Four browser-runtime gates, a fifth over the markings themselves (a declaration gate, because retention is not observable through a listener at all — a closure cannot capture a resource, so no drop order could move), two compile pins for the types, and the formatter and analyzer retention pins extended with the marked/unmarked pair this surface is declared in. Four plants, each watched red and restored: coordinates crossed, listener identity broken, the flag dropped from a registration, and the flag added to a removal — the last being exactly the exhibit's own over-marking. (backlog A27, kolt.local 037; proposal/router.md §5; docs `std/browser.md`, `std/reactive.md`, `guide/ui.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A file can now be read a chunk at a time — `Reader::of(File::open(p))` and `next(size)` — and every open mode has a scoped form whose close is awaited, which is the one that matters when you are writing.** These are the two halves the filesystem arc had left: the owner's vision named *"files can be opened, read incrementally"*, and until now the incremental half did not exist — `read_bytes` materializes the whole file, so a 2 GiB log had to be fully resident, and the only chunked reader in std read HTTP bodies. `Reader` is that loop, and the interesting thing about it is what it is built ON. Node's `FileHandle.read` keeps an implicit position when you pass none, and binding *that* as the primitive would have put mutable state under a loan where the move checker cannot see it: two loans of one handle, each reading, would silently move each other's position. So the handle stayed positional and stateless when it shipped, and the cursor arrives now as an ordinary vilan value layered on top — `next(size)` reads at a position this program owns and advances it, `position()` reads it, `seek()` moves it. A cursor can always be built from a position; a position can never be recovered from a cursor. The payoff is visible in one program: `reader.file.read_at(buffer, p)` reads anywhere in the file **without disturbing the cursor**, so a header probe and a sequential scan share one open file — the property the host's hidden position would have destroyed. A chunk is shorter near the end and **empty at end of file**, which is the loop's terminating condition and the one trap the docs state twice: stop on empty, not on short. That is `std::fetch`'s `BodyReader` loop with the mechanism swapped rather than copied — `BodyReader` answers `{ done, value }` because a `ReadableStream` may legitimately yield an empty chunk mid-stream, so emptiness cannot carry the end there; a file's read cannot, so a second field would be one the caller could always derive. Each `next` allocates and hands back a slice of exactly what was read, so a chunk is never a window on memory the next call overwrites — the reuse is what `read_at` is for. `Reader` declares no teardown of its own and needs none: it is a resource **by containment**, which buys the whole R-rule surface unmodified — it moves, no closure captures it, no `List` holds it, and dropping it closes the file it owns. One shape is deliberately absent and the reason is recorded rather than assumed: the position is a `Shared<i53>` rather than a plain field behind `&mut self` because the language *forces* it — `next` awaits the read, and an async function cannot take a `&mut` parameter, since the view would be held across the suspension. The sketch had guessed right for a reason it did not know it had. **The other half is the scoped form, which shipped read-only and was therefore available in exactly the mode where it matters least.** `with_file(path, |file| …)` awaits its close, so a close failure is a failure of the call — but a close failure is information almost exclusively on a *writing* handle: a write that resolved was handed to the OS, and the OS is entitled to report its failure at `close(2)` rather than at the write (a full disk, a quota, a network filesystem that defers), which is why POSIX warns that ignoring close's return can lose data you believe you wrote. Under the destructor that error goes to stderr and your program carries on. So there is now one scoped form per constructor — `with_file_create`, `with_file_create_new`, `with_file_append`, `with_file_modify` — the suffix naming the constructor it opens with, for the same reason five named constructors replaced node's flags string in the first place: a mode is a name here too, and threading an untyped one through `with_file` would have reintroduced what the constructors exist to prevent. All five delegate to **one** implementation, so the close-before-return ordering that IS the idiom is written once and a form added later cannot quietly drop the await; the emitted shape is `try { body; await close; return } finally { drop }`, so a throwing body still releases the handle through the safety net, and what it wrote before throwing is on disk. Nothing was needed from the compiler for any of it. Fifteen pins, against real files and against the emitted bytes: the chunk loop over a size that divides neither the file nor any prefix of it, a file shorter than one chunk, an empty one, the interleave, R1/R5/R9/R10 and the coloring refusal on the new type, the four writing modes proved by what each does to a file that already exists, the exclusive claim refusing an occupied path without touching it, the throwing body, and the descriptors coming back — plus a corpus program holding the emitted shape. The behavioural ones are plant-proven: a cursor that advances wrong reddens the three reading pins, a shared form with its `await` removed reddens all five scoped forms, a close taught to swallow reddens the seam pin, and the wrong constructor behind `with_file_append` reddens the mode pin. One honesty note the fd instrument earned: the descriptor counts prove release, not ORDERING (measured with the await removed, they still read zero, because the counting call itself awaits), so the ordering stays pinned on the emitted bytes, where the await is. (kolt.local 031 S5; proposal/filesystem.md §3.4's build note and §5.3; docs `std/process.md`, `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A static estate is now three lines of vilan: `asset::read_dir_all` enumerates it, `asset::bundle_as` places each file at a url its path does not spell, and `asset::digest` mints the content-hashed one.** `bundle`'s rule has always been *the path IS the url*, which is what makes it impossible for two files to claim one output name and impossible for anything to be renamed behind your back — and also what forced a path-pinned name (`/favicon.ico`, `/robots.txt`) to sit at the package root, and what made an estate a hand-written list of calls: the dogfood app spells twelve of them. Both costs come off without giving the rule up, because the rename is spelled AT THE CALL. `bundle_as(path, url)` is `bundle` in every other respect — same registry, same copy, same tracked build input, same `chunks.json` row, same `serve_build` route, same `--watch` recopy — so `bundle(path)` shares one implementation with it rather than sitting beside a second one, and is exactly `bundle_as(path, i"/{path}")` for any path free of `.` segments — the one divergence is that `bundle` skips a `./` in the SOURCE spelling when minting the name, where the target grammar refuses one, because the target IS the url. What the target must be is stated and refused with the fix named: it starts at the site root, it is `/`-separated on every host (a backslash is refused, never translated — kolt.local 017's ruling, since the target becomes an output name, a manifest row and a golden), and every segment must be a name, so an empty segment, a `.` or a `..` is a compile error. The reason is that the value returned IS the url the copy answers on: `/a/./b` is a different url to a browser while being the same file on disk, so a target that is not already the url it promises would be a lie the caller cannot see. The target passes the build-owned-name fence `bundle`'s does by reaching the same check — `<leg>.js`, `<leg>.css`, `<leg>.chunks.json` and the route-chunk namespace, for every leg, because `dist/` is one directory — and gains the one refusal the identity rule used to give for free: two files bundling to one url is a compile error naming BOTH sources, because a collision is a statement about a pair and naming half of it sends the reader to the wrong call. That check is per compile, so the same collision across two legs of a workspace — separate compiles into one `dist/`, where the second copy would silently have won — is caught at the copy instead, naming both again. Enumeration is the other half. `read_dir(path)` lists a directory's immediate files by bare name and `read_dir_all(path)` every file beneath it by a path relative to it, both `/`-separated on every host, with two deliberate divergences from `std::fs`'s versions and each forced by what a const result is. Entries are **byte-sorted, always**: a const result is compiled INTO the output, so host iteration order would make one source tree produce two builds, and determinism here is the property the whole const feature rests on rather than a nicety. And only **files** are listed: nothing in this channel consumes a directory — `read`, `bundle`, `bundle_as` and `digest` all take a file — and there is no compile-time `stat`, so a directory entry would be one the program could neither act on nor filter out, and the estate loop would break on the first nested folder. A symlink is classified by what it points at, so a link to a file is bundleable; the walk never follows one into a directory, so it cannot loop. The listed directory is a **tracked build input** on `read`'s doctrine, recorded per directory walked, which is what makes it compose: a file appearing or disappearing anywhere in a listed tree moves exactly one recorded key, so both directions invalidate — you drop a file into `static/` and the next build ships it with no source edit at all. The re-hash the per-leg watch skip performs learned one arm to match (a recorded directory re-hashes as its listing, not as a file's text), so an unchanged tree can still be skipped instead of disqualifying the leg by failing to read. `digest(path)` is the last piece: the file's sha-256 as lowercase hex, 64 characters, over the BYTES — a fingerprint of a font or a `.png` must be a fingerprint of the file and not of a decoding — tracked exactly as `read`'s input is, so an edit re-mints the url. It closes a recorded exhibit: the dogfood app's fingerprints were minted OUT OF BAND because `asset::read` is text-only and no digest was in const reach, and `bundle_as(p, i"/static/{stem}.{digest(p).substring(0, 8)}.{ext}")` is now spellable in the language that ships the file. Fuel prices are argued rather than picked: a listing charges 1,024 per entry (the directory itself counting as one, so an empty listing is not free), a quarter of `bundle`'s 4,096, because an entry costs the walk a listing row and the stat that classifies it where `bundle`'s charge prices a whole-file read on top of both (the count is the files returned plus one for the listed directory; the directories a recursive walk descends are not individually charged, a recorded open question) — which leaves the dominant per-entry cost to what the program does with the entry and keeps the binding fence on the work that copies bytes; and `digest` charges one per eight bytes, an eighth of `read`'s rate, because `read`'s rate prices the arbitrary-length `str` the program then computes over while a digest is 64 characters whatever the file weighs, so the charge bounds how much a build hashes rather than how large a value it holds. Nothing about plain `bundle` moved: the corpus goldens are byte-identical, which is the negative this slice is checked against. Why the const channel and not a manifest key or a build hook is the owner's own argument, recorded in the paper: a toml key walls off programmatic logic, and a hook exchanges no values with the program, has no manifest contract to serve through, needs the whole freshness machinery to approximate tracked inputs, and is first-party-only arbitrary execution where the const channel is fenced interpretation a library can safely ride. Fifteen behaviour pins in the inference suite and eight end-to-end in the CLI (the served target, the sorted listing, the tracked directory in both directions, the fingerprint against sha-256's canonical `abc` vector, every url-shape refusal, both collisions, the owned-name fence), plus a corpus program holding the whole recipe. (kolt.local 035; proposal/const-eval.md §3.1; docs `std/misc.md`, `spec/const.md`, `std/process.md`)

<!-- family: feature -->
**`db.migrate(steps)` carries a schema forward — the thing `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` cannot do.** An idempotent schema *ensure* is right exactly until the first schema *change*: add a `description` column and the clause is a no-op, so the deployed database keeps the old shape while the code querying it has the new one, and the choices left are hand-run SQL against production or deleting the file. `std::db` is batteries-included by doctrine and had no third answer. It does now: `Migration { name, sql }` steps applied **in list order**, the applied ones recorded in a `vilan_migrations` table the migrator owns (`name TEXT PRIMARY KEY`, `applied_at_ms INTEGER`), and the names it applied returned — so a boot log reads `applied 2 migrations` the first time and `applied 0` on every restart after. The design is one invariant and everything else follows from it: **a step is recorded if and only if its SQL committed**, because the record insert rides *inside* the step's own transaction. There is no window where the schema moved and the record did not, so a process killed mid-migration leaves a database whose recorded set exactly describes its schema and the next boot resumes there. The transaction is per STEP and deliberately not per run — all-or-nothing sounds safer and is worse, because a failure in the fifth step would then re-run the first four, and a first step that is not re-runnable (a `DROP`, a backfill that doubles) could never recover. Three conditions stop the boot loudly, all of them *before* a single statement is applied, so a database this refuses is a database it did not touch: a step whose SQL FAILS, named alongside SQLite's own diagnosis (`migration '002-bad' failed and was not applied: no such table: taks`) with nothing recorded for it and its half-applied DDL rolled back; a RECORDED step absent from the list, which is a rolled-back deploy about to run its old queries against a newer schema; and an UNAPPLIED step ordered before an applied one, which is two branches merged into an order no fresh database could ever reproduce. Names must be unique and non-empty, but they need NOT sort in order — the list's order is the authority, and the second refusal already catches every misordering an ascending-name rule would, on a fresh database vacuously and on one with history exactly. Deliberately absent from v1 and stated as such: down-migrations (roll forward with a new step, which is what production does anyway), locking beyond SQLite's own file lock, and diff-GENERATION, which wants the declared-schema question answered first. One documented requirement the surface does not check: a step must never contain `BEGIN`/`COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK` of its own — that commits the migrator's transaction out from under it, landing the schema change permanently while its record is still unwritten, the one way to break the invariant — and it is not checked because substring-scanning SQL is not parsing SQL (a comment, a string literal and a `commit_sha` column would all be refused wrongly, and a line break evades the scan). How an app COLLECTS its steps stays the const channel's business: `const asset::read("migrations/001-create-task.sql")` compiles each file's text into the bundle, so the deployed app is still `dist/` and every migration edit is a tracked build input. Five pins drive real SQLite files in per-test temp directories across two boots each, every one proven red first. (proposal/db-migrations.md, kolt.local 036; docs `guide/persistence.md` and `std/process.md`)

<!-- family: feature -->
**`str::index_of` and `str::last_index_of` — locating text, and three diagnostics that finally name their cause.** `contains` answered *whether* and `substring` cut, and nothing in between computed a bound, so text was taken apart with `split` and put back together — which is exactly what writing a real generator in vilan turned up. The name is the house one rather than a new coinage: `List::index_of(value)` already returns `Option<i32>`, and in this vocabulary `find` takes a PREDICATE, so `str::index_of(needle)` is the spelling that was missing rather than a second one. **Absence is `None`, never `-1`** — the whole reason this is not the host method renamed, and the same doctrine `substring` states: `-1` is an index nothing in the type system tells apart from a real one, and it would be reported one call later, by `substring`, about a number this call produced. The empty needle sits at each end, as it does in the host. Both live in `std::option` beside `strip_prefix`/`parse_i32`, so the always-loaded core `str` stays off the `option` chain. Beside them, three parse errors that each reported something true about the token stream and nothing about the mistake, each now a curated rule recognized structurally: **`pub fun helper()`** reported a missing `;` three columns in — `pub` is an ordinary identifier here, so the statement `pub` simply ran into an item — and now says that vilan has no visibility marker to reach for, that a module's items are importable as written, and that `export` is the *re-export* form and not this; **`let mut x = 1`** reported "found 'let' expected a statement", naming the one token that was right, and now says that `let` and `mut` are the two binding forms rather than a keyword and a modifier, spanning both words; and **a literal `{` in an `i"…"`** reported a failure inside an expression the author never wrote (`found ')' expected an expression` for the bare `{}` pair), and now carries the note that `{` opens an interpolation hole and `\{` is the brace — recognized from the hole's own parenthesized desugaring, so an ordinary broken expression gets no such note. That last one is not a papercut for a generator: a CSS rule, a JS body or a JSON object writes braces on nearly every line. Pinned per case with the negatives beside them — `pub` still an ordinary identifier where it is one, the escaped brace still printing a brace, the misleading messages proven GONE rather than merely joined — and the std pair proven non-vacuous by planting the `-1` leak. (backlog E101; docs `std/strings.md` and the errors appendix)

<!-- family: feature -->
**A `type` binder may be written inside an impl head's bound, and is in scope for the whole head.** `impl type S: Source<type T> with MaybeSignal<T>` — "for every `S` that is a `Source` of some `T`" — reported `cannot find type 'T'` at the `with` clause and again at every member signature that named it. Binders were registered for the subject's own arguments and for the `with` clause's, never for the arguments of a bound the subject's binder carries, so the one place a blanket impl can say what it is generic OVER was the one place a binder could not be introduced. That left only the monomorphic bounded blanket spellable (`impl type S: Source<str> with …`, one impl per element type), which is not what a family like `MaybeSignal` is. The nested binders now register before the bound they sit in is walked, so a bound's binder is the impl's parameter like any other: bound per receiver from the impl its subject provides, reusable by a sibling bound and by the `with` clause, and reported where it is written when no binder declares it. (backlog B165)

<!-- family: feature -->
**`Signal<T>` is a trait, so a signal can be your own — a clamping setter, a persisted cell, a debounced write.** `Source` reads (`get`/`sub`/`effect`) and `Signal` writes: `set` and `notify` are requirements, `set_with` a default of exactly the line std used to carry, and `new` a default-bodied associated function returning the canonical cell. A component bounded on `Signal<u32>` accepts the cell and accepts an implementation whose `set` clamps 1000 to 800 — after which the component, the owner and every observer read 800, because there is one value and the implementation decided it. The trait promises nothing about notification frequency; a cell that wants to skip an unchanged write says so in its own `set`. `update` is deliberately NOT on the trait: its value is in-place mutation with one notify, a generic default could only read-copy-mutate-write-back, and an implementation may want its own or none — so it stays inherent to `SignalCell` and general consumers are locked out of it. `bind_value` and its SSR twin bound on the trait, and the `Slot`/`AttrValue` arms element syntax dispatches through are now blankets over `Source<str>`, so `<p>{anything reactive}</p>` places and `<a href(anything reactive)>` tracks. std also gains `MaybeSignal<T>`, the static-or-reactive component parameter: one effect-shaped `bind`, a blanket for the static case and a `Source`-bounded blanket for the reactive one, chosen at the call with no runtime discrimination. (backlog A32, B157)

<!-- family: feature -->
**`[expose]` asks whether a field's type implements `std::Source`, instead of whether it is spelled `Signal`.** The check matched the written type node — `Signal<Element>` — which made the canonical cell the only exposable type in the language: a storage-backed cell of your own, a mirror over a transport, or a field written through an alias was refused for the wrong reason, and the refusal said "is not a `Signal`". It now reconciles the field's resolved type against the nominal std trait and reads the element off that implementation, so any `Source` is exposable and the Wire rule rides the element the implementation provides rather than the first type argument someone wrote. Widening what may be observed did not widen what may cross the wire. (backlog A32)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A module of a fullstack package is coloured by the entry that REACHES it, in the editor and in `vilan check <file>` alike — not by the manifest's default.** A `[entry.<name>]` package builds one leg per entry, each over the modules that leg loads, each under that leg's target; but a single file addressed by path took the `node` default, and the language server took its own imports' inference. Neither is what the build does, and the platform decides more than which functions may run: it selects `std`'s layer overlay, so it decides what the file's types ARE. Under the process overlay a browser module's `View` is `{ tag, attributes, children, text }`, `self.element` is a field that does not exist, and every browser-only module of a fullstack app was reported broken on correct code while `vilan build` was clean — the report this closes, against an app whose `default-entry` is its server. Reachability answers it: `platform_color::file_platforms` walks the loader's own `pkg::` import graph from each entry (`analyzer::package_modules_reachable_from`, the same `collect_module_refs` seeds and the same resolution the load loop uses), and a module reached by exactly one leg takes that leg's platform. Reached by SEVERAL, it takes each: a shared module is compiled once per leg and must type-check under every one of them, so `vilan check` on it reports the union its legs would and the editor publishes the union too, deduplicated to one squiggle per mistake — the mirror lie, passing a file the package check refuses, is now closed in the same place. Reached by NONE, the designated `default-entry` answers, which is the old behaviour kept where it is the only thing left to say. The two surfaces take one answer from one function: G20 had already unified them, on the manifest's default rather than on reachability. Found fixing it and fixed with it: file mode compiled every path it was handed as if it were the program, so `vilan check src/widget.vl` on a clean module answered "Cannot execute program without a main function" — a demand no module can meet, and the reason the fix could not be verified until it went. An ENTRY still gets the demand, because an entry without `main` is a build that cannot succeed.

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Clippy and `cargo audit` are gates now, which closes N21 — and getting there took 217 findings to zero, deleted two functions nothing called, and removed a test binary with no tests in it.** The `fmt` job shipped a release ago and the item's other two legs did not, so the tree had been accumulating lint findings with nothing to report them and rustc's own warnings with nothing to fail on. `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` and `cargo audit --deny unsound` are jobs in `ci.yml` feeding the required `check`, and `rust-toolchain.toml` now pins `clippy` beside `rustfmt` — the clippy leg deliberately carries no `RUSTUP_TOOLCHAIN`, for the formatter's exact reason: the lint set widens every six weeks, and an unpinned gate that denies warnings goes red for a tree nobody edited, so a lint sweep belongs in the deliberate bump commit. The audit leg is the one that reverses that argument and says so in its comment: an advisory published against an unchanged `Cargo.lock` reds a commit nobody wrote, and it is wired to stop the line anyway, because a rustfmt default moving is a false alarm while a vulnerability in a dependency of a shipped compiler is a true finding that merely arrives on someone else's schedule. Its strictness is chosen rather than inherited — a vulnerability fails, and so does an `unsound` advisory, since a memory-safety defect is that class of problem whatever RustSec calls the row, while `unmaintained` and `yanked` stay warnings that are printed and read: neither is a defect, and an upstream maintainer stepping away must not block a PR that changed a comment. **180 of the 217 findings were fixed, not silenced.** The largest block by far is 102 nested `if`s that edition 2024 lets collapse into let-chains, which is why the analyzer, the transformer and `context.rs` lose a level of indentation in a lot of places; the machine-applicable ones were applied by `clippy --fix` and the result checked the one way that matters for a repository this commented — the multiset of comment lines the diff removes is byte-identical to the one it adds, so nothing was dropped, only reflowed. The rest of the machine-applicable set rode along with it — needless borrows and derefs, `bool::then` closures, a hand-written `Default` impl that is a derive, `map(..).flatten()`, a single-character `push_str` — and one of those is a small find in its own right: an `if !compiling_std {}` in the analyzer's std loader, empty since July, the residue of the `gate_library_imports` call the platform-coloring engine moved out of that function, sitting in the pipeline ever since with nothing to report it. The remainder was hand-fixed: `&PathBuf` and `&mut Vec` parameters that wanted slices, three `Default::default()`-then-assign blocks that are now struct literals, three `let`-`else`s that are `?`, two `loop`/`match`/`break`s that are `while let`, a `.then().unwrap_or()` that is an `if`, and a doc comment in `parsing.rs` whose 21-item chumsky statement order was a paragraph markdown read as a broken list and is now the list it always was. **The exceptions are collected in one place with one reason each** — the root `Cargo.toml`'s `[workspace.lints.clippy]`, which every crate opts into with `[lints] workspace = true`, and which each of the four entries earns by being a decision about the shape of this codebase rather than about one site: `too_many_arguments` (the compiler threads explicit context through its entry points; the tree already carried **22** per-site allows for this one threshold, and those 22 are deleted, because that was one decision written down 22 times), `type_complexity` (a return tuple in the compiler IS the documentation of what a phase yields — two more per-site allows go the same way), `only_used_in_recursion` (the recursive walkers are methods because they belong beside their siblings), and `large_enum_variant` (boxing a hot AST variant trades a memcpy nobody measured for a pointer chase in every match). Three findings are per-site judgments and keep their allow at the site with a `reason`, each recording something worth knowing: `platform_color.rs`'s `to_vec` ends a `self.graph` borrow before `walk` takes `&mut self` and does not compile without it, `interpreter.rs`'s `to_js_string` takes `&mut self` because `self` is the interpreter and the subject is the parameter, and `tests/support/boot.rs` reaps its child through a `try_wait` loop that `zombie_processes` cannot follow. Denying warnings also inherits rustc's, which is how the dead code surfaced: `Document::expr_references_definition` and `Document::use_in_entry_or_generated` — reported as pre-existing by three separate lanes across two orders, and called by nothing — are deleted; `Document::references`/`rename_edits` and `SessionTrace::total_requests`/`stat` are real API that only the pins drive (the server answers through the `_across` union), so they are `#[cfg(test)]` and no longer shipped in the binary; and `tests/parse_expr_fixtures.rs` moved to `tests/parse_expr_regression/fixtures.rs`, since cargo makes a test target out of every `tests/*.rs` and that one had been compiling as a binary containing no tests, whose entire output was four "never used" warnings against the arrays the file beside it consumes. The audit leg is green on arrival: zero vulnerabilities and zero informational advisories across 137 dependencies, so no version moved and `Cargo.lock` is untouched. Wiring both legs into `check` was caught by an existing pin doing exactly its job — `ci_ignored_pins.rs` asserts `check`'s needed jobs LINE BY LINE, so that a non-blocking leg cannot drift into the required status context and strand a PR — and its expected list is updated deliberately, with a note in the failure message, because two senses of "advisory" now meet in that file: the RustSec `audit` job blocks on purpose, while `ignored-pins.yml` is the advisory leg the pin exists to keep out. `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` gain the local ritual beside the `cargo fmt` rule, so the command a lane runs is the command CI runs. (backlog N21, closed)

**A removed std alias now outranks the case-spelling diagnostic on case-ignoring filesystems.** On Windows and macOS, `import std::Default;` resolved to std's own `default.vl` and reported "rename one to match the other" — advice pointing at std's tree — instead of the curated removal steer. The alias check now wins at the one case-mismatch site for std-origin names. Caught by the Windows CI leg on the Order 22 tip (the arm is unreachable on a case-sensitive filesystem, so that leg is its end-to-end check).

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan init`'s three scaffolds adopt the prelude: the web templates declare `prelude = "std::web"` and the std imports it covers are gone from their sources.** The prelude shipped with nothing standing on it — a new user's first project still opened with `import std::reactive::Signal;`, `import std::style::{ Color, Display, FlexDirection, Style, space, style };` and `import std::ui::{ View, view };`, which taught the pre-prelude style as the idiom on the very first file anyone reads. Now the `browser` and `fullstack` manifests carry `prelude = "std::web"` and `node` carries `prelude = "std::prelude"` — the base set, which is also what omitting the key means, written out so the knob is visible where a reader will meet it. Nine std imports across four files became one: `Signal`, `view` and `View` are ambient, so they are simply written; `mount_root` and the style enums come through the ambient MODULES the web set carries, so the scaffolds now read `ui::mount_root("app", …)` and `style::style().display(style::Display::Flex)…` — the module-carried spelling, qualified rather than splatted, which is what the prelude's `style`/`ui` entries exist to demonstrate. What is left imported is left deliberately and says why in a comment beside it: the node template still imports `std::io::assert`, which sits next to `print` in the same module but is not in the prelude (the prelude is a floor, not the module); the full-stack server still imports `std::build`, `std::document` and `std::http`, because a server's surface is not ambient in any set, and its `print` needed no import before or after; and both web templates import `std::style::Style` for one reason worth knowing — a TYPE is written bare, so a return type cannot be spelled through an ambient module today. The full-stack manifest also demonstrates that `prelude` is a PACKAGE key: one declaration governs both entries, and every name in the web set exists on the node layer too, so the server leg shares the client's prelude and loses nothing. Each scaffold is still built and run by `crates/vilan-cli/tests/init.rs`, the browser one down to the compiled `app.css` and the full-stack one down to a served `/client.css`, so the adopted sources are gated the same way they were before.

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The diagnostics ledger and the errors appendix are gated: a message the compiler adds, rewords or drops now reds the suite until the record catches up.** Both records described the compiler's user-facing message surface and neither was checked by anything. The ledger — every message form, its site, its verdict, its pins — went three orders stale (audit run 6's F8): seventy-odd shipped message forms were unrowed, and two rows keyed on sentences that no longer existed, one of them since B163 merged `match` legs and `if` arms behind a `{construct}` slot in the order before. Every previous sweep was a lane reading the tree by hand, twice by an audit's catch and once by eye. The appendix had no equivalent at all (F12): nothing checked that a curated message kept its entry, or that an entry still named something the compiler prints — five flagship messages had none, and six entries quoted sentences the compiler stopped printing. `crates/vilan-cli/tests/diagnostics_ledger.rs` closes both, over a committed index (`diagnostics-ledger.tsv`) that carries one line per ledger row, since the proposals repository the prose lives in is not present in a CI checkout. Three properties over the index: every row's key still appears in the tree, fixed-string, with Rust's string-literal line continuations normalized away first (the artifact that made the two previous mechanical re-keys hand-triaged work); every diagnostic the compiler builds at one of two enumerated anchors — `Error { .. msg: <literal> }` in the compiler crates, `errors.push(<literal>)` in `manifest.rs` — is described by some row's key; and the index is well formed, with each keyless or fragment-less row recorded by number and reason rather than skipped. Three more hold the appendix: every entry's quoted head still names a live message, every entry opens with one (a head that names a CONDITION instead is recorded), and every row the index marks `flagship` is quoted by some entry — so dropping an entry, or rowing a new flagship message without writing one, is red. The appendix now states in its own header what it is: a curated subset by three criteria, not a catalogue. What the gate deliberately does not check is written into its header beside what it does — no verdict, pin, span or anchor is verified, helper-built messages and std's runtime refusals are held by the key check alone rather than enumerated, and `Note` sites are skipped because a C3 note belongs in its primary's row. Regeneration is deliberately not automated: a new message is meant to cost its author a row and a verdict, and a `--fix` flag is how the record went stale in the first place. Both directions are plant-proven red (a reworded message, a new unrowed one, a reworded appendix head, a dropped entry).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Spec §3's normative grammar is gated too, and the gate found four things wrong with it.** `grammar_sync.rs` has held the two syntax-HIGHLIGHTING grammars to the compiler's word tables for releases, and the anchors golden pins the book's headings, but nothing read the EBNF itself — which is how `extern-args` went three forms stale, and one form WRONG, across two releases with nothing red (N34, the smalls lane's finding). `crates/vilan-cli/tests/grammar_ebnf.rs` checks four things and says plainly in its header that it is not a parser-equivalence check: every `lexing::KEYWORDS` word is spelled as a terminal somewhere in the EBNF and every identifier-shaped terminal the EBNF quotes is a word the compiler knows (contextual words recorded with what each is); every `KNOWN_ATTRIBUTE_MARKERS` name appears, asserted on its own because that is the D15 rot site; the `extern-args` production offers exactly the form words `extern_binding_from_args` matches on, read from the parser's own source rather than from a second table, with `retains` in trailing position as the flag it is; and the grammar is internally closed — every rule a production names is one some production writes or a token class §2 declares, and every production is reached from another or is the start symbol. Bringing it green fixed four real gaps: `operator-expr` was named and never written, so `await` and `is` appeared nowhere in the normative grammar (only in §3.7's precedence table); `place` and `literal` were referenced with prose definitions alone; `struct-init` was written and reached by nothing, although §3.8's own prose makes it an operand; and the `if`/`for`/`match` productions said `secondary-expr` where §3.8 says those three positions parse `condition-expr`. Red-proven on all three axes: a form word the parser gains and the grammar does not, a keyword the grammar drops, a production naming a rule nobody writes.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**An empty subdirectory under a declared hook input woke a `--watch` round that then did nothing, because the watcher and the freshness stamp read the same directory differently.** The watcher inserts an entry per nested directory — N30's rule, which is why a declared directory *appearing* is visible at all — and the stamp's walk pushed files and symlinks only. So `mkdir icons/empty` produced `change detected, re-running` followed by `Fresh   gen`: a round that reliably does nothing. That is the safe failure direction, a spurious round rather than a missed one, and it is still a defect, because the two consumers of one declaration were reading it two ways and the freshness gate was answering about a tree the thing that woke it does not share. **The stamp learns directory rows, rather than the watcher unlearning its entries**, and the argument is N30's own: a directory appearing is a change that nothing about its (absent) files can express — which is precisely why the watcher gained the root's own entry — and a nested directory is that same rule one level down, so the reading to keep is the one already settled. It is also the reading `inputs = ["src/static"]` looks like it has: `cp -r`, `tar` and `rsync` all carry an empty directory, and a copy hook that stopped re-running when one appeared would be the silent-stale-artifact class, where the extra round is merely cheap. N30's own pin is untouched and green — the watcher did not change at all. While the row was being added, **each row gained its KIND** (`file <digest>`, `link <digest of the target path>`, `dir`) rather than the directory getting a digest of no bytes, which would be the exact row an empty *file* at that key produces: a generator replacing a directory of parts with a single file is not an exotic edit, and a stamp that cannot see it is a hook that never runs again. The kind row is pinned by planting the digest-of-nothing alternative — the kind-swap pin goes red on it and the empty-directory pin stays green, which is what makes the two rows distinct rather than merely present. One upgrade note: a hook whose declared directory input contains subdirectories re-runs once on the first build after this change, because its recorded tree now describes more of the tree than it did. Three pins, two of them red first, and the third the end-to-end one that gives the item its shape — a `--watch` session where `mkdir` under the declared input both starts the round and re-runs the hook, measured before the fix at `rounds.txt` 2, `ran.txt` 1 for the whole liveness bound. (backlog G16, audit run 5's F4; docs `guide/dev-loop.md`)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A `[[build.hook]]` whose declared directory input is a *symlink* re-ran the hook on every build, forever, and said nothing.** The declaration has two consumers and the whole design of it is that they read it the same way — the freshness stamp and `--watch`'s wake-up set — and on a link they did not. The watcher resolved the declared path through the link (`fs::metadata`) and watched the tree behind it; the stamp stat'd the link itself (`symlink_metadata`), so a link to a directory was not `is_dir()`, fell through to the file path, got `EISDIR` from the read, and digested as *unreadable* — which is never fresh and is never recorded, so the hook ran on every single build. Nothing reported it: the `Fresh` line you were promised simply never appeared, and an expensive generator paid full cost forever. A link to a FILE hid the split completely, because reading one follows it — which is exactly why the code comment beside the watcher claimed the two already matched, and that comment is fixed with the code. Measured on the item's repro: three builds over `inputs = "linked_static"` ran the hook three times where the same tree declared by its real name ran it once. It is the silent-forever-loop class the generated root closed two slices ago, at a different door. The fix is that the declared path resolves through the link, exactly as the watcher resolves it — a link is one of the ordinary ways a static tree gets its name in a package, and hashing what the declaration *reaches* is what "one reading of the manifest" means. **Inside a tree nothing changes and nothing may:** a link found under a declared directory still hashes as its own target path and is never followed, because following one could walk out of the declared tree or into a cycle, and that fence is pinned against a re-planted follow (a re-pointed link at a byte-identical file must still be a change, which is invisible to a walk that follows it). One reading falls out of the same resolution: **a link with no target is MISSING**, not unreadable — the answer the watcher already gives it — which is a real improvement in both positions. A broken input link used to be the same forever-loop one level down; it is now recorded as missing, is fresh on the next build, and invalidates when the target appears. A broken *output* link used to be neither recorded nor reported, so the hook re-ran silently — the precise failure the "did not write it" note exists to prevent — and is now named, by hook and by path. Four pins, three of them red first on the unfixed tree and the fourth proven by planting the follow it forbids; they are `cfg(unix)` like every other symlink pin in the tree, since Windows does not grant link creation by default. (backlog G15, audit run 5's F3; docs `guide/dev-loop.md`)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The editor now knows what a `css` block is: property names and condition heads paint as themselves, completion offers CSS instead of your scope, three refusals ship with the fix that answers them, and the two spellings convert into each other.** This is the arc's last slice, and its whole shape was decided two slices ago. Element syntax reached its own LSP slice and found *"the desugar's scaffolding spans were the real fix"* — the generated wide spans had painted `<div` as a function and attribute names as methods — which was repair work in S5 for a decision made in S2. So the css desugar cut zero-width anchors for every accessor it generates and carried the property-name, value and per-declaration spans from its first commit whether or not it used them, and the gate on the whole arc was that **S5 must require no S2 rework**. It required none: `crates/vilan-core/src/css.rs` is byte-identical after this slice, and every span this needed was already there. **Semantic tokens** come from a raw re-parse, which is the same machinery the markup tokens ride and for the same reason — `Node::Css` is retired before analysis, so property names and condition heads exist in no analyzed program — and the one accessor that deliberately KEPT a real span (the outer `style()`, at the `css` keyword, so a missing `import std::style::style` underlines the word that asked for a `Style`) is suppressed here exactly as `<div`'s is, or the keyword would light up as a function. A property paints as a property, a condition head as the method it lowers to; the two are the precise form of the TextMate rule's two regex approximations, one parse serving both sub-languages. **Completion** answers the four positions the design named — property, custom property, dotted, hole: an undotted item offers CSS property names except at a `--` custom property, whose name is the author's and gets nothing; a dotted one offers the condition combinators; a `{…}` hole is ordinary expression ground; and value position offers nothing by design (the design's own exclusion), since a binding's NAME is what would land on the sheet. The part that matters is where the vocabulary comes from — E67 refused to invent an HTML attribute list on the ground that it "would be a second source of truth with nothing to gate it", and this clears that objection on a real disanalogy rather than an exception: every property offered is a slot some `Style` method already writes, read out of the canonical-order table's own `properties` column, which six gates in `style_table_sync.rs` hold to `style.vl`'s method bodies. It is std's surface, not a list. A value position offering the enclosing scope would be worse than offering nothing, because a value is source text — a binding's NAME is what would land on the sheet — and value completion proper needs a property→enum map that does not exist. All of it lands in the editor and the playground at once, because it lives in `vilan-ide` and needed no new completion kind to reach either. **Three quickfixes** join the two a block already inherited. `#` and `@` cannot lex at all — lexing is context-free and finishes before the parser exists, so a block cannot make them lex there either — which means each diagnostic is one column wide and each fix reads its subject off the line: `color: #336699` becomes `color: {Color::hex("#336699")}`, and `@media (min-width: 768px) {` becomes `.md {`, with the breakpoint chosen by the query's own min-width and an arbitrary one becoming `.media("900px")` rather than no fix at all. That width map is not a fourth copy of std's breakpoints: it sits beside the condition table and a fifth `style_table_sync.rs` gate holds each row to the `self.media("768px", inner)` the method actually delegates with. `!important` removes itself, space included. Each fix declines where it cannot be faithful — a `#` that is not a colour (3, 4, 6 or 8 hex digits, and the run must end there) keeps the rule and gets no edit, and the three at-rules with no combinator spelling get none either. **And the two spellings now convert into each other**, which is the estate's migration path and the first `refactor.rewrite` action the server has ever offered: the cursor in a block offers "Convert to a `style()` chain", the cursor in a `style()` chain offers "Convert to a `css` block", and on everything they accept the two are inverses — a block converted and converted straight back comes out byte-identical, nesting included. They are mechanical because the lowering is total and one-to-one: a declaration is a `.raw` link, a nested rule is a combinator link carrying the inner chain as its final argument, and there is no third row. Which is also why the inverse is PARTIAL, and says so by not being offered: `.padding(space(4))` writes its slot through `with_length`, which is not the node `padding: {space(4)};` lowers to, so a chain carrying a typed property method has no block spelling this refactor is entitled to invent. Two more refusals, both about meaning rather than shape: a **comment** anywhere inside the construct stops the conversion, because its attachment is not recoverable across the reshape — the same refusal `vilan fmt` makes when it declines to reorder a commented block — and so does a value carrying a **backslash**, because a chain's string literal has its escapes processed at emission and a block's token run does not, so the two spellings would quietly stop meaning the same thing. A quoted value is fine: escaping a `"` into the literal round-trips exactly. Twenty-three pins, each red first and the refusals each proven by re-planting the guard they rest on; nothing in the desugar, `class_hash` or any emission path was touched, and no corpus golden moves. (proposal/css-block.md §7 and §11's S5; docs `appendix/editor.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**One platform-coloring mistake now draws one diagnostic.** Constructing a `@process` resource in a browser build — `let file = File::open("data.txt")` — reported twice: the construction's rejection, and a second one for the compiler-inserted teardown, anchored inside `std`'s own `File::drop` at a position in a file the user cannot edit. `Database` showed it identically, so it was the resource class rather than `File`. Two causes, both general. The synthetic destruction edge (`destruction.md` §8) carried no site at all — the teardown is inserted by the transformer and has no spelling in the source — so its diagnostic fell back to the offending node's own definition, which is by construction inside the library that seeded the requirement. The edge now carries the CONSTRUCTION it answers to: the initializer behind a `let`, the assignment behind an overwrite, the argument behind an early `drop(x)`, the expression itself for an owning temporary, and the owner's site for a member reached through the drop glue. And coloring had no dedup at all: violations now collapse on `(anchor, layer, what the chain hangs from)`, and one raised beneath a teardown edge is dropped when the construction it answers to is already rejected for the same cause — which is what closes `Option::Some(File::open(p))`, the sanctioned container, where the rejected `open` sits *inside* the construction rather than at it. The same dedup fixes a third instance nobody had filed: a fence over a FAMILY is checked against every host in it, so `[platform("@process")]` reaching browser-only code drew three identical diagnostics differing only in which host was named — one broken promise, now reported once, with the fence still quoted so the family is not lost. Distinct mistakes still each report, which is the other half of the claim and pinned as such: two off-platform calls in one function, two fences broken the same way, and a resource whose only off-platform surface is its `Drop` standing beside an unrelated call. (backlog E98, found by Order 17's fs-handles lane)
