<!-- family: tooling -->
**A `[[build.hook]]`'s declared `inputs` now wake `--watch`, not just the freshness stamp.** The declaration had two consumers and only one of them was reading it: the stamp re-ran a hook the moment a declared input's bytes moved, while the watch loop polled `.vl` sources and nothing else — so editing a file the manifest names in `inputs` produced **zero rounds**, and the hook re-ran only when some unrelated `.vl` save happened to wake the session (measured on 0.39.0 with a lucide icon pipeline: three edits to declared inputs, no round each time, the banner cheerfully reporting `watching . for .vl changes`). The wake-up set now extends to every declared input, resolved against the manifest's own directory the way the stamp resolves them — so an input outside the watch root is watched exactly as it is stamped — and a declared **directory** means its tree, which is already what the stamp reads it as: adding a file under `inputs = ["icons"]`, or editing one inside it, starts a round. One reading of the manifest, both consumers. This is the recorded-inputs doctrine `markdown.md` §11 settled for `const asset::read`, and it is literally the same mechanism: hook inputs join the set the compile's read inputs already join, rather than a parallel one. Declared `outputs` are deliberately left out — watching what a hook writes would let a build trigger the round that ran it, which is the invariant only `.vl` tracking has protected so far, and the negative is pinned. The session banner now says what it watches, too: it read `watching . for .vl changes` while already polling recorded `asset::read` inputs beside the scan, and that understatement is how the defect came to be diagnosed backwards (the banner looked truthful, so the stamp looked guilty). Pinned by three bounded `--watch` sessions (an edited file input rounds; an added file under a declared directory rounds; an undeclared file does not, on a session proven still alive), the first two proven red first: both waited out the full liveness bound against the unfixed tree. (backlog G10; `build-hooks.md` §9's owed watch-round pin; docs `guide/dev-loop.md`)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A build hook that generates Vilan and `vilan fmt` were undoing each other forever — a package can now declare where its generated sources live, and the formatter leaves them alone.** The loop was live and silent in both directions. A `[[build.hook]]` names the module it writes in `outputs`; freshness digests declared outputs by content; so a `vilan fmt` that reformatted that file re-staled the hook, the next build re-ran the generator, the generator wrote the file unformatted again — a generator emits what its templates produce, not what the formatter would — and the next format rewrote it. Neither tool is doing anything wrong from where it stands, nothing reports a loop, and with format-on-save the whole cycle runs on a file you only opened to read. It also defeats the freshness gate exactly where that gate was aimed: the `Fresh <name>` line you were promised never appears, and an expensive generator pays full cost on every build after every format. The fix is one manifest key, `[package] generated = "src/icons"` (and the same key on `[library]`), naming the directory that holds the package's *products*: `vilan fmt` and `vilan fmt --check` leave every source file under it byte-identical, and say so once per run with a count and the root's name — a dim `note:`, never a warning, with the exit code unchanged, because skipping a product is the right answer rather than a degraded one. The exclusion is deliberately unconditional, holding even when you name the file on the command line, and it is one predicate in the compiler rather than a filter in the CLI's directory walk — which is what lets your editor's format-on-save honor exactly the same rule, since it reaches a file by its exact path and nothing else. A rule expressed as a walk filter could not have covered the case that most needed covering. The key is validated where the other layout keys are, with four named refusals: it must be relative and free of `..` (lexically, before any filesystem look, the way `asset::read` already refuses an escape), it may not name the package directory itself, and it may not be the source `root` — pointing it there would silently stop formatting every hand-written module in the package, so the message names both keys. A root that does not exist yet is fine; before the first build it usually doesn't, and validation never touches the disk. A declaration that *is* refused excludes nothing, so a typo fails towards formatting too much rather than towards a formatter that has quietly switched off. Nothing else reads the key: module resolution is untouched, and a generated module still resolves where it always did — `src/icons/lib.vl` is `pkg::icons`, which is the configuration that makes the whole thing useful today. The escape is the manifest rather than a flag: if a file should be formatted it isn't a product, so move it out of the root or drop the key, and either way the change lands in review. Pinned end to end — the loop, dead, across a build → format → build cycle, and the same cycle with the key absent kept as the record of the defect it closes. (proposal/build-hooks.md §12, slice S6, on the owner's Q5 ruling; the loop was found by Order 19's lucide evidence run; docs `guide/dev-loop.md`, `appendix/cli.md`, `tour/projects.md` and `spec/platform.md` §11.4)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A parse error in an imported module now anchors where it is, instead of at line 1 in prose.** The same syntax error rendered at its true position in the ENTRY file and at 1:1 in a module: the loader rendered `line N, column M` into the message and pushed an EMPTY span, so everything a span buys was gone — the caret in the terminal, the squiggle in the editor, the position an editor can jump to, and the file-then-position order the diagnostic sort imposes. Generated code made the cost acute rather than cosmetic: one bad character out of a generator produced **798 errors, every one of them at line 1 of an 18k-line file, in an order nothing determined**. The span was never missing — `render_at` had `error.span` in hand at the moment it threw it away — so the fix is to stop discarding it: a loaded module carries `(span, reason)` pairs the way the entry has always carried the parser's own error, and the module's `SourceId` (which the loader already attributed) is what gives the span a file. Both loader paths change together — the process-global cache and the analysis-owned overlay path the language server uses — so an error reads the same however it reaches you. `vilan build` now points at `util.vl:2:11` with the source line under a caret where it used to name the file in a sentence. One consumer keeps the old shape deliberately: `vilan check <library>` walks a package's modules without registering any of them as a source and renders messages only, so with no file to hang a span on the position stays in the text. Pinned per case: the true line and the exact token spanned, several errors in one module keeping distinct spans in source order, the module error being the parser's own message and span unaltered, the library-contract path keeping its prose, and the end-to-end terminal rendering — each red on the unfixed tree, where the multi-error module reported `[1, 1]`. (backlog E100, measured by the lucide evidence run)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan build --explain` — every `dist/` file names what put it there.** The const channel scatters its contributions across files *by construction* — that scatter IS import-driven composition — so "where did this output come from?" was a grep, and for an accumulated kind a grep over every `emit` site in the program. The compiler held the answer the whole time and never said it out loud: each channel call knows its `const` site, the flush knows which kind each line landed in, the copy knows what file it carried, and the hook runner knows what ran and what was `Fresh`. The new flag builds — exactly as `vilan build` does, because explaining a stale tree would describe whatever the last build happened to leave — and then prints one block per file it wrote: an accumulated kind lists the `const` sites that contributed to it (`emitted  src/theme.vl:9`), a bundled copy its source and the site that named it with the spelling used (`named  src/client.vl:17 (asset::bundle_as)`), a hook's declared output the hook and this build's verdict (`hook  icons (Fresh)`), and the build's own files their role in the words the build already uses to refuse a resource that tries to take one of their names — `compiled bundle`, `build manifest`, `route chunk`. Then one block per tracked input — `read`/`read_dir`/`read_dir_all`/`digest`, a bundle's source read, a `[[build.hook]]`'s declared `inputs` — with an `invalidates` line per output a change to it would move. That last join is read off the records rather than reasoned about: a const input moves its leg's compiled bundle (the channel's inputs are sources to the compile, which is why editing one starts a `--watch` round — so a `digest` whose value reaches no file but the bundle still gets a true answer) plus the flushes and copies of the sites that read it, and a hook input moves that hook's declared outputs; the build manifest is left out on purpose, because every build of a browser leg rewrites it and naming it under every input would be true and would say nothing. The verb READS and SAYS: no second source of truth, and the only record extension is a `const` site's location beside each channel fact — needed because the operational halves are deduplicated and sorted precisely so a build's bytes are a function of the SET of contributions and never of call order, which is the property that erases who contributed. Every output line is written by the same call that writes the file, so a build that stops producing something stops explaining it. A site is the enclosing `const` expression rather than the `emit` call inside it, which is the granularity the record keeps (the const evaluator runs a lowered tree whose frames carry function names and no spans) and the one a const-eval *error* already points at. The shape is line-oriented and a contract — fixed keys, paths spelled as the build's own `Compiled`/`Emitted`/`Bundled` lines spell them — so `grep '^output'` lists a `dist/` and two builds diff cleanly; it is the refusal doctrine's cousin, a build that can refuse by name being able to explain by name. `--stdout` is refused with it: that flag prints a bundle, not a build, so there is nothing to explain and a report would corrupt the one stream it exists to produce. A plain `vilan build` prints not one line of any of it.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The raw table under `Map` and `Set` now refuses a resource, closing a use-after-free nothing public could reach.** `NativeMap::insert(&mut self, key: Hash, value: V)` declares its value BARE, and a bare parameter is a loan: with C11's temporary predicate back at its ruled width, the caller destroys the value it hands over *at the insert statement* while the table goes on holding it. Measured, not inferred — `drop r`, then `after`, on a program the compiler accepted, with the host map still pointing at a freed value. It is B153's third sibling, and the one that sweep left open because the obvious fix is the wrong one: declaring `own value: V` closes it by buying a redundant deep copy on EVERY `Map` and `Set` insert in the language — twelve corpus goldens move — to fence a module the standard library documents as not public surface, imports in exactly two places (`std::map`, `std::set`), and no docs page mentions. The zero-cost fix is that R10 already refuses a resource argument to `List`, `Map`, `Set`, `Shared`, `Context`, `Promise` and `Task`, and the internal head simply was not in the list. It is now, so no resource ever reaches `insert` and the bare declaration is honest again. The key position stays bare and is pinned as such: a `Hash` is a non-generic external struct nothing can declare `resource`, so no destructor can ever hang on it. **Reachability is the reason this is hardening rather than a miscompile you might have hit:** `Map<K, resource>` and `Set<resource>` are refused at their own heads and always were, so the only route to the defect was importing `std::native_map` directly, which is why the ruling took the free fix over the language-wide one. Adding the head opened a diagnostic question worth more than the head itself. `Map<K, V>` stores a `NativeMap<(K, V)>` and `Set<T>` a `NativeMap<T>`, so a `Map<str, Database>` now offends at BOTH heads — and reported at both, the second one anchored inside `std`'s own `Map::new` at a line the user cannot edit. One mistake is one diagnostic, and the collapse is general rather than a name check: the per-instantiation body scan exists for the container a generic builds out of its own `T` and never hands back (`fun stash<T>(own value: T) { let items = [value]; }`, where no type the caller holds is a `List<Guard>`), so it now stands down exactly when the callee's own instantiated SIGNATURE is refused and the caller has already been told — the offense is the type they wrote, and the storage inside it is not a second thing to fix. That is E98's shape reused: a consequence is dropped when the thing it is a consequence of is already rejected. "Already told" is asked of the reported set rather than assumed, so a refusal nobody saw can never silence the body's account of it, and the other half of the claim is pinned too — `stash` still reports. Every `Map` and `Set` diagnostic in the tree is byte-identical to before. Nine pins: the ignored one from B153 un-ignored and passing (it compiled cleanly on the unfixed tree), the measured use-after-free program, a `NativeMap` of a non-resource still compiling and running, the bare `Hash` key still readable after the insert it was passed to, `Map` and `Set` each reporting exactly once, neither construction naming the `NativeMap` inside it (both red first, on the head fix without the collapse), and the generic-body container the caller cannot see still reported. (backlog B154, found by Order 20's b153 lane and audit run 4; docs `spec/memory.md` R10)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A `--watch` round whose action fails is retried once, instead of losing the change that started it.** The loop consumed a snapshot difference BEFORE it ran the round — `snapshot = next; action()` — so a round that failed for a transient reason could never re-fire: the difference was spent, and the session sat healthy and silent until some OTHER file happened to move. That is the right design for a compile error, whose fix IS the next edit and whose retry would spin on a broken tree; it is a silently lost round for a `cmd` hiccup on a loaded runner, a hook racing something outside the tree, or any other flake, and it is what a Windows CI leg had already been diagnosed as once. The ruling is a restored difference and ONE retry: a failed round leaves the old snapshot in place, so the next poll sees the same difference and runs the round again — and a second consecutive failure consumes the difference and goes back to waiting for the next change, which is the posture the loop has always had, now the fallback rather than the whole policy. The guard is once, and once is what makes it safe to apply to failures the loop cannot classify: a broken tree retried once compiles-fails a second time and then rests, one extra compile per broken save, where anything unbounded would hammer a tree nothing but an edit can fix. Nor can it spin — a retry is an ordinary round riding the poll interval, and a change landing *during* a pending retry is folded into that retry rather than refreshing its budget, so "at most two runs per difference" holds however edits are timed. The reason this could not be done before is that the verdict was invisible: `run_or_watch` took an action returning `ExitCode` and discarded it, and `ExitCode` is write-only — it is built from a verdict and handed to the process, and nothing can read the verdict back out of it. So the three commands a watch session drives (`build`, `check`, `test`) now report a `RoundOutcome` the loop can read, and the exit code is minted at the one boundary that needs one; every fallible step beneath them already answered with `ExitCode::FAILURE` and nothing else, so no code carried information the verdict does not. `run --watch` deliberately keeps today's posture, and the reason is recorded at the code rather than assumed: that path has no honest failure signal to thread — an HMR round and a plain restart round both answer with an `Option<ManagedChild>`, where `None` means "no Node child to hold" (which a browser-only workspace produces on a perfectly good round) and a failed round hands back the previous child, alive — while `WatchState::failed` is the classifier's recompile-everything flag rather than a verdict, and the failure it would carry is the compile error the ruling declines to retry, at the price of a second full recompile of every leg and a second error overlay per broken save. Inventing a signal there would have bought the one retry nobody asked for. The session says which round is which: a retry announces itself, the first failure says it will retry on the next poll, and the second says it is waiting for the next change — the narration a `--watch` log needs to explain itself after the fact, which is how this defect was found in the first place. Two bounded watch sessions pin it, both proven red first (each waited out the full liveness bound against the unfixed tree, its log showing one failed round and then silence): a `[build] run` command armed to fail exactly once loses NO round — the edited input's hook still runs, and the pin deliberately does not re-touch its trigger the way the positive watch pins do every 20 s, so the retry is the only thing that can produce that second run — and a command that fails every time runs exactly TWICE per change, held across a window scaled to the machine's own first round, then rounds normally once disarmed. The undeclared-file negative is untouched and still green. (tracker G14, filed by Order 20's close; docs `guide/dev-loop.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Two different errors at one offset in an imported module are both reported again.** Giving a module parse error its real span (E100) came with a new dedup — one module reaches the loader through several seams, and the cache hands each seam the same errors — but the key it chose was `(file, position)` where the code it replaced keyed the full rendered message. Position is not what makes an error distinct: a stray `"` produces two refusals over the same one character, the LEXER's (an unterminated string cannot span lines) and the PARSER's, over the tokens that survive (this statement has no `;`), and the second one silently vanished. The audit caught it as a key narrowed without the doc comment noticing the reason had been dropped. The key is now `(file, position, reason)` — all three of what an error *is* — so the dedup still collapses the same error arriving through a re-export and a direct import, and no longer collapses two errors that merely happen to land together. Pinned both ways round: the two-refusals-at-one-offset module, red before the fix with the parser's half missing, and a module reached through two seams still reporting each of its errors exactly once. (backlog E102, audit run 4 finding 8)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`dist/` now sweeps a bundled resource the build stopped naming — the last of the four writers sharing that directory to learn the rule.** `write_bundled` copied files and wrote nothing down, alone among its three neighbours: `sweep_stale_chunks`, `sweep_stale_sidecar` and the per-kind prune all treat their outputs as belonging to the leg's LAST build, and the per-kind prune's own doc comment states why — a stale flush in `dist/` SHIPS, which is worse than a missing file under "a built app needs nothing but `dist/`". A copy had no such counterpart, so `dist/` accumulated. The gap pre-dates the estate verbs and they made it reachable with no source edit at all and UNBOUNDED: `read_dir_all` means a DELETION out of a listed tree is invisible to the copy (the listing loses the file, the folded array loses the url, and the copy in `dist/` stays), and the `digest`-minted fingerprint recipe mints a new name on every save, so a `--watch` session orphans one copy per edit for the life of the session. `serve_build` routes from the manifest and so never serves a stray, which is exactly why nothing noticed — but `dist/` is the DEPLOY artifact, and a static host in front of it goes on answering for a `robots.txt` the source tree no longer has. The fix is the mechanism that was already there, extended: every build records the copies it carried and the next build removes a recorded copy no leg names any more. It is a SEPARATE record file (`.vilan-bundled`) beside the kind record rather than more rows in it, and that is a determination rather than a convenience: a record is a row *and* the file that row names, and the two name their files by different rules — a kind row names `<leg>.<kind>`, a bundled row names its target verbatim, so in one file `client/logo.svg` would be ambiguous between `dist/client.logo.svg` and `dist/logo.svg`, and a pruner guessing wrong deletes a file it does not own. A bundled name may also carry `/` (a subdirectory is what `bundle` keeps and what `bundle_as` may spell, and it is the sanctioned escape from a name a leg's build owns) where a kind is fenced to one segment, so one file would need two row grammars; and each record is removed with its last row so it never becomes its own stale artifact, which sharing would tie to whether the *other* mechanism had anything to say. What the two do share is the row shape and both record primitives, so there is one format and one pruning law, not two. **That law is the whole safety argument and it is G6's, unchanged: the pruner acts only on its own record.** A file placed by hand beside the copies, another leg's outputs, and anything unrecorded are untouchable however bundle-shaped the name — the general "delete whatever this build did not write" sweep is still deliberately unbuilt. Rows are keyed by leg because `dist/` is one directory every leg copies into, with the one subtlety that shape forces: a name is NOT one leg's to delete just because that leg stopped naming it, since two legs bundling one file to one url is legal and expected, so a file is removed only when the record the build is about to WRITE no longer names it at all. A resource that is already its own destination is deliberately never recorded — that is the case `same_file` exists to protect, and recording it would put the build's own source on a future build's deletion list. Six pins, each proven red first by planting the defect: the disappearance pin's missing `dist/` half (a file dropped out of a listed tree takes its copy with it), the fingerprint's orphan (the old digest's copy is gone and the new one is there), the record's exact contents, an empty record removing itself, a hand-placed file surviving the sweep that empties the record around it, and the cross-leg case — where dropping the SECOND leg's bundle is what makes the pin non-vacuous, because dropping the first one's lets the second's copy put the file back and hide a prune that had no business running. (backlog G13, audit run 4 finding 2; docs `std/misc.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The extern documentation now names `retains` where externs are actually written.** The flag that tells the compiler a host KEEPS what it is handed — an event listener, a queued callback, a stashed value — appeared in the whole book at two lines, both inside the memory chapter's retention section. Every page a person reads while binding a host API was silent about it: the grammar's `extern-attr` production did not admit the flag *at all*, the bindgen appendix stated the `[extern(…)]`/`[platform(…)]` ordering rule without ever naming the third thing that can appear in the attribute, and the platforms tour's four-binding-forms table listed four forms and no flags. That silence has a sharp edge — omitting `retains` where it applies is a use-after-free, the compiler is free to destroy the argument's binding at its last use, and nothing warns — so the fix is to name the flag at each form reference and point at `spec/memory.md` for what it promises. The grammar gains the trailing `[ "," "retains" ]` and the note that it is a FLAG recognized in trailing position only, which is what lets it compose with every binding shape; the tour's table gains the composing sentence with the listener spelling; §5.3 gains it beside `external struct`; and the bindgen page says the part only bindgen's reader needs — a `.d.ts` cannot express retention, so the generator emits no flag and you add it yourself, on the very `Marker.on` binding the page prints. (backlog N29, audit run 4 finding 6)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Creating a declared directory that the build was waiting for now starts a `--watch` round, even when it is empty.** A directory input means its whole TREE to both the freshness stamp and the watcher — the reading G10 unified — but the watcher expanded it to its FILES and inserted nothing for the directory itself. So the directory's own *existence* was not in the watched set, and a recorded-missing directory appearing EMPTY was invisible: `asset::read_dir` had failed on it and recorded the miss, `mkdir` made the same call succeed against an empty listing, and the session sat still. The narrow shape is what made it easy to miss — the first file created inside the directory *did* fire, so the only silent step was the one where you create the directory and then look for what to put in it. A directory now contributes its own entry alongside its files, and each half answers exactly what the other cannot: the directory's entry makes its appearance and its membership observable, its files' entries make an edit *inside* it observable (a directory's own mtime does not move for that). Nested directories get the same treatment for the same reason — a subdirectory appearing empty is a change to the tree that no file entry can express. Measured on the fixture before and after: round 1, then `mkdir icons` and nothing at all, then a file inside firing round 2; now `mkdir icons` fires it, with a quiet window after proving the extra entries add no spurious rounds. Pinned end-to-end against a live `--watch` session and at the snapshot, both red on the unfixed tree — the end-to-end one waiting out its whole liveness bound. (backlog N30, audit run 4 finding 10)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**An `#[ignore]` reason must now name a tracker item, and a gate says so.** Two house rules meet at an ignored test — a known-but-unfixed bug is pinned `#[ignore]`d, and an open defect is not tracked unless it has an item — and nothing enforced the join, so a defect could live its whole life inside a test attribute. It repeatedly did: audit runs 2, 3 and **4 each found an ignored pin whose bug the tracker had never heard of** (run 4's was a measured host-side use-after-free, now B154), each discoverable only by someone reading test attributes on purpose. The gate sweeps every tracked `.rs` file for `#[ignore]` and requires each reason to carry something shaped like an item id — capitals then digits, `B154`/`E102` — with a short EXPLICIT allowlist for the ignores that are deliberately not bugs. The allowlist is a list of exact reason strings, never a pattern: matching "run deliberately" would turn the escape hatch into a phrase anyone can spell their way into, and the point is that adding a member is a decision visible in a diff. Its members are the two perf baselines, the leak soak, and the `mdbook` tool gate — cost and tooling, where nothing is broken and running them in the ordinary suite would buy a wrong answer or none. The sweep enumerates through `git ls-files`, so it reads the committed tree and can never wander into `target/` or a sibling worktree; it recognizes an attribute only where one is written (at the start of a line), which is what keeps it off the many `#[ignore]` mentions in this tree's prose, and it decodes a reason across the `\` line breaks a long one takes. The sweep's own two halves are pinned beside it — the id shape accepts ids and refuses prose, and the scanner finds the attribute and not the doc comment above it — and the gate itself was proven by planting both failures. One existing pin was corrected on the way: the `NativeMap` one named the spec rule it violates and not the item filed for it. One was allowlisted with a `TODO(orchestrator)` note instead of being given an invented id — the async-return-assimilation gap has no item, and the gate should not be the thing that files one. (backlog N31, audit run 4's process recommendation)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A connection that closes for good now takes its client's graph with it — the terminal `Closed` disposes the `ReactiveClient` that was holding the mirrors.** A26 gave the reconnect path two honest refusals: a re-dialled server whose contract has DRIFTED, and one that answers `__contract` and then REFUSES the `__attach`. Both reach the terminal `Closed` and close the socket, because a mirror that cannot be rebound is pointed at the previous connection's dead channel ids and would never update again. What the refusal did not do was let go. The client kept every route — one deliverer per mirror, each holding that mirror's cache — and the transport kept the inbound handler `ReactiveClient::new` installed, which captures the whole client: the exact `client → transport → handler → client` retention A29 measured and gave `dispose` to cut, left standing on the one connection that can be certain it will never carry another frame. A wedged client is now a disposed one. `close_for_good` is the single line both branches take — state, then `close()`, then the client's own `dispose()` — so the honest report and the release happen together rather than one of them being remembered. The ORDER is deliberate and the pins read it: a `connection_state` observer runs inside `state.set(Closed)`, one line before the disposal, so an app watching the signal sees the state it is being told about and not a half-torn-down client. Nothing a program can call changes. A disposed client's mirrors read their last value forever, which is what they did before — the socket was closed, so nothing was going to arrive on it either way — and that is the same reason A29's repairs were unobservable. What moves is what stays reachable after the app has been told the connection is over. The negative is the load-bearing half and it is pinned as such: an ORDINARY redial disposes nothing, and neither does the third failure the same function meets — the server unreachable when the re-attach runs, which is a delay rather than a desync, because that socket keeps trying and its mirrors resync when it succeeds. Disposal belongs only where the mirrors are unrecoverable, and `reattach_mirrors`'s three arms are exactly that distinction written out. `dispose` is idempotent by construction (both lines assign an empty), which stops being incidental the moment std calls it: an app that tore its own client down before the socket gave up must not be punished for it. Four pins, each proven red first — the refused re-attach and the drift case ride A26's failure-mode-as-a-spawn-argument harness against real servers and assert the disposal on top of the existing `Closed` assertions, the resync case asserts the opposite over the same wire (removing the wiring reddens both terminals and leaves the negative green; wiring the disposal onto every reconnect reddens the negative), and the double-dispose is a unit pin reading the routes count and the handler slot across two passes. The standing heap-snapshot SCC gate is unmoved and could not have moved: its exemplar disposes its client by hand over an in-process `duplex_pair`, with no socket and no `reattach_mirrors` in reach. (backlog A30, ruled 2026-08-29; completes A26; proposal/lifetimes.md §5 V5, proposal/transport-robustness.md §2.5; docs `std/rpc.md`, `guide/services.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A second, unrelated container refusal is no longer swallowed by the first one's stand-down.** B154 gave R10's per-instantiation body scan a stand-down, and it was the right rule stated correctly — *a refusal the caller has already been told about is not repeated a layer deeper, and a refusal nobody saw never silences the body's account of it* — but it was IMPLEMENTED as a single early return over the whole instantiation, so the first refused signature type dropped every body-container report at that call, including ones that had nothing to do with it. `fun two<A, B>(a: Map<str, A>, own b: B) { let items = [b]; }` at `A := Guard, B := Other` is the case: the caller was told about `Map<str, Guard>`, which it wrote and can fix, and the `List<Other>` the body builds out of a completely different parameter — a type no caller holds, which is the entire reason the body scan exists — vanished without a word. That is the half of B154's own sentence the code was not keeping, and it is the failure direction these rules must never take: a silent accept, not a noisy one. The question is now asked **per offending type** rather than once per instantiation. A refused-and-reported signature type contributes the generic parameters its unsubstituted spelling is written in terms of, and a body container stands down only when every parameter IT is written in terms of is among them — so `Map<K, V>`'s storage collapses into the `Map<K, V>` the caller wrote exactly as before (`NativeMap<(K, V)>` traces to `K` and `V`, both covered), while `List<B>` under a signature that only refused `Map<str, A>` traces to `B`, which is nobody's consequence, and reports. A type mentioning no parameter at all is never vacuously covered. The mixed shape both halves have to agree on is pinned in the middle: one parameter refused at the signature AND that same parameter's container built in the body — `fun wrap<A>(own a: A, table: Map<str, A>) { let items = [a]; }` — stays exactly one report, because the caller made one decision and fixing `A` fixes both. Found by audit run 5 rather than by a user, and fixed inside the same order that introduced it, so the suppression lived one union-tip day and before any release carried it. Three pins red first — the two-parameter case (both heads present, once each) against the early return, and the mixed case proven non-vacuous by re-planting the stand-down's removal, which also reddens B154's two `NativeMap` pins. (backlog E104, audit run 5's F2; docs `spec/memory.md` R10)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The import steer was never the value resolver's private property: a missing `Some` written in a MATCH ARM now carries the same one-line fix a missing `Some` in value position always did.** `cannot find 'Some' in this scope` arrives with `; import it first (\`import std::option::Some;\`)` for a bare `Some(1)`, and arrived bare — the name, no fix — when the `Option` was minted by a `List` method, because then `Some` appears ONLY in the pattern and nothing in the program takes the door the steer was wired to. The `List` method is not the class and neither is `Option`. Three sites in the analyzer raise a name-not-in-scope diagnostic, and they raise the SAME SENTENCE: the value-name resolver, which consults B4's steer; a pattern's variant path, which grew its own `resolve_variant_path` lookup; and a `use` statement's root, which walks its segments by hand. The two that own their lookup never joined the steer, so the identical message came with the fix through one door and without it through the other two — that is what a reader experiences as "the steer is lost", and fixing the `List` shape alone would have left the `use` root and every qualified pattern exactly as broken. Both now steer, through one helper rather than a second copy of the join, and the helper encodes the one rule a PATH makes necessary: a path resolves head-first, so the name an import could supply is always `path[0]` — the name itself for a bare `Some`, the `Option` for a qualified `Option::Some` — and a head that DOES resolve means a later segment is the miss, which no import fixes, so `Signal::Nope` against an in-scope `Signal` stays plain rather than being steered at a name already there. That negative is pinned beside the positives, because a steer pointing somewhere useless is the failure this whole feature is careful about. Four pins: the reported `List`-method shape, the `use` root, the value-position control that must go on steering after the fix as it did before it, and the resolvable-head refusal; the two positives red first against the unjoined tree, the two controls green across it. The LSP's add-import quickfix reads the steer out of the message, so it reaches pattern position with no work of its own. (tracker E103, found by Order 21's prelude census)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A `css` block you are still WRITING is still a `css` block: the editor offers property names inside one whose closing `}` you have not typed yet, where it used to offer `fun`, `for … in { }` and every name in scope.** The 23 pins css-block S5 shipped all describe blocks that close, and the block that matters most in an editor is the one that does not. `css_position` asks the RAW PARSE which body the cursor is in, and `parse_css_atom` declines a block that never closes — correctly, because a region with no end has no span — so there was no `Node::Css` to find, `css_position` returned `None`, and the dispatch fell through to ordinary expression ground. The code comment beside it said completion "simply does not fire there", which was the one thing that was not happening: it fired, with the wrong sub-language. **The parser is deliberately left alone.** Minting a node to end-of-input would have traded the located `unclosed `{`` the statement recovery reports at the opener — the diagnostic the author actually needs — and handed the css desugar a block nobody has finished, to answer a question the editor can answer for itself. What the fix does instead is let the LIVE LEXIS answer the first question, which is the same instrument, and the same reason, as the second: the position walk has always read the live buffer, because the character being typed is in no parse tree, and a block whose `}` has not been typed is exactly that kind of text. The parse stays the authority wherever it has an answer, so all 23 S5 pins are untouched by construction. The fallback is one pass with a stack of open brackets, each remembering whether it is a css BODY, and the two markers that decide are the grammar's own (§3): a block's own `{` is the one directly after the `css` keyword, and a nested rule's is one opened while the enclosing item has taken the `.` that commits it to a condition — so an unterminated `.md {` is a body too, and a `{…}` hole, a condition head's argument and an ordinary block are not, however css-adjacent. Comments and strings cannot plant a phantom `css {`: comments are trivia that never reach the token stream, and a string is one token. Five pins, and the negatives carry the weight — the unterminated block, its four positions (dotted, value, custom property) answering as they do in a closed one, the unterminated nested rule, an unclosed HOLE staying ordinary expression ground, and a CLOSED block not leaking past its own `}` with the enclosing function still open. The three positives are red against the tree without the fallback; the two negatives are red against a fallback that calls every unclosed brace a body, which reddens an existing S5 pin at the same time. (tracker E105, audit run 5's F10; docs `appendix/editor.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The grammar's `extern-args` production now states what the parser accepts: the two `new` forms, the bare `[extern(method)]`, and — the direction nobody was looking — `get`/`set` WITHOUT their property name, which it promised and the parser rejects.** N29 widened `extern-attr` for the `retains` flag and left the arms below it alone, which is where the gaps were. `new` was absent from the EBNF entirely while being live in three places: `[extern(new, "Uint8Array")]` in `bytes.vl`, `[extern(new, "node:sqlite", "DatabaseSync")]` in `db.vl` — the module form, which imports the class and then constructs it — and the constructor `vilan bindgen` GENERATES for every `.d.ts` class it meets, printed in the bindgen appendix's own worked example. The bare `[extern(method)]`, which takes the host name from the vilan function's own and which `bytes.vl` and `db.vl` both spell, was admitted only by accident of the arm it shared with `get` and `set`. And that shared arm is the defect running the other way: `( "method" | "get" | "set" ) [ "," STRING ]` says a property name is optional for all three, when `extern_binding_from_args` matches `[Word("get"), Text(symbol)]` and nothing shorter — a bare `[extern(get)]` is not an accessor at all, it falls to the malformed default and emits the empty global symbol, so `w.id()` compiles to `(w)`. A production that permits what the compiler silently mis-lowers is worse than one that is merely silent. Each arm is now its own line with the parser's own shape, `new` reading its strings exactly as the module/global arm does, and the trailing comma the attribute's list has always taken is written down beside `retains`. Every form was verified through the built compiler by reading the emitted JavaScript rather than by a clean exit — `new Widget()`, the `node:sqlite` import plus `new DatabaseSync()`, `w.poke()`, `w.id`, and the bare `get`'s `(w)` — because a malformed extern attribute compiles happily. The tour's binding-forms table gained the same three rows for the same reason; it is what a person reads while writing a binding, and it said four. **The wider finding, recorded rather than fixed: no gate holds this EBNF to the parser.** `grammar_sync.rs` gates the two SYNTAX-HIGHLIGHTING grammars against the compiler's word tables, and `markdown_anchors.golden` pins this page's headings for cross-link integrity, but nothing anywhere reads `spec/grammar.md`'s productions — the normative grammar is hand-written prose kept true by review alone, which is exactly how a production goes three forms stale across two releases without anything going red. Building that gate is its own piece of work and is deliberately not attempted here. (tracker N32, found by lane smalls' N29 sweep; docs `spec/grammar.md`, `tour/platforms.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The ignore-gate now asks for a reason that POINTS: it must lead with a tracker item id, one family letter and its digits, and `ARM64`, `UTF8`, `ES6` and `ISO8601` stop counting as ids. Its scanner also stops reading its own fixture as a real pin.** N31 shipped a gate whose predicate was "one or more capitals followed immediately by a digit", which is the shape of an ACRONYM at least as much as of an item id — and audit run 5 found both ends of that slack. The predicate now takes a single letter from the tracker's twelve family sections, one to three digits, and an end: `ES6` fails because `S` is not a digit, `I18n` fails because the id must stop at a non-alphanumeric, and `A2026` fails on the digit bound, which is why a year wearing a family letter is not an item. Coupling the gate to the roster of FAMILIES is a deliberate cost, and the smallest one available — the sections change when one is added, where the items change several times a cycle and could not be tracked from another repo at all — and its failure direction is a RED gate and a one-character diff, not a silent pass. **The lead is the other half, and it closes a different hole.** `borrows.rs` satisfied the old gate by naming `P4c` (a proposal slice label) and `C12` (closed) in its prose while the OPEN owner of the defect, `C13`, went unwritten — three plausible ids and none of them the answer, because a reason that MENTIONS an id has as many candidate owners as it has capitals. Leading with it means there is exactly one candidate and it is the first thing a reader sees. Nothing in the tree had to be re-pointed: all three bug reasons already led with their item, which is the discipline this writes down rather than introduces. **The scanner's second weakness was that it swept itself.** The gate's own fixture is a Rust string literal containing `#[ignore = "B1: …"]` at column 0, so the line-oriented scan read it as a real attribute — and it passed by luck, `read_string_literal` swallowing the fixture and `read_attribute` picking up whatever quoted text it ran into next as the reason. The general fix would be to track string state while scanning, and that is the wrong trade in this tree: 63 files use raw strings and dozens carry a `'"'` char literal (`main.rs` alone has seven), so a quote-toggling scanner desyncs, and the direction it fails in is a gate blind to every attribute after the desync. A file marks such a block with a fence comment instead, spelled with `concat!` so the marker text appears nowhere but where it fences something, and the fence is inventoried the way the non-bug allowlist is — one region, in one named file, so it cannot quietly become an escape hatch. Six pins across the two halves, each planted: the false friends and the mention-not-lead reasons go green again under the old predicate, and removing the fence reddens both fence pins, the second of which asserts the real whole-repo sweep finds NO attribute in the file that owns the scanner. (tracker N33, audit run 5's F5+F6; docs `AGENTS.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The HTML scanner stopped folding the whole document once per `<script>`: `skip_raw_text` compares in place, and on a page-shaped fixture that is 182,015 strings minted down to 5.** F14 renamed the full-Unicode fold and gave `_ascii` a real meaning, and it also turned `to_lowercase_ascii` from one native call into a per-code-unit vilan loop — a `code_at`, a bounds-checked `substring` minting a one-character string, and a concatenation, per unit. Ten call sites are short tokens and are fine. The eleventh, `skip_raw_text`, folded the ENTIRE markup and handed the copy to `index_of`, once per raw-text element, so a page with five `<script>`s folded itself five times; and `index_of` here is `document.vl`'s own vilan loop, which mints a needle-length substring per position on top of that. The CHANGELOG that shipped the rename priced none of it. **The cheaper shape is the windowed compare, not the folded-once copy, and the argument is `document.vl`'s own structure.** `tags_of` is the only caller, and caching a folded copy there would allocate a second whole document, thread mutable cache state through the scan loop, and still pay `index_of`'s per-position substring for every element. Comparing `A`–`Z` to `a`–`z` in place pays neither: it allocates nothing at all, and its work is bounded by the distance to the element's CLOSING TAG rather than by the length of the document, which is the actual asymptotic change — a small script at the top of a one-megabyte page went from folding a megabyte to reading a few dozen code units. The answer is the same one because the needle is already ASCII-folded by its caller and folding `A`–`Z` preserves both length and index, which is the F14 property the old comment was there to assert. **Measured, in counts and never in milliseconds** (the item's own instruction): an 18,190-unit page carrying five `<script>` elements, with the driver's own search left uninstrumented — 182,015 strings minted and 90,950 code units read before, 5 strings and 290 units after. The five are the one `"</" + name` per element. **Byte-identical behaviour is the gate, and it is proven as a differential rather than asserted**: the two implementations were run over the same eight shapes and their output diffed to nothing — an ordinary page, an UPPERCASE element with an uppercase close, a lowercase element with a `</ScRiPt>` close, a mount spelled inside a script body (which must stay text), non-ASCII including the length-changing `İ` that made the pre-F14 fold report an index into a string the caller did not hold, an unterminated element with the mount before it, an unterminated element SWALLOWING a mount after it, and a `<STYLE>`, because the skip is not pinned on `script` alone. Those eight are now a pin, and it is red under three plants: the case fold removed (`upper` fails), the give-up value changed from the document's end to where the skip started (`swallowed` fails, which is the shape that exists to discriminate it), and the search disabled outright. No corpus golden moves — no corpus program reaches `std::document`. Audit run 6 carries "std's emitted cost" as a standing angle beside the shipped sentence, per the run-5 charter. (tracker M14, audit run 5's F7)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The outage every app actually meets — a server that never comes back — now releases the client's graph like the two exotic refusals already did: the terminal `Closed` disposes, on all three arms.** A30 gave the law and covered two thirds of it. A re-dialled server whose contract had DRIFTED, and one that answered `__contract` and then refused the `__attach`, each reached the terminal `Closed` and disposed the `ReactiveClient` holding mirrors that could never be rebound. The third arm — the retry budget running out, ten attempts against a server that is simply gone — set the state and released nothing, and it is the *common* one: a redeployed surface and a refused session are things a reconnect meets occasionally, where a server that stays down is what an outage IS. So the path most likely to run was the path that reported the connection over while the client went on holding every route, every mirror cache, and the inbound handler that captures the whole client — precisely the retention A29 measured and gave `dispose` to cut. The reason was structural rather than an oversight, and it is worth stating because it decided the fix: **that arm has no client in scope, and cannot be given one by passing it**. The duplex is built before the client exists (`connect_socket` returns, and only then does the generated `connect` call `ReactiveClient::new`), and the socket's own failure handlers are armed at connect time — so threading a client down to `handle_drop` means carrying a value that does not exist yet through `watch_socket` and two event closures. The treatment is hoisted instead. `close_for_good` is now the ONE place `ConnectionState::Closed` is written, in the whole runtime, and it takes no client: state, then `close()`, then the teardowns the DUPLEX carries. The three arms say only "this connection is over" and cannot say it without paying for it — an arm that reports the state is calling the function that releases, and a fourth arm cannot be added that forgets, because there is no other way to spell the state. What the client costs to lose is settled where the client is born: the generated `connect` calls `dispose_on_close(socket, reactive)` one line after `ReactiveClient::new`, which pushes the disposal onto `on_terminal` — `on_reconnect`'s mirror for the connection that never comes back. The forgettable half is off the call sites entirely, which is the whole difference between three callers of one function and three arms each remembering a second step. `reattach_mirrors` loses its `client` parameter in the same move: it decides WHETHER a connection is over and no longer what being over costs. That parameter is a generated-hook seam — the expansion is the only thing that has ever spelled it, and it is updated in the same commit — and the function's two refusal arms are byte-for-byte the behaviour A30 shipped. Nothing a program can call changes, the ORDER A30 pinned is unmoved (the state signal fires first, so an app observing `Closed` sees the state it is being told about, not a half-torn-down client), and the negative is unmoved with it: a redial that merely takes a while disposes NOTHING, because it is `Reconnecting` and its mirrors resync when it succeeds. The new pin rides A26's failure-mode-as-a-spawn-argument harness with the one variant it did not have — no restarted server at all, the port left unbound for the whole ~24 s budget — and asserts the terminal state, that no attempt reconnected, and the disposal, off the same polled `disposed:` probe A30 built. Red first twice, and the second one is the shape's own experiment: re-planting the bare `state.set(Closed)` reddens the new leg alone, with `disposed:false` while `state:Closed` still arrives on time — the report was never the broken half — and removing the single `dispose_on_close` registration reddens all three terminal legs together while the redial negative stays green, which is what "one seam, three arms" means when it is measured instead of asserted. (backlog A31, ruled 2026-08-29; completes A30 and A26; proposal/lifetimes.md §5 V5, proposal/transport-robustness.md §2.3 and §2.5; docs `std/rpc.md`, `guide/services.md`)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**An unresolved TYPE name written in a module was reported against std's `lib.vl`, at an offset into a comment nobody wrote.** `fun make(): Widget` in `src/util.vl` printed its error with a file, a line and a caret — all three belonging to `~/.vilan/std-cache/…/std/src/lib.vl`, whose first line is a comment about what a package root is for. The VALUE-position twin, `widget_value` in the same file, has always been right. One mechanism, one difference: both diagnostics are raised out of a queue drained in `build()`, long after every per-file walk has finished, and the value site attributes its push to the file the name was walked from while the type site simply pushed. An unattributed push keeps whatever `current_source_id` the last walk left behind — std's root, since std is loaded last — and the span, which is an offset into the module, is then drawn over whatever the wrong file happens to hold at those bytes. That is the exact harm the attribution channel exists to prevent, and the fix is to use it: the type site now pushes through `push_in_source` with the `source_id` the walk already recorded when it prepped the annotation. The BARE-TRAIT refusal beside it (`fun size(shape: Shape)`, §12.2) is raised from the same drain and carried the same defect, so it is fixed in the same move rather than left to be found again — both are one-line changes to the same two pushes. Three pins in the attribution channel, which is the only place a diagnostic's file is observable from a compile-only harness: the type case (red first, reporting `lib.vl`), the bare-trait case (red first, likewise), and the value case as the control, green on both sides of the fix — which is what makes the pair a claim about one mechanism rather than two coincidences. (tracker E108, the prelude lane's find in Order 22)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Semantic highlighting and inlay hints drifted four characters after `Some(let (a, b))` — every token from the tuple onward painted the wrong text.** The owner's site was kolt `interact.vl:129`, `if handler.on_double_click is Some(let (delay, on_double_click))`: `delay`'s highlight sat on `y, ot` and its hint anchored inside the next name. Tokens and hints read ONE span — a capture's `Variable::name_span` — so both slid together, and the fix had to be at that source rather than at either consumer. The analyzer used to rebuild that span arithmetically, as the pattern's start plus `"let ".len()`, under a flag threaded down from the caller. The flag is right for a `match`/`is` capture, whose pattern span really does cover the keyword; it is wrong by exactly four for any binding reached through a BINDER tuple or array (`Some(let (a, b))`, `Some(let [a, b])`, and the `mut` spellings of both — `"mut "` is four characters too), because a binder's elements carry bare identifier spans. No flag could have told the two apart: they are the same `Pattern::Tuple` in the tree. So `Pattern::Binding` now carries the name's own span, recorded by the parser at the one place that unambiguously knows it, and the arithmetic is gone. Pinned per shape — tuple, array, `mut`, and a `match` leg, which reaches the walk by a different caller and drifted identically — against absolute offsets in the fixture text, alongside the three shapes that were already right (a bare payload, a pattern tuple whose elements each spell their own `let`, and a destructuring `let`), so the change reads as a narrowing rather than a swap of one drift for another.

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The one performance pin that runs beside every other test in the suite measured wall clock, so a busy machine could red it with nothing wrong — it measures thread CPU time now, and holds at 2.9x while the number it replaced wanders between 2.4x and 5.3x on the same runs.** `the_const_pass_scales_with_its_const_sites_and_not_with_their_square` asserts that four times the `const` sites cost at most six times the post-analysis passes, and it is a good pin: the const pass compiles every site to its own mini-program, so anything it does per site that is a fact about the whole program turns a linear pass into a quadratic one. It had already been repaired once for load — the two measurements were interleaved in 2026-08-19 after a contended run read 6.63x — and interleaving was not enough. In Order 22 it read **8.83x at loadavg 82** and 2.84 s alone on the same tree, taking a lane's verdict with it; that is the second such incident in two orders, and with roughly ten test suites sharing a machine it was going to be the fleet's problem, not one lane's. Interleaving equalizes *when* the two subjects are measured, but the large subject's window is four times longer, so under contention it cannot get as lucky as the small one and the min-of-rounds is biased upward for it alone — a bias no amount of alternation removes. **The repair is the measurement, again, and this time it is the ratio's actual intent:** the cost is the CPU time the measuring thread was given, via `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID` on unix and `GetThreadTimes` on Windows, so preemption by ten sibling suites simply never accrues to it. The two other options on the table were both refused with a reason. Widening the bound is vacuous — a genuinely quadratic plant reads 7.6-8.0x at this size, so a bound wide enough for loadavg 82 waves a real quadratic through. Moving the pin to the deliberate-run tier (`#[ignore]`, where the rest of this file's measurements live) was the fallback if no honest CPU clock existed; both platforms have one, and a scaling regression caught the day it lands beats one caught at the next quarterly run. The pin is single-threaded work on a thread of its own, so the thread clock is exactly the pass under measurement and nothing else. Both directions are measured rather than argued. Under 72 deliberate CPU hogs the gate stayed green across six runs from loadavg 29 to 107 with the CPU ratio at 2.82/2.96/2.84/2.88/2.90/2.89 and the *absolute* small-subject cost moving 189.6-196.8 ms across the whole range — while the wall-clock ratio for those same runs read 2.76/3.91/2.45/3.22/5.30/4.74, one of them already brushing the bound. Planting the documented quadratic — a whole-program mini-build per other const site — took it to 12.45x and red. Every run now prints both clocks and the loadavg (`PERF-SCALE const_pass clock=thread-cpu load=… ratio …x (wall …, ratio …x)`), so the load-immunity claim is checkable from an ordinary suite log instead of asserted in a comment, and M13's provenance rule reaches the one measurement that had escaped it. One honest limit, unchanged and worth restating: this pin guards the ASYMPTOTE in the entry's own size, not per-site work proportional to `std` — a lighter plant that rebuilds one whole world per site reads 3.11x and passes, exactly as the pin's own comment has said since M4. The two new dependency edges are test-only and add no package to the lockfile (`libc` and `windows-sys` are both already there), so the shipped binary's third-party surface is untouched. (backlog M15, flagged by lane a30 and paid for by lane fs-s5; `proposal/perf-baseline.md` §6.3)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`pub fun helper()` drew two diagnostics — the useless one and the curated one — and `public fun helper()` was refused in a word its author never typed.** E101 gave the visibility marker a curated rule and proved the misleading missing-`;` message gone; what it did not do is remove the STATEMENT the recovery kept beside it. `pub` lexes as an ordinary identifier, so the parser reads it as an expression statement, notices the `;` that never comes, replaces the message with the rule — and then keeps the statement, because `recover_missing_terminator`'s whole argument is that dropping a statement whose only fault is a missing `;` unbinds names the rest of the file uses. True of `import std::io::print`, and false of `pub`, which binds nothing and IS the mistake: the analyzer duly reported `cannot find 'pub' in this scope` beside a rule that had already refused the word. The recovery now has three outcomes where it had two — declined, kept, and DROPPED — because "reported it" and "kept it" were never the same decision, and the marker is the one statement that is reported and dropped. Both statement loops (file scope and a block body) route through it, and the cursor still advances past the marker, so the item below parses normally and neither loop can spin. The second half is the message: it was a fixed string naming `pub`, so `public` — the same reflex one synonym over, which the parser has always recognized — was refused in words the reader has to translate. The rule is now written from the marker that was WRITTEN, which is why it is a function where every other curated rule in the parser is a constant; only the two spellings the recognizer admits can reach it, so the payload stays `'static`. Five pins, four of them red first: the count itself at file scope and inside a block, the follow-on proven GONE rather than merely reordered (the same claim the `;` half already makes), `public` quoted back, and `pub` quoted back as the control. The negatives are untouched and still green — `let pub = 1;` is still an ordinary binding, and the `let mut` sibling, which never had the cascade, is unchanged. (tracker E109, audit run 6's F10 and F21)

**A builder chain offered no completions at all after its first link, on one line or ten.** Reported as "a `.` on its own line between two `.calls` offers nothing", which pointed at the line break; the line break turned out to be innocent. The cursor-context classifier has read member position in token space since kolt.local 001, and every trivia shape resolves — what went missing was the receiver's TYPE. Member completion resolved a call receiver by reading the callee's DECLARED return type, and vilan does not require one: kolt's builders are written `fun on_drag(own self, handler: || DragHandler) { self.on_drag = Some(handler); self }`, so `InteractionHandler::new().` was already silent and so was every link after it. Binding the same value to a name first (`let h = …; h.`) worked, which is what made the fault look positional. The analyzer had inferred the answer all along and memoizes it per function; it simply never left the analyzer, so the fix is one exported field — `Program::inferred_return_types`, the record keyed by function alone and written only for an exact answer under an empty substitution, so an entry is the function's own return type and never a caller's specialization — and one fallback where the declaration used to be the last word. Pinned in both spellings side by side (annotated and not) across the mid-chain dot on its own line, the trailing dot, the dot before a closing paren, the same-line control, an element head's argument, and a chain nested inside a closure argument.

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`[vilan leak] total` omitted the biggest thing the compiler holds on to — the base cache's resolved worlds — and it read 0 B for them because they are not a `Box::leak`.** The leak tally's literal contract is the `Box::leak`/`String::leak` sites, and it was never violated; that IS the finding. `BASE_CACHE` retains a whole resolved `World` per `BaseCacheKey` and `macros`' `FAILURES` retains rendered error text per failing definition set, both with per-key overwrite as their only eviction, and neither had a tally site — so the soak's strongest assertion (`total == counts().named()`, "no unnamed site grew") was blind to the largest per-process retention *by construction*, and the item could only say the magnitude was unmeasured. It is measured now: **three distinct import sets retain three worlds and record 449,301 B — about 150 KB per world**, where the tally previously reported nothing at all. Both sites `record` on insert and `release` on every eviction — a per-key overwrite, a stale-content removal, and each cache's `clear` — so `outstanding` at them is the LIVE retention rather than a running total, which is the one shape difference from the leak sites, where gross and net part only where a handle was reclaimed. The world figure is **source-proportional**: the module text a world was built from, summed, because the derived analyzer state it retains scales with that text and the texts themselves belong to the parse cache's own (shared) sites. Like the AST sites it is a proportional figure and not a heap audit, and it is a pure function of the world, so an eviction computes exactly what the store recorded without the map carrying the number alongside the value. `WORLDS`, the sibling that already had sites, deliberately gains no release: what a compiled macro world retains really is `Box::leak`ed, and dropping the `Arc` gives back none of it. **The item's worst case turns out not to happen, and that is now pinned rather than assumed.** It feared that "an LSP session mints a retained world for every distinct import set it ever sees, intermediate states included" — unbounded growth per keystroke. Typing `import std::io::print;` one character at a time is 22 analyses and retains **three** worlds: an intermediate prefix either does not parse, so nothing is stored, or seeds the same `std::` reference set as its neighbours, because the key is the reference set and not the text. Three import sets revisited five times each also retain three. So the answer to M11 is a tally site and not an eviction policy — what the cache retains is bounded, and a bound nobody can read is not a bound anybody can check. A new `base_cache_retained()` gives the world COUNT the hit/miss pair could never express. Three pins, each proven by planting the omission it closes: dropping the record reads 0 B for three worlds, dropping either `clear`'s release leaves the retention outstanding forever, and the growth pin is what will say so the day the key set starts tracking text. (backlog M11, N16 audit run 3; `proposal/leak-soak.md`)

**The add-import quickfix beside the web-set prelude steer is a pinned guarantee now, where a code comment had claimed the opposite for a release.** When a package on the base prelude reaches for `Signal`, the analyzer steers at the manifest — `` `Signal` is in the prelude of the web set — set `prelude = "std::web"` `` — and `web_prelude_steer`'s comment said the arm's distinct suffix kept the language server's `unresolved_name` parser from offering an import quickfix, "a quickfix that inserted an import here would be the wrong repair". Every clause of that was false. The parser keys on the `cannot find '` PREFIX and never looks at the suffix, so the quickfix was always offered; and it is the right repair, not a tolerated one — the module-carried entries (`style`, `ui`, where a bare-name miss genuinely is not fixed by an import) left the steer's set with the F2 fix earlier this cycle, so every name that still reaches it has a real declaring module and `import std::reactive::Signal;` compiles. Two correct repairs, offered together: the manifest line the message argues for, because a program reaching for `Signal` will want the rest of the set, and the one-name import the editor can apply without opening `vilan.toml`. Nothing about the behavior changes; what changes is that it is now true in the comment and held by a pin — an LSP test that reads the steered diagnostic, takes the quickfix, applies it and re-analyzes to prove the name resolves, proven non-vacuous by planting the suppression the old comment described and watching it go red. A claim about another crate's behavior, written in a comment and checked by nobody, drifted for a release; this one is checked. (tracker E110, audit run 6's F22)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The language server's session slowness is measured now, and the one leak the measuring found is fixed: the extension leaked a workspace file watcher on every restart.** The owner's decisive datapoint was that `Vilan: Restart Language Server` did not help while restarting VS Code did — which only means something once the restart command is known to really replace the server, so that was checked first: the command stops the old client and constructs a new `LanguageClient`, whose node layer spawns a fresh process and terminates the old one. A restart therefore does reset everything living inside the server process, and the suspicion belongs to state that OUTLIVES the client. One such piece was `workspace.createFileSystemWatcher('**/*.vl')`, written inline in the client options and so re-evaluated on every start. The client does not own a watcher handed to it that way — `FileSystemWatcherFeature` disposes the LISTENERS it hooked and leaves the watcher to its creator — and nothing disposed it, so each restart left another workspace-recursive watcher running for the life of the window. Restarting the server was what created it; only reloading the window cleared it. It is created once now and registered with the extension's subscriptions. Alongside the fix, both halves report: the extension times every round trip through one general middleware hook, names any request past 400 ms, logs the profile before each restart, stops swallowing the outcome of the stop it just performed, and answers `Vilan: Show Language Server Status` on demand; the server times every request at its own panic fence, names any past 250 ms, and every 500 requests emits the profile together with its retained-state cardinalities — documents, semantic-token cache, manifests, pending edits, line indices — since a count that climbs while the open-document count does not is a leak with a name. Deliberately not RSS: `leak_tally`'s module doc is the standing ruling that allocator retention from building and dropping a `Program` per analysis swamps it. A scripted open/edit/close session driven straight through the handlers pins that every one of those maps returns to baseline, so the server's map layer is cleared of the charge by measurement rather than by argument.

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A package's `generated` root declared through a symlink failed OPEN: `vilan fmt` and your editor's format-on-save rewrote the very products the exclusion exists to protect.** `generated = "src/icons"` over a link to a tree outside the package — a shared icon set, a sibling generator's output, an ordinary way a static tree gets its name — put the product's real path outside the package's real tree, and the predicate behind the exclusion climbed only that real path, so it never reached the manifest that had declared it. The exclusion silently stopped, the formatter rewrote the module, the hook that digests that file went stale, the generator rewrote it unformatted, and the two undid each other on every round: `build-hooks.md` §12.1's loop, back with the honesty note never printing because, as far as the tool could tell, nothing had been skipped. The generated output really did land unchecked — measured, in a fixture, both ways the file can be reached. The fix is one resolution rule for both halves of the question: a symlink gives one file two honest ancestries, so the search climbs the SPELLED path first (which is how `vilan fmt`'s walk and an explicit path both reach it) and the resolved one second (which covers an editor opening the product by its real path), while every containment test stays canonical on both sides — so the two names for one file cannot come to two verdicts. Pinned over paths in the predicate itself, in the terminal, and in the language server's formatting gate, each proven red-first by planting the old climb; the fmt↔hook loop is pinned as the fixed point it has to be. Unix fixtures (creating a symlink needs a privilege Windows does not grant by default); the fix itself is platform-neutral, and the Windows half of symlink behaviour is chartered separately. (backlog G17, audit run 6's F5)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan fmt` followed directory symlinks with no cycle guard and no scope: a self-link hung it with zero output, and an ordinary link out of the package let it rewrite files in someone else's tree.** Both measured. `src/l1 -> .` with a second link beside it fanned the walk out exponentially and `vilan fmt --check` never returned — and reported nothing at all, since the report comes after collection; one link alone terminated only because the kernel refused the fortieth symlink resolution, after handing the same file back forty-one times. Separately, `src/linked -> ../../outside` had `vilan fmt .` reformat a stranger's `.vl` files, which the `--watch` scan and the manifest fingerprint walked into just as happily. Following links is right — a link is project layout, not an escape — so the fix is the two guards a following walk needs and had neither of: a visited set keyed on directory IDENTITY (`(device, inode)` on unix, the resolved path elsewhere), so a directory reached again under another name is recognized as already walked; and a scope, the resolved project tree, so a link is followed when the tree it names is inside the project — or is a package's declared `generated` root, which is a package saying that tree is its own however the filesystem spells the path there. A link out of the project draws one dim `note:` naming it, because a formatter that quietly stops is the failure this design cannot afford and here it was doing too much. The cycle pin drives the binary under a talking timeout and fails if it does NOT terminate, rather than passing on the absence of evidence. Unix fixtures; the fix is platform-neutral. (backlog G18, audit run 6's F6)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan check <FILE>` read no manifest at all, so three of its answers were lies — including a clean check over a project that cannot be built.** An explicit file path built a unit with no package directory, and every answer that depends on the manifest went with it: a package already on `prelude = "std::web"` was told, by name, to "set `prelude = "std::web"` in vilan.toml" — the edit it had already made; `prelude = false` was silently ineffective, so a name the package had deliberately removed still resolved and the file checked clean where the build fails; and a manifest the validator refuses passed file-mode check wordlessly while directory mode failed the build on it. A file named by path is now a file OF its package: the nearest `vilan.toml` above it supplies its source root, dependency workspace, prelude, build options and platform (the language server's own rule — the package's target colors a file under its source root, and a multi-entry package colors an entry by its own), which is exactly what `vilan test` has done for a test file since distribution.md §7's S4, now shared as one discovery so the two cannot drift. What file mode deliberately does NOT adopt is the package's `[build]` hooks: naming one file asks for that file, not for the package's build pipeline, and a shell command is not a side effect to acquire by accident. A file with no manifest above it keeps its old context exactly — its own directory as the package root, the default prelude, no dependencies — and so does one under a `[project]` or `[library]` root that has no `[package]` to belong to. (backlog G20, audit run 6's F11)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Symlinks are a supported spelling of project layout, and the const channel's refusal no longer claims a resolution it does not perform.** `asset::read`, `asset::bundle`, `asset::read_dir` and `asset::digest` fence their arguments LEXICALLY — relative, no `..` — before any filesystem look, which is what keeps the refusal deterministic. The message said something else: "paths resolve inside the package root; `…` escapes it", asserting a resolution that never happens and implying that a name resolving elsewhere is an escape. It is not one. A link is an ordinary way a static tree gets its name inside a package, the freshness stamp has followed a declared one since G15, and the watcher polls what it points at — so the channel reads through it, bundles through it, lists through it, and tracks what it finds as the build input it is. The refusal now reads "paths stay inside the package root as written; `…` leaves it", and the doctrine is written where the fence is specified (`spec/const.md` §9.2), stated once for the whole toolchain: resolve honestly, apply every fence and scope to the RESOLVED tree, guard a following walk against cycles, and never word a message as though a link were illegitimate. The `dist`-side note is settled under the same ruling rather than fenced: a copy destination that is a link back at the source counts as already carried, because overwriting a link somebody put there on purpose is the outcome that predicate exists to prevent. (backlog G19, audit run 6's F7 and F24; the doctrine ruled by the owner)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The watcher read a `const`-listed directory the way it reads a build hook's, so `--watch` woke rounds its own gate could not possibly have moved for.** `asset::read_dir` keys a directory on its IMMEDIATE membership — the listing verbs read names, never bytes, and a file whose contents matter to the build is tracked in its own right by the `read`/`bundle`/`digest` that touched it — while a `[[build.hook]]`'s declared directory is digested as its whole tree, so an edit anywhere inside it really must wake a round. The wake-up set expanded both to the tree, so editing a listed file's content, or any file deeper in, started a round the leg then skipped by re-keying the directory and finding it unchanged. Harmless, which is why it went unnoticed, and still the watcher reading a declaration differently from the gate it wakes. The reading now travels on the row, the way G16 put the entry kind on the freshness stamp's rows: a const-recorded directory contributes its own entry (its mtime moves precisely when a direct member appears or vanishes, which is what the listing key is a function of), a hook-declared one contributes its tree as before, and a path recorded under both keeps the tree — the superset, and the safe direction here is the spurious round rather than the missed one. (backlog G21, audit run 6's F23)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A generic function whose emitted body does not mention `T` shipped one byte-identical JS copy per instantiation; it ships one copy now, and the corpus lost 2,747 bytes across eighteen programs without a single behavioural change.** The five `with_file*` forms share one `scoped_file<T>` whose nine emitted lines name no type at all, and `file.mjs` carried two copies of it — the shape audit run 6 filed this on.
