The memo one seam up keys on the type substitution, which is exactly right for "have I emitted this INSTANTIATION" and exactly wrong for "have I emitted this CODE", so every distinct `T` minted another copy of the same function. The question is now asked a second time, of the thing that actually matters: **the emitted body**. Two instance bodies that render identically once each is stripped of its own name ARE the same function, whatever the substitution did on the way there — which makes the test exact where a `T`-independence analysis would have to be conservative, and means nothing has to reason about which of the transformer's dozens of per-monomorphization decisions a body happened to consult. The name is stripped through the same tree walk the release rename uses rather than by substituting text: a string LITERAL containing another instance's name would make two different bodies look alike to a textual swap, and that is a miscompile rather than a missed optimization. **One condition makes the share sound rather than merely plausible**: a body may be shared only if walking it pushed no keyed emission of its own. If it pushed none, every name it mentions already existed, so an identical earlier body calls exactly the same functions; if it pushed one, that emission's freshly minted name would be in this body and not in the earlier one — and the one shape where it could still render alike, a mutually recursive emission naming this very instance, is refused by the same condition. The duplicate is always the LATER instance, whose nested requirements are all memo hits, so the condition costs nothing. Trait defaults specialized per concrete type take the same path, for the same reason. **The corpus is the census and it was verified mechanically, not read.** Eighteen goldens moved, 37 bodies merged, 248,540 → 245,793 B (−1.11 % overall; −24 % on `generic-methods.mjs`, −5 % on `file.mjs`), and every moved golden was PROVEN to be the old one with some set of duplicate bodies collapsed onto an earlier copy and their references repointed — a search over the possible merge sets reproduces each new file exactly, up to the generated-name renaming that a vanished function frees up. No file grew. Independently, all 124 corpus programs were run before and after and produced byte-identical output, the one apparent difference being the runner's own path inside a stack trace. **The corpus understates it, because corpus programs are small.** The split fixture — a routed browser app, the most application-shaped emission the suite pins — went 21,492 → 19,476 B, **−9.4 %**, its 105 top-level functions becoming 88 with its 78 distinct bodies untouched: one reactive helper had six copies, another five, another four. Two counts elsewhere follow from the same merges and are recorded, not worked around: the copy-elision census reads 259 → 257 (`expression-lift` 4 → 3, `iterator-adapters` 19 → 18 — a `__clone` site inside a body that used to be emitted twice was counted twice), which is the improving direction its own gate distinguishes from a dataflow regression. Three pins, red-first: the sharing pin goes to 3 copies with the share disabled, its control (two instantiations that resolve to genuinely different code) collapses to 1 when the body key and the safety condition are both removed, and a third runs the shared body at both of the types it was shared across. **One existing family had to change its instrument, and that is the interesting part of this entry.** B95 and B102's twelve pins ask whether the instance key discriminates two instantiations, and they asked it by counting how many copies of a distinctive body the output carried — a faithful proxy only while one key meant one emitted copy. Every one of those fixtures uses a maximally `T`-independent body *on purpose*, so that nothing but the key can split it, which is precisely the class this change collapses: left alone, four of them would have gone red and the other eight would have gone quietly vacuous. They now count INSTANCE KEYS, read from the memo through a new thread-local log — the same move the const pass's name-seed and call-graph pins make, and the same reason: where the output cannot distinguish two mechanisms, count the mechanism. The new instrument is itself plant-proven, a collapsing key reddening exactly the three splitting pins. (backlog M16, audit run 6's F18)

## v0.39.0 — 2026-08-29

<!-- family: breaking -->
**A resource is now destroyed after its LAST USE, not at the end of its scope.** `memory.md` §6.8's ratified timing sentence moves: a still-owned resource drops after the last statement of its declaring scope that reads it, and only the *simultaneous* discharges keep the reverse declaration order they always had. The reason is a scope that does not end — a server's `main` never returns, and under the old rule every handle it opened was released never, which is the shape of every vilan server. Three clauses make "its last use" total, and each is a deliberate ruling rather than a consequence: a binding NOTHING reads again is released at its declaration (binding a value is how the language says *keep this*; reading it is how it says *still*); a loan extends its owner to the VIEW's last use, so an owner is never torn down under a live projection; and a last use inside a branch or a loop resolves to the branch or the loop, so the drop lands at the join and the arm that read the resource and the arm that did not both release exactly once, with no runtime flag deciding which — Perceus's drop specialization, and R7's no-flags doctrine intact. Where the compiler cannot stand behind an answer (a binding read from two regions, a loan it cannot follow to its end) the resource keeps the scope-end teardown, which is the previous law: the fallback is never a guess. Ordinary programs see teardown earlier and nothing else — every drop still rides a `finally`, so `ret`, `jump`, and a panic release on the way out exactly as before, and no program gains or loses a destruction. **Migrating:** the one shape that can observe the change is a `Drop` body that reads *another* binding's liveness, which may now find it already destroyed; bind the later value earlier (`let` it above), or give the earlier one a use that spans the window. A resource you meant to hold for a whole scope needs something in that scope to read it — which is also the honest statement of what "holding" it means. (proposal/lifetimes.md §6, slice S3; the owner's ruling of 2026-08-28)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**A derived signal now dies with the boundary that built it — `map`, `combine` and `flatten` were leaking, permanently, with no way to stop them.** All three pushed a `Subscriber` onto their source and handed back only the derived signal: no id, no `Subscription`, nothing anyone could ever detach — `combine` computed its per-input subscriptions and discarded them, `flatten` discarded its outer one. The cost was measured on the idiom `std::router` itself documents. `current_path().map(parse)` inside a `mount_root` body, disposed and re-mounted 25 times, left **25 dead subscribers on the module-level path signal, 26 signals and 50 closures retained forever** — 256 objects per mount/dispose round that no amount of unmounting could reclaim — and it was a time leak as much as a space one: every later navigation notified every dead derivation the session had ever made, each recomputing a `parse` for a signal nothing would ever read again. The mechanism to fix it was already there and already correct; the derivations simply never reached it. They now register with the ambient owner the way `Source::effect` and `RemoteSource::map` do, so a derivation built inside a mounted view, a `bind_each` row or a `when` arm is released when that boundary is disposed, and `flatten` defers the rolling INNER subscription too — the one it disposes on every switch has no other holder, so whichever inner is current at disposal would otherwise keep driving a dead signal. **What changes for you:** a derivation you build inside an owner and then keep using *after* disposing that owner stops updating, where it used to keep updating forever; re-derive it, or build it outside the boundary. Nothing else moves — the signatures are unchanged (a returned `Subscription` would have been the breaking API change, and owner registration is not), and a derivation built where **no** owner is ambient is unchanged in every respect, which is deliberate: module-level `current_path().map(parse)` and `RemoteSource::status` above every boundary are documented idioms that must keep compiling, so the derivations read the ambient owner OPTIONALLY where `effect` demands one statically. Refusing the ownerless case is the stronger law and a second breaking change, so it is left for the owner to rule rather than taken here. Pinned by subscriber count across the 25 rounds (25 → 0) and per combinator, each proven red first. (backlog A28; proposal/lifetimes.md §5 V2; docs `guide/reactive.md` and `std/reactive.md`)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**A closure may no longer capture a view — the ban `memory.md` has always stated is now enforced, and the capture rule itself finally has a spec section.** Two things were wrong at once. Rule 3 said a view "may not be captured by a closure that outlives the place" and nothing checked it: a closure over `let v = &mut local` compiled, could be RETURNED or stored, and read through the escaped view afterwards — memory-safe today only because the JS host boxes the place and the collector traces it, and a use-after-free on any backend that does not (C12, reproduced on 0.38.0: `fun make(): || i32 { mut n = 7; let view = &mut n; || *view }` printed 7). The half that *was* enforced covered only a captured `&`/`&mut` PARAMETER, whose closure P4c already makes second-class; capturing an outer view BINDING was a recorded deferral in the impl plan, and it is the hole. It is now refused at the CAPTURE rather than at the escape, because deciding "this closure does not outlive the place" needs the liveness dataflow that is only now being built, and a view's surveyable interval is lexical by design — so the ban is total for view bindings and unchanged for view parameters, which view the caller's place and therefore only dangle by escaping. **What changes for you:** a closure naming a view binding from an enclosing scope stops compiling — a `let v = &x` / `&mut x` local, a `for e in &mut list` item, or a `borrows`-call result like `Shared::write()` — with a message naming both fixes, and both compile: read the value out first (`let n = *v;`) or take the view as a **closure parameter** (`|v: &mut i32| *v`), which is a per-call loan and never a capture. A closure that suspends keeps the sharper async text it already had. **The census that priced this is zero:** std, the corpus, every fenced docs example, all ten examples plus the benchmarks, the website, and kolt — 245 `.vl` files and 58 docs pages — contain not one view capture, statically or through a full rebuild of every package, so no program in the estate changes. It is filed family breaking regardless, because a program *could* have compiled before. Alongside it, the rule the language never wrote down: **a closure captures BINDINGS, not values** (new spec §6.9). The tour said the opposite — "by value at the moment they are created; Vilan copies, remember" — and it was false in both halves. A capture is the same binding: writes flow in and out, and a whole-binding REBINDING is visible through it (`mut label = "before"; let show = || label; label = "after"; show()` is `"after"`). The one copy a closure takes is the per-CALL return copy it already had; resources still cannot be captured (R9), and an ambient context value is still snapshotted at creation — the one true capture-time copy. Pinned by thirteen cases: the five aliasing shapes as running programs, the refusal proven red-first at each of the ways a view binding arises (a `&`/`&mut` local, a for-each item, a `borrows` call), and both boundaries — a view declared *inside* the closure, and the `&mut self` parameter capture that stays legal. One residue is recorded rather than closed, as §6.9's honesty limit and an `#[ignore]`d pin: the ESCAPE rule does not follow a closure through an ordinary call, so a closure over a `&mut` PARAMETER handed to a function that stores it still leaves its frame — a different hole from C12's, needing the closure-escape analysis rule 4's dynamic remainder is future work for. (lifetimes.md §4 + C12, ruled 2026-08-28; spec §6.9, tour "Functions and closures")

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**An overwrite that destroys a resource ran the destructor BEFORE computing the new value — a right-hand side that panicked left the scope's `finally` to destroy the same value a second time.** R2's `holder = f()` lowers to "drop what the place holds, then write", and the drop was emitted ahead of the new value's own expression, which made the gap between them a live window: `f()` throws, the destructor has already run, and the scope-end teardown walks the corpse on the way out — the probe printed its `drop` line twice. On the JS backend that is a double `close()` of a `File`; on a native backend it is a double free, which is why this was taken now rather than left to the last-use lowering that subsumes it structurally later. **The fix is evaluation order, not lifetimes:** the new value is computed into a temporary first, then the old value is destroyed, then the write lands, so a throwing right-hand side leaves the place holding its original value — still owned, still dropped exactly once at the scope end. R2's ratified sentence survives unamended, because "drops the old value first, then moves the new one in" orders the destructor against the *write* and the drop still sits between the two; what the spec now says out loud is the half it never did, that the right-hand side's own effects are observable before the outgoing value's `drop` body runs. The temporary appears only where it can matter: a right-hand side that emits as literals and local reads can neither throw nor observe a destructor, so its drop stays exactly where it was, and every existing R2 golden is byte-identical because the corpus writes constructor literals. Both halves of R2 are covered — the owned binding and B94's write through a `&mut` view — each pinned red-first on a throwing right-hand side, alongside a pin that the success path still destroys the old value before the new one is stored. (backlog B151, reproduced on 0.38.0 by the lifetimes session's error-path probes; proposal/lifetimes.md §6; docs `spec/memory.md` §6.8)

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**`drop(x)` took its scope's teardown away with it, so a panic between the acquisition and the explicit drop leaked the resource permanently.** The sink moves the binding, drop planning unenrolled it, and the scope's `try`/`finally` disappeared: `let file = File::open(p); …; drop(file)` emitted a bare destructor call with nothing around it, so any panic before that line left the handle open for the life of the process — where the very same program *without* the `drop(file)` would have closed it. The language's own sentence is that every exit runs drops, panic unwinding included, "because a resource-owning scope lowers to a `try`/`finally`", and the one spelling that asks for an early teardown was the one that had none. **The binding stays enrolled now, and the pair is made idempotent instead:** the explicit drop empties the slot — `file = null`, the moved-out state `Option.take` writes for exactly the same reason — and the scope's `finally` destroys only a slot that is still full. The panicking path therefore releases the resource, the fall-through path still destroys exactly once, and the early teardown is still early: a statement after `drop(file)` observes a released handle, as it always did. What the emitted test is, and is not, is the ratified distinction: mR7 bans runtime drop flags for CONDITIONAL moves, R7 rejects a conditional `drop(x)` outright, and what this guards is an unconditional early teardown against a path that never reached it — emission machinery, invisible to the source semantics. The delta is scoped to the bindings the sink actually names: a scope that never calls `drop` emits the same bare `finally` to the byte, and `drop(f(x))`, which names no binding and was never enrolled, is untouched. Three corpus goldens move deliberately — `file.vl`, `watch.vl` and `resource_take.vl`, the three programs that drop a binding early — each growing the teardown it was missing. (backlog B150, reproduced on 0.38.0 by the lifetimes session's error-path probes; proposal/lifetimes.md §6, whose last-use lowering subsumes this structurally later; docs `spec/memory.md` §6.8)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A resource built inside one expression and never bound is destroyed at its statement's end — the C11 leak, closed.** `File::open(path).read_at(buffer, 0)` was the fs tier's own documented idiom, and it neither dropped nor rejected: the drop planner enrolls BINDINGS, and a temporary has no binding to enroll, so every such handle leaked on every path until the process exited. Ten of them in a loop climbed the descriptor count by ten and it never came back down. A resource-typed value that is neither bound nor moved is now an *owning temporary*: the compiler gives it a name at its statement's position and destroys it in a `finally` around the rest of that statement, in reverse construction order among the temporaries of one statement. It is the same law S3 states, read at its narrowest — a temporary's last use *is* its statement — so the two arrived together rather than as separate mechanisms. The predicate is P5's, taken off the drop scan's own ownership walk rather than re-derived: a temporary is a value in a LOAN position (a `&`/`&mut` view, or a bare `self` receiver — C11's whole idiom is a postfix off a constructor call); anything moved into an `own` parameter, into `drop`, into `ret` or into an aggregate belongs to its destination and is left alone, and an unresolved callee's conventions are a signature nobody has read, so nothing is claimed there. The failure direction throughout is a leak, never a double destruction. One shape is REFUSED rather than lowered: a resource constructor on the right of `&&` or `||` runs only on some paths, so no statement can own it, and admitting it would mean v1's first runtime drop flag — the diagnostic names binding it as the fix, and it is the one place this work turns a compiling program into an error. A branch ARM needs no refusal: an arm is a scope with statements of its own. The fd staircase from the paper's measurements is an e2e pin, read with no settling poll on purpose — a poll lets node's own `FileHandle` finalizer close a leaked descriptor and report zero. (proposal/temporary-drop.md, backlog C11)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`[extern(…, retains)]` declares that the host KEEPS what it is handed.** Last-use disposal assumes an extern's loan is call-bounded: the host reads the argument until the call returns and not after, which is what lets the caller's binding be destroyed at its last use. A host that stashes — an event listener, a value held across a hot reload, a request body — breaks that assumption, and the probe battery reproduced the consequence exactly (`tag=["<FREED>"]`, read host-side after the caller had already torn the value down). The rule is now stated in both directions: an extern loan is call-bounded UNLESS the declaration says otherwise, and `retains` is what says otherwise — an argument to a retaining extern keeps its liveness for its binding's whole scope, the conservative envelope. Spelling settled against the existing grammar: a trailing FLAG rather than a form word, because `[extern(..)]`'s arguments are matched positionally (`method` / `get` / `set` / `new` plus the symbol shapes) and a form word would need an arm per combination — `[extern(method, "addEventListener", retains)]` composes with all of them. Trailing position only, which is the one place a flag can sit without displacing a form word and is what makes the formatter's round trip byte-exact. std's externs were audited against "does the host read this after the call returns, and is what it keeps a VILAN-owned value" — both halves, because the second is what makes the answer actionable. 23 declarations across eight files are marked: `addEventListener` on an element and on the window, `queueMicrotask`, both HMR channel helpers (`__hmr_stash` holds its value ACROSS the reload), the two chunk-loader helpers, the three WebSocket handler properties, every node `EventEmitter` registration in the http tier, and the four `fetch` request-init setters — a closure, a stashed value, a body, a header list, a signal, each of them something vilan allocated and can reason about the lifetime of. Left unmarked deliberately: every read-through accessor and pure computation; the `File` / `Database` / `Watcher` method surfaces, whose host reads the receiver within the call; the HMR synthesized getters, which `TransferForm` already excludes; and — the case that taught the second half of the question — `appendChild`. The DOM does keep the child, but the child is an `Element`, a host handle vilan neither allocates nor destroys, so declaring retention there buys no safety and costs real elisions: it made a *sibling* aggregate opaque and put back a deep copy the split fixture's golden had elided. Retention is about values whose lifetime this compiler manages; a host handle has none for it to extend. (proposal/lifetimes.md §6.4)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::asset::emit_keyed(kind, key, line)` — a build asset's order is now the contributor's to state, not the flush's to guess from bytes.** The accumulator has always ordered a kind's lines by their own bytes, which is right for CSS (that order IS the cascade) and arbitrary for everything else: a route list sorts by path, an icon manifest by name, a ranked list by rank, and none of those are functions of the line the way a lexical sort needs. The alternative — a flush-side comparator, or a manifest `order` key — would have to recover each line's sort key by parsing the line back at flush time, which is a second source of truth for a fact the contributing code already had in hand. So the contribution carries it: `emit_keyed` takes a key, the flush orders a kind by `(key, line)` and deduplicates by that same pair, and the key is never written — only the line reaches the file. Ordering is therefore still a function of the SET of contributions and never of the order const evaluation reached them, which is the one property the channel cannot trade. Two contributions of one line under two keys are two contributions; one contribution made twice is one line; a key shared by several lines still orders them by content, never by call order. **Nothing that already shipped moves a byte**, and that is by construction rather than by inspection: `emit(kind, line)` IS `emit_keyed(kind, line, line)`, spelled once at the single place either function records anything — one fence call, one push — so ordering and deduplicating by `(line, line)` is ordering and deduplicating by the line, which is exactly G5's shipped rule. Measured three ways: the corpus goldens (`.css` assets included) byte-identical, every example tree stylesheet byte-identical against a binary built from the branch point, and a differential pin plus an e2e that push one line set through both spellings and compare the written files. `css` is the one kind `emit_keyed` refuses, naming the reason — the sheet is ordered by the cascade, so a key handed to it would be silently dropped, and refusing now is also what keeps open the migration where `Style::rule` passes a band character as its key. Everything else about a kind is unchanged for the new spelling because it is the same code: E94's one-path-segment shape fence, G7's `build_owned_emit_kind` list (now one list, three consumers), and the const-only fence — a runtime path reaching `emit_keyed` is refused at the outermost crossing and named for the spelling the program actually wrote. **What changes for you:** nothing you have compiles differently; a kind whose order is not its bytes now has a spelling for it. (build-hooks.md §5.3/§8 S3, §10 Q3 ruled 2026-08-28)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`Event::target_value()` — read what the user typed from the event, not from the element.** An input listener that reaches for its own element to read `.value` closes an element ↔ listener cycle, and that one straddles the language/host boundary: half of it lives in the DOM, so no vilan-side disposal scheme can see both ends and no reclaimer without a tracing collector can free it. The accessor binds `event.target.value` beside the `Event` surface's existing `key`, `button` and the modifier flags, and `bind_value`/`bind_draft` are built on it. Hand-written handlers should be too — `view("input").on_event("input", |event| query.set(event.target_value()))` replaces capturing the element to interrogate it later. (proposal/lifetimes.md §5 V3; docs `std/browser.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**Copy elision runs on a real last-use liveness dataflow now — the corpus emits 7.4% fewer deep copies and the example apps 16.9%, with byte-for-byte identical behavior.** Rule 2 (elision) has always downgraded a copy to a move where the aliasing could never be observed, and its test for that was `reference_count == 1`: a whole-program, syntactic count of how many times a name resolved, guarded by a lexical set of every id sitting inside a loop or a closure. The census in `proposal/lifetimes.md` §3 priced what that costs — **25.4% of entities fail the count test, half of them with exactly two uses**, the read-then-move shape (`print(total(items)); Some(items)`) where the second read is plainly the last one and the copy is plainly pointless. The replacement is the dataflow §6 has been waiting on: a backward walk over the same scope-structured tree the move checker walks forward, one region at a time, answering **"is this read the binding's last use on every path out of here?"** — branches merging by union, a loop body seeded with everything it can still read on a later iteration, and a `jump` re-admitting that seed. It subsumes both of the old tests and beats each of them at its own job: a later read now refuses the elision *because it is a later read*, a closure capture refuses it because the capture reads the binding from another region (§4 — a closure captures BINDINGS, not values, so a captured binding is refused wholesale), and the loop question is asked **relative to the declaration**, which is the half the lexical set could not see — a list the loop body itself builds is fresh on every iteration, so handing it away at its last use is a genuine move and now compiles as one. Loans get §6.1's extension rule, built to the strength slice S3's drop placement will need rather than to elision's: reading a view marks its origin roots live, so an owner is copied out from under a still-live projection and moved past a dead one, and the two orders of the same three lines now emit differently on purpose. What the pass will not answer for, it refuses: a binding read from two regions, one whose declaration it never reached, one loaned somewhere the loan cannot be followed to its end (a `&` in a construction slot, an argument to a callee whose signature nobody read), and — the net that makes a traversal gap cost elisions instead of correctness — any binding whose reads the walk did not reach in full. **What changes for you:** nothing you can observe. Emitted programs behave identically, which is what the whole corpus proves twice over: every one of the 15 goldens that moved — 14 corpus programs and the route-split fixture's eager bundle — is a removed `__clone` wrapper and nothing else (checked by derivation, not by eye), and all 121 corpus programs plus the todo and SSR examples produce byte-identical output before and after. The new `crates/vilan-cli/tests/copy-elision-census.tsv` commits the per-program copy count so a regression in the dataflow reddens with a number rather than as an unlabelled byte diff. (proposal/lifetimes.md §6/§10 slice S2, §11's test plan)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The pages repo's workflow actions are SHA-pinned, closing L14's sweep on the fourth repo its inventory never named.** `vilan-lang.github.io/.github/workflows/docs.yml` carried two bare `actions/checkout@v4` and the repo had no `dependabot.yml`, while vilan (L14), proposals (N18) and the website (K19) were all pinned and tended. It is the worst place in the fleet to have left a mutable tag: `docs.yml` is the workflow holding `permissions: contents: write`, and it auto-pushes the rendered book readers visit — a tag re-pointed at hostile code would execute there, with that grant. Both checkouts now name the same commit the rest of the fleet pins, with the tag in the comment beside it, and `.github/dependabot.yml` groups the github-actions ecosystem into one weekly bump PR, because a pin nobody tends is a frozen action that stops receiving its own fixes: the pin and the file are one measure, not two. (backlog L18; the change lands in `vilan-lang/vilan-lang.github.io`)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The `css` block is in the spec, the tour and the editors now — and a block written without `import std::style::style` says so in its own words.** S2 shipped the guide section its own surface needed and left the rest for this slice. `spec/grammar.md` gains the `css-block` productions beside `element`'s, in the same `atom` block, with the prose that goes with them: the dot decides and decides alone, a property name is the element-name rule's span-adjacent run, the `;` is required after every declaration including the last, a value is tokens and holes with no typed grammar behind it, and the block desugars before analysis to a `std::style` chain — plus the brace-initial exclusion, recorded where the struct initializer's already is (§3.8), because a `css` block is suppressed in a condition, a `for … in` iterable and a `match` subject for exactly the same reason and takes exactly the same escape. `spec/lexical.md` gains two things it was missing: `css` in the reserved-word list, where a hard keyword since S2 had never been written down, and the first statement in that file that a character can fail to lex at all — `#` and `@` are in neither token class, so either one is a lex error wherever it is written, a `css` block included, *because* lexing finishes before any parser exists (§2.5 was already the sentence; this is its consequence). The two costs are stated with their spellings: a colour is a hole through `Color`, which carries its own `:root` line, and a media query is a breakpoint rule. `guide/styling.md` now shows both spellings of one style **in one compiled program** — the docs gate builds it, so the claim that they mint the same classes is checked rather than asserted — and documents what `vilan fmt` does with a block, barriers and the comment refusal included. `tour/coming-from-javascript.md` gains six phrasebook rows and one thing to unlearn (don't type `#` or `@`). The VS Code grammar gains a hand-written `#css` repository rule beside `#elements`: a begin/end over the block so a property name is coloured as a property only inside one, with condition rules taking their own braces and `{expression}` holes falling back to the whole vilan grammar. It is structure, not a generated word list, so it sits outside every `grammar_sync.rs` seam — and the generated `keyword.other` row that colours the word `css` is untouched. Finally, the diagnostic: a block lowers to `style()`, so a missing import fails on the generated accessor, and S2 kept that one accessor's span at the `css` KEYWORD for exactly this. The report was honest but disjointed — `css` underlined, `style` in the message, nothing drawing the line — so it now carries a note that does, on the element-syntax precedent (`element syntax lowers to std::ui::view`), gated on the span reading `css` so it cannot fire on an ordinary unresolved `style`. (proposal/css-block.md §11 S4)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`book_sync`'s mdBook backstop checks the renderer's version before trusting it.** The book's heading ids are a compatibility surface — LSP keyword hovers and every cross-page link resolve through them — and the compiler ships a reimplementation of mdBook v0.5.4's id algorithm whose PROOF is an `#[ignore]`d test that builds the real book and compares every heading of every page. That test shelled out to whatever `mdbook` happened to be on PATH, so its verdict was undefined in both directions: a false red against a correct parser, or a green blessing an anchor change the published site — built with the pin, fetched by sha256 — would never make. The pin was already enforced in the two other places that hold it (`scripts/regen-markdown-golden.py` refuses outright; the pages repo's workflow fetches the pinned tarball), and this was the third consumer and the one with no assertion at all. It now reads `mdbook --version` first and REFUSES on a mismatch or an absence, loudly, naming the pin, what it found instead, and the `cargo install` line that fixes it — refusing rather than skipping, per this harness's standing convention for a missing tool. The pin's constant is also held against the regeneration script's, so the golden's renderer and the proof's renderer cannot drift apart in silence. Four pins, driven by a planted shim printing a wrong version rather than by mutating `PATH` (which is process-global, and this harness has to be honest under `cargo test` as well as under nextest). (backlog N28)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A weekly, non-blocking CI leg runs the `#[ignore]`d pins and reports any that now PASS.** CLAUDE.md's rule is "a known-but-unfixed bug is pinned `#[ignore]`d and un-ignored when fixed", and nothing enforced the second half: none of CI's four legs carried `--run-ignored`, so a pin whose bug was fixed in passing kept its `#[ignore]` forever and only a manual audit dimension could notice. The new `ignored-pins` workflow runs `cargo nextest run --workspace --run-ignored only` on a Monday cron, excluding the one 247-second measurement pin whose ignore reason is COST and cannot expire, and parses the run into a job summary. **The signal is an unexpected GREEN, not a red:** the bug pins in that set are supposed to fail — reproducing a symptom nobody has fixed is what they are for — so a blocking leg here would be red by design; a pin that passes has either had its bug fixed (un-ignore it) or stopped reproducing the bug it names (rewrite it), and each one gets a warning annotation naming it. The job carries `continue-on-error: true` and lives in its own workflow rather than in `ci`, so it is structurally incapable of stranding a PR on a required check that never reports — the failure mode `ci.yml`'s own header was written to prevent. It deliberately does NOT go red on a passing pin either: one pin in the set is expected green every week, and a leg that failed every Monday would be read as broken inside a month. Four pins hold the leg's shape against exactly the drifts that would hollow it out — the deprecated `ignored-only` flag spelling, a name-based filter whose test no longer exists, the loss of `continue-on-error`, and the advisory job creeping into the required check's `needs`. (backlog N27)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The leak soak refuses to pass when it measured nothing.** `leak_soak_corpus_plateaus` gates both of its corpora behind environment variables nothing in this tree sets — they live in sibling checkouts — and the body `continue`d past each absent one with a skip line, so the default outcome of running the soak was a PASS in five milliseconds with zero assertions against its own "thousands of analyses" charter. Nothing was left unchecked by that (the synthetic fixtures beside it hold the same plateau invariant on every suite run), and that is exactly the defect: "I ran the soak" and "the soak ran" were the same green, and a soak that measured no corpus has no verdict to report either way. It now fails when it measured NO corpus, with a message naming every variable and the file each one points at — the failure is in the invocation, and the reader is whoever just typed it, so what they get is the export they forgot rather than a diagnosis. A PARTIAL run is still a run: one corpus present is thousands of analyses of a real file and the plateau claim is per corpus, so a soak with one of two variables set is unchanged. The test stays `#[ignore]`d (it is minutes to hours) and the in-suite fixtures are untouched; the refusal itself is pinned twice, once on the verdict and once on the soak body taking it, both in microseconds. (backlog M12)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`-d` dumps the raw parse now, and its help says what each stage is.** `.parse.out` was never the parser's output: `css::rewrite_items`, `elements::rewrite_items` and `lift::rewrite_items` are hooked at every parse entry and run before the dump is written, so the file readers reached for to answer "did the parser build this?" answered a different question — a probe's `.parse.out` came back full of `LiftRegion`/`LiftHole` nodes no source ever spells. The desugars had no dump of their own and no way to see the tree on either side of them, which is exactly the split needed to tell a parser bug from a desugar's. `-d` now writes `.parse-raw.out` beside it, carrying the tree the frontend produced; `.parse.out` is unchanged, still the tree analysis receives, so the pair BRACKETS the desugars and a node in one and not the other is something they added or removed. The raw dump is taken from a fresh parse of the entry's own text rather than from the pipeline's tree, so a `--watch` round served from the content-keyed clean-parse cache dumps one too — one extra parse, paid only under `-d`. The flag's help and `appendix/cli.md` name all four stages and what each holds, where they used to list three filenames and nothing else. (backlog E99)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`fun a(): i32 { ret 1 }` emitted `return return 1;` — a bundle that does not parse.** `ret` is an expression of the never type, so it may sit in a tail position; JS `return` is a statement. Every seam that consumed a walked tail wrapped or assigned it blindly, so a tail that had already LEFT was wrapped a second time — the function body's tail (`return return 1;`, and `return return;` for the bare void form), a value-position block bound by a `let` (`const y = return 1;`), and, through the restructured resource path, the `try`/`finally` tail under both the return and the assign dispositions. Filed as a miscompile and classified tooling on the §7.2 test: the artifact never runs, because `node` refuses the file — the failure is loud at the bundle's first parse, not a program quietly computing the wrong answer. The fix names the category the emitter always had and never said: a `Node` is **divergent** when the emitter renders it as a bare statement (`return` / `break` / `continue` / `throw`), and a divergent node is emitted AS-IS at every seam instead of being wrapped, assigned, or returned — which is what the if/match arm seam had been doing alone, in an inline `matches!` that is now the shared predicate. The leak itself is closed one level down: a block whose tail leaves reports NO value and emits the statement into the enclosing block, so `{ ret 1 }` composes to any depth and in any value position rather than handing a `return` to whatever expression slot the block sits in. `ret` in an if-tail and in a match arm were already correct — they went through the arm seam's divergence check — and are pinned so they stay that way. Every corpus golden is byte-identical: the broken shapes could not have been in one, since they do not parse. (backlog B152)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan fmt` prints a `css { … }` block, and orders it exactly as it orders the chain it desugars to.** S2 shipped a verbatim source-slice passthrough — enough to keep the bail set empty, so a file holding a block still formatted — and left the block's interior untouched. It now has a real printer: one item per line, a nested rule's own items one level further in, `{expression}` holes canonicalized as the ordinary vilan expressions they are (`{space( 4 )}` → `{space(4)}`), and blank lines between items dropped. A one-declaration block still collapses onto its line (`let active = const css { padding: {space(6)}; };`); a nested rule never does, because a rule sharing its line with its declarations is not CSS. And the items **sort**: properties in Tailwind's category sequence, then the conditions in the axis order the selector nests them — the same order a `style()` chain gets (proposal/css-block.md §8, Q2 ruled 2026-08-28), reached through the *same order function and the same tables*, so one style cannot have two canonical spellings. What the block deliberately does not get is a table of its own: the rank tables are keyed by METHOD name and a block writes CSS PROPERTY names, so the property rank is derived from the rows' own `properties` column, and `style_table_sync.rs` gates that no property is claimed by two families — the condition that makes the derivation well defined at all. Two differences from the chain, both earned: `raw` is a barrier in a chain because its slot is an argument the formatter cannot evaluate, while in a block the property is a token and really does rank; and a property no `Style` method writes — a vendor property, a custom property — is a barrier that nothing crosses, which is `raw`'s escape hatch surviving into the block whole. Reordering is **refused outright** for any block holding a comment, nested rules included: a permuted body would carry its comments to the wrong item, and the comment cursor only moves forward. The safety net gained the matching token-level pass, so the net and the printer cannot come to disagree about what canonical means, and the external corpus tripwire mirrors it through that one implementation rather than a copy. The corpus program reflowed under the new order, and what moved is the whole argument: **not one byte of emitted CSS changed** — class names are content hashes of the slot and the declaration, so a moved hash would have shown — and the only `.mjs` movement is two `class_list()` strings carrying the same classes in a different insertion order, which is what a reorder is allowed to change. Pinned per form through `assert_construct` (token identity, no silent bail, the canonical form, idempotence, round-trip), with the family and comment rules proven anti-vacuously — plant the plausible mistake and watch the pin redden. (proposal/css-block.md §8 / §11 S3)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The reactive graph is acyclic after disposal, and a heap-snapshot walk holds it that way on every suite run.** Three back edges the lifetime session measured by SCC analysis of live heaps are gone. `Signal`'s notify closure captured the whole signal — signal → subscribers cell → list → subscriber → closure → scope → signal — minting one 6-node cycle **per subscription in the program**; it captures the value cell instead, which is the only thing it ever read. `bind_value`/`bind_draft`'s write-back listeners are built in functions of their own, which is subtler than it looks and is the actual repair: a JS closure captures its enclosing SCOPE rather than the names it mentions, and one scope is allocated per invocation, so a listener written inline beside the effect that captures the element reaches that element however carefully the listener's own body avoids naming it — the fix is where the listener is BORN, and `Event::target_value` is what lets it be born somewhere else. And `ReactiveServer`/`ReactiveClient` now clear their transport's inbound handler when disposed (`DuplexEnd::clear_on_frame`, `on_frame`'s missing teardown half): the handler captured the whole session, so `dispose` emptying `sources`/`live` left the wire holding it — a session's server and its channels retained per closed connection, which `drop_session` inherits since it disposes what it drops. None of it is observable to a program: a disposed session dispatched nothing anyway, and a notify reading the cell reads the same value it read before. The standing gate is a Tarjan SCC walk over a `v8.writeHeapSnapshot` of a real mounted exemplar built by the real CLI and run under node — property, element, context and closure-scope edges, with the module-scope declaration records excluded structurally (a scope holding a closure whose own scope is itself) rather than by name. Its contract is the **post-disposal** graph, deliberately: a mounted app of any realism holds V4, the one semantic loop — a handler writes a signal a binding on the same element reads — which unmount dissolves rather than prevents, and the exemplar mounted holds it plus a live session's own transport loop and a `RemoteSource` lease. What must be zero is what survives the teardown. All three repairs were proven to redden it by planting the bug back. (backlog A29; proposal/lifetimes.md §5 V1/V3/V5, §11's S1 line; docs `std/rpc.md`)

## v0.38.0 — 2026-08-28

<!-- family: breaking -->
**An `asset::emit` kind may no longer name a file the build writes itself — `emit("vl", …)` in a bare build OVERWROTE THE ENTRY SOURCE and exited 0.** E94 fenced the kind's SHAPE (one path segment, no `..`, no separator) and stopped there, and shape was half the question: a single segment beside the build output can still be a segment the build writes there itself. A lone package's outputs sit exactly where its entry does, so `emit("vl", "CLOBBERED")` resolved to `app.vl`, the flush wrote it, and the build printed `Emitted  app.vl`, then `Compiled`, and exited 0 with the program's own source replaced by one line. The same hole reached `mjs`/`js` (the compiled bundle — under `vilan run` the canonical `app.mjs` was replaced by the emitted text), `chunks.json` (the build manifest) and the whole `<arm>.js` route-chunk family, and that last is worse than an overwrite: `sweep_stale_chunks` DELETES what lands there, so a kind parked on it does not merely collide, it disappears on the next build. The refusal now sits in the same const-time fence, taken on the EVALUATED kind rather than on the syntax — a computed kind folding to `"vl"` clobbered the source exactly as the literal did, and there is no other route in, since `asset::emit` is const-only — and it reads ONE list, `const_eval::build_owned_emit_kind`, which is the list the per-kind prune (G6) already read to decide what it may delete. One list, two consumers, so the write side and the prune side cannot come to disagree about what the build owns; the sharing is pinned by a plant that breaks the list and reddens both consumers' tests. `css` is the one member the fence admits, because the build owns it AND `emit` is how it is written — the whole styling system reaches `<leg>.css` that way, and `build-hooks.md` §5.6 reserves `css` against a build *hook*, never against the const channel that produces it. **What changes for you:** a program emitting `vl`, `js`, `mjs`, `chunks.json`, or any `<arm>.js` kind stops compiling, with a diagnostic naming which of the build's own files the kind collides with and telling you to pass a kind of the program's own; nothing in std, the corpus, the docs, or the examples emitted one of them. (backlog G7, ruled 2026-08-28; proposal/build-hooks.md §5.6)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`css` is a keyword now — and `Length::css(…)` is `Length::raw(…)`, the `.css` field of a `Length`/`Color` is `.text`.** The word was taken so the `css { … }` block has a grammar seat (proposal/css-block.md, Q3 ruled 2026-08-28). A contextual gate — `css` immediately followed by `{`, in atom position — would have served the headless block at zero cost, but the headed form `css [data-theme="dark"] { … }` collides with indexing a variable named `css` and `css :root` with a path, so it needs a real keyword; and taking one is three renames and a grammar regeneration in alpha, impossible after the v0.40.0 beta contract. The rename is mechanical and the compiler names it at every position the word can appear — a member access (`space(4).css`), a `::` path segment (`Length::css(..)`), a binding, a struct field declaration, a struct-initializer field — each reaching the same curated refusal through a different seam in the parser, because a bare "found `css`, expected an identifier" would leave the reader to guess what their `.css` became. `Length::raw` is the escape-hatch spelling `Style::raw` already uses for the same job (a complete value, verbatim, no `calc(..)` wrapper), and `.text` is what `CssValue::css_text` was already calling it. Nothing else moves: every emitted class name, every `:root` line and every corpus golden is byte-identical, because `Length` and `Color` are const-folded and their field name never reaches the emitted JavaScript. The three-place keyword rule is paid by generation as ever — one `KEYWORDS` row, one `Token` variant, one `KEYWORD_ROLES` row, then `VILAN_REGENERATE_GRAMMARS=1`. (proposal/css-block.md §5.4/§12 Q3; docs `guide/styling.md` and `std/style.md`)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`fs::exists` is deleted — probe with `stat(path).is_some()`.** Its stated justification named a category ("so boot code can branch on it directly") rather than a caller, and the standing rule for this surface (proposal/filesystem.md §4, minted after the sync-read claim that turned out to be false the day it was written) demands a name a later reader can look up. Looked up: all three callers in the tree — two in `std::build`, one in the todo example — were async functions already awaiting file reads a line or two later, so the one thing `exists` offered over `stat(path).is_some()`, its syncness, served nobody, and it blocked the event loop for a full stat to do it; `stat` also answers strictly more, with the same non-throwing posture on a missing path. Migration is mechanical: `if fs::exists(p)` → `if fs::stat(p).is_some()`, `if !fs::exists(p)` → `if fs::stat(p).is_none()` — and where the question was really "then act on it", the new `File` handle answers without the TOCTOU race `exists`-then-act always was. With it gone `std::fs` is async end to end; the rule stands for anything that would come back. (kolt.local 031 Q3, ruled 2026-08-27; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`Style::dark` is deleted; the theme condition is `within("data-theme", "dark", ..)`.** A census across the whole estate found zero user `.dark(` calls — the website, the examples and kolt all theme through tokens or their own systems — so a deprecation window would have protected nobody, and the ruling (kolt.local 014, 2026-08-27) is delete rather than deprecate. The replacement is strictly more general: `within` guards on ANY ancestor attribute, so the binary `dark` becomes one spelling among n-ary theme ids (`within("data-theme", "iron-dark", ..)`), composing with `attribute`, pseudo-classes and breakpoints exactly where `dark` composed (`md(within(.., hover(..)))`) and refusing the same misnestings by name. What moves in the emitted CSS: the ancestor selector drops its `:root` (`[data-theme="dark"] .sX`, specificity (0,2,0) where dark's was (0,3,0)); the recorded outcomes survive — a theme still beats a state on a shared property, composed rules still beat their parts — with one stated flip: a plain guard no longer beats `md(hover(..))` on a shared property, and `md(within(hover(..)))` is the spelling that says so (proposal/ui-styling.md §0bis.6's ledger). Every class name outside the old dark band is byte-identical.

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**An `asset::emit` reached only through trait dispatch escaped the const-only check — a clean compile, then a `ReferenceError` at run time.** The check propagates "compile-time-only" over call edges, and a bounded generic's `value.text()` has none: an emitting impl behind it was invisible, so while both concrete spellings of the same call were refused, the generic one landed in the emitted JavaScript as a live `__emit_asset(...)` with no runtime binding. The check now follows the analyzer's recorded dispatch — the same refinement the context coverage check uses, extracted into a shared `dispatch_refine` module: a generic call is charged at the entry whose substitution selects an emitting impl, so the refusal is per call site and a clean impl of the same trait member through the same generic stays legal; an inherited trait default re-dispatched onto a concrete receiver narrows by the receiver's head; module-level initializer entries are boundaries like any other; and anything the compiler cannot resolve — a shared default body's `self` call — refuses every receiver, conservatively, because the alternative is the silent crash. Inside a `const` nothing changes: the interpreter makes the call, which is the whole styling shape. Pinned in both directions, with plants confirming the escape pins and the clean-admission pins each redden alone. (backlog B143)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A build hook can say what it reads and writes, and then stop running.** `[build] run` executes every command on every build and every `--watch` round, which is right for a Tailwind pass and wrong for anything that downloads or generates at scale — the motivating case (a script that fetches an icon set and writes a package's worth of modules) paid its full cost per keystroke. A hook that should run only when something moved now gets a name and a declaration: `[[build.hook]]` with `name`, `run`, and optional `inputs` / `outputs`, running after every `run = [...]` command in declaration order. It is **fresh** — skipped, with a dim `Fresh <name>` line rather than silence — when it has a stamp, every declared output exists and re-digests to its recorded value, every declared input re-digests to its recorded value, and the command string is unchanged; otherwise it runs. **Content, never mtime**, on the watch loop's own recorded ground: rewriting a file with the same bytes is not a change, and a stamp that trusted timestamps would reintroduce the bug leg reuse already refused. Three details are decisions rather than defaults. A declared input that is **missing** is recorded as missing, so its later appearance invalidates — the same posture `asset::read`'s reader takes to its misses. A declared path is matched **literally**: a file, or a directory (which digests its whole tree, so `inputs = ["src/static"]` means what it looks like), and a glob such as `src/**/*.css` is a manifest error naming the fix, because an unmatched pattern would digest as a file that is never there and freeze the hook after its first run. And a hook that declares **neither** `inputs` nor `outputs` is never fresh and always runs — which is today's behavior, so `run = [...]` is unchanged to the byte and a `run`-only project grows no new files. The stamp lives at `dist/.build-hooks.json` in the project's own directory, not a machine-global cache: `rm -rf dist` means rebuild everything, hooks included, and `vilan build --rerun-hooks` is the one-off form. Every failure to read it — a truncation, a hand edit, a version this binary does not know — reads as *no stamp*, so the worst a broken stamp can do is cost the hook runs the toolchain would have paid anyway; a hook that fails records nothing, so the next build re-runs it. The staleness is over *declared* inputs and is knowingly unsound for a hook that reads what it did not declare (the failure is a stale artifact, never a wrong program — the compiler still hashes what it actually read), and skipping is a cost optimization over code already trusted to run, never a containment property. (proposal/build-hooks.md §3 / S1, the owner's Q2 ruling of 2026-08-28; docs `guide/dev-loop.md`, `spec/platform.md` §11.4, `appendix/cli.md`, `tour/projects.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`const preflight()` — an opinionated base stylesheet, opt-in and opt-out-able by construction.** std shipped no reset at all: UA defaults were fully in force, `body` kept its 8px margin, `box-sizing` was content-box everywhere so `width(px(200)).padding(space(4))` measured 232px, and no page said so — every app hand-shipped a `preflight.css` and served it itself. `preflight()` is Tailwind's reset-nearly-everything scope adapted: the universal border-box, the UA's margins zeroed, its form-control chrome stripped so a button inherits the page's font and colour instead of the platform's, replaced elements laid out as blocks that cannot overflow their container, list markers and heading sizes gone — **plus one rule no reset it is adapted from carries: `a`, `button` and `select` are `display: block`** (the ruling's own addition, and the most visible thing in it — an anchor in prose becomes a block, so a page that wants inline links styles them back). It is a std style asset the app EMITS, not a `Document` option or a build flag: the only door into the stylesheet is a `const` expression, so one line opts in, deleting it opts out, and a build that never calls it emits a byte-identical sheet — which also keeps it available to a browser-only project, the one that wants a reset most and has no server leg to configure. **Ordering was the design risk, and it is settled by the cascade rather than by the sheet's sort.** Every reset rule emits inside `@layer vilan.preflight`, a *sub-layer* of the layer declaration blocks use, so one sentence of css-cascade-5 §6.4.3 — unlayered rules sort after layered ones within the same parent layer — applies twice in the same direction: every `Style` beats every reset rule whatever the specificity (the guarantee `declare` already gave, unchanged), and every `declare` block beats every reset rule too, so an app tightening `a` writes `declare("a", …)` and needs neither `!important` nor a longer selector. The reset is the weakest author-origin thing in the sheet and still stronger than every UA default, because layers order *author* declarations only. Nothing about any of that reads a byte offset, which matters because the sheet's order is a lexical sort with no notion of a reset. Pinned four ways — opt-in emits, opt-out emits nothing and leaves the atomic sheet byte-identical, the three cascade tiers proven on one property, and a new `preflight.vl` corpus program holding every rule and its layer to the byte — each proven red first, and no existing golden moved. (kolt.local 008, ruled 2026-08-28; docs `std/style.md` and `guide/styling.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`serve_build` gained a caching hook — opt-in, one line, and nothing changes for a server that does not call it.** The artifacts a build serves re-downloaded on every page load, and the two-tier policy every static layer converges on was spellable everywhere except where it was wanted: `std::http` shipped `etag_of`/`etag_response` (v0.38.0) for a page you render yourself, but `serve_build`'s asset routes sent one `Content-Type` header and the bytes, so the motivating server hand-rolled its own static layer and recorded its ETag surrender as a comment. `ServerBuilder::cache_build(|url| CachePolicy)` is the opt-in: it asks your policy, per artifact, what to send beyond the bytes — `CachePolicy::validated()` for a validator and its `304`, `CachePolicy::none()` for neither, `.cache_control(value)` for the field value verbatim on either base — so the fingerprinted-plus-revalidated pair is one expression on the chain a server already writes. Composition rather than re-derivation: `validated()` *is* `etag_of` + `etag_response` over the artifact's own bytes, and the still-open `ResponseBuilder` those helpers were deliberately given is what carries `Cache-Control` onto the `304` arm as well as the `200`, exactly as RFC 9110 §15.4.5 asks. The policy is a function of the **url** and of nothing else — "which of my build's files is this?" is the whole question a two-tier policy asks, and keeping the request out is what stops a caching policy from quietly becoming a second request handler behind `serve_build`'s back; anything genuinely per-request stays `on_request`'s, built from `etag_response` directly. The validator is minted over the bytes actually being served, taken once, so it stays right under `run --watch` where each artifact is re-read per request — one sha-256 per hit on a validating route, which is the trade `etag_of` documents and a boot-time digest could not make. **The default is untouched, and by control flow rather than by arithmetic:** with no policy on the chain the asset route calls the same function it always did, so the un-opted response is byte-identical because of which branch runs, not because a neutral policy happens to add nothing — pinned as a header-set equality on a default server, proven red by planting the hook into the default arm. `fullstack-dx.md` §5.10's fence stands as written: `serve_build` still serves a build and not a directory, still honours no `Range`, and still has no caching default at all. (kolt.local 025b, ruled 2026-08-28; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A `css { … }` block is a `Style`, written as CSS.** `style().raw("display", "flex").hover(style().raw("background-color", Color::gray(100)))` now also spells as `css { display: flex; .hover { background-color: {Color::gray(100)}; } }` — the element syntax's twin on the style side, and sugar over the chain in exactly the sense markup is sugar over the `view` chain: a pure pre-analysis `Node → Node` desugar running in the same slot, at every parse entry (macro-expansion output included). **There is no third emission channel and not one byte of change to the atomic model** — `Style::rule` is still the one chokepoint, `class_hash`, `render_rule`, the family table, the `@media` sort band and the `@layer vilan` rule are untouched — and the arc's headline gate is what that buys: the same program written as a block and as the chain it desugars to emits **byte-identical CSS and byte-identical JS**, pinned both ways (the CSS half alone would miss a reordering, because the sheet is an over-approximation; the JS half catches which slots actually resolved). **One rule, and the feature falls out of it:** an undotted `property: value;` is a declaration and lowers to `.raw(property, value)`; a dotted `.name { … }` is a condition combinator and lowers to `.name(style() … )` with the block's own chain last. The dot is the whole disambiguator, so the desugar is NAME-BLIND — the grammar never consults `Style`'s method list, every combinator that exists works on the day it ships (`.within`, `.children`, `.divide` included), nesting order is combinator order, and a method added to `Style` can never change what existing `css` means. Values are token runs and `{expression}` holes: a value that is *exactly* one hole passes its expression through untouched, so `gap: {space(4)};` keeps a `Length` and the `:root` line the sheet needs beside it; anything else is the value's own source slice as a `str`, holes interpolated into the very tree an i-string builds. Written order is preserved; nothing is reordered, deduped or merged at lowering. Four refusals, each naming its fix: `#` and `@` are in no charset and cannot lex (lexing is context-free by spec, so a block cannot make them lex there either) and now carry curated rules pointing at `{Color::hex("#333")}` and `.md { … }`; `!important` is refused permanently, because merging a style is a record update and a later declaration already wins; and the `;` is required after every declaration, including the last, reported as the missing terminator the editor already has an "Insert `;`" quickfix for. A block is brace-initial, so unlike an element it IS suppressed in a condition, a `for … in` iterable and a `match` subject exactly as a struct literal is — the refusal says to parenthesize. `vilan fmt` prints a block back verbatim this slice (the canonical printer is the next one), which is what keeps the formatter's bail set asserted-empty rather than silently returning every file holding a block unformatted. And the spans are cut NOW, not repaired later: every generated scaffolding accessor is zero-width and the AST carries the property-name, value and per-declaration spans whether this slice uses them or not — the one mistake the plan was shaped to avoid, after element syntax paid for it in its LSP slice. (kolt.local 016 S2, proposal/css-block.md §4/§5/§7.3; corpus `css-block.vl`; docs `guide/styling.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::fs` grows its handle tier: `File`, positional reads and writes, `fsync`, and `with_file`.** An open file is now a first-class value — a `resource` on the `Database` template exactly: it moves rather than copies (a stale handle is a compile error, not an `EBADF`), a closure cannot capture it, `List` cannot hold it (`Option<File>` is the sanctioned container, so a pool of open files is not expressible yet), and its destructor closes the host handle at the owner's scope end, with `drop(file)` as the early form and **no public `close()` to fall out of sync with it**. Construction is five named constructors rather than node's flags string — `open` ("r"), `create` ("w"), `create_new` ("wx", the exclusive create a lockfile is built from), `append_to` ("a", where the host makes every write land at the end, position ignored — kept, not papered over), `modify` ("r+") — because `"wx"` is an untyped eleven-member enum whose failure mode is a runtime `EINVAL`. Reads and writes are positional and stateless by design (`read_at`/`write_at` at an explicit byte position; the host's hidden-cursor mode is deliberately unbound, so two loans of one handle cannot interleave through invisible state), `file.stat()` answers with no `Option` and no TOCTOU window (the handle addresses the open file — remove the path and it still reads), `truncate` shrinks or zero-extends, and `sync`/`data_sync` are `fsync`/`fdatasync`: the durability step `write_atomic`'s own comment always admitted it could not give. One semantic is deliberately novel and deliberately narrow (kolt.local 031 Q1, ruled 2026-08-27): the host's `FileHandle.close()` is async and a destructor cannot await, so a dropped `File` *initiates* its close without waiting — written data is safe, the process cannot exit under it, and only the close's own error goes unobserved — while `with_file(path, |file| …)`, the documented idiom, *awaits* the close, making it the one spelling where a failure to close is a failure of the call. The exception is scoped to `File` alone; "drop is synchronous" stays the law. The tier's intended idiom — `File::open(path).read_at(buffer, 0)`, a postfix straight off an implicitly-awaited call — is exactly the shape B141 miscompiled to `undefined` until v0.37.0, which is why the tier waited for that fix; both historically-broken spellings are now pinned positive, corpus and runtime. One `Database` idiom does not carry over: every `File` constructor is async and a module-level `let` cannot await, so a process-lifetime handle is a local in `main`, not a module global. (kolt.local 031 S3, proposal/filesystem.md §3.2/§5; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::fs` can watch: `Watcher`, `Change`, and a pull-shaped `next()`.** There was no watch API at all, and `std::watch` was a false friend — it is the dev-refresh channel (am I a `--watch` child; tell browsers to reload), not a file watcher — so std's own answer to "watch a file" was a comment telling you to poll `stat` yourself. Now `Watcher::watch(path)` observes a path and a directory's immediate entries, `Watcher::watch_all(path)` the whole tree beneath it (`read_dir`/`read_dir_all`'s reach, and `_all` means the same thing here), and `watcher.next()` hands back the next `Change` — a `path` already joined and ready for `read_file_to_str`, plus one of `Created`/`Modified`/`Removed`. **The mechanism is a 300 ms stat-diffing poll, not the host's `fs.watch`, and that is the design rather than an expedient:** node's binding coalesces events and also duplicates them, folds creation, deletion and renaming into one `"rename"` you must `stat` to disambiguate, varies by platform and version on recursive watching, can report a null filename on macOS, and throws outright on a path that does not exist yet — so a watcher built on it could promise only "something under here changed, go look", which is why the design sketch this replaces carried a deliberately impoverished two-variant kind. Comparing two stats promises strictly more on every platform for free, it is the choice the compiler made for its own `--watch` loop in 2026-07, and it lets a watch start on a path that is not there and report its arrival. The costs are stated in the surface rather than discovered: up to an interval of latency, no report at all for a change that cancels itself out inside one interval, blindness to a rewrite that alters neither size nor mtime, and one `stat` per watched entry per interval. A rename arrives as `Removed` plus `Created`, which is what a rename is to an observer comparing two listings; a directory reports `Created` and `Removed` but never `Modified`, because its mtime moves whenever its contents do and those entry changes are already reported one by one. **The shape is a pull, and the language decides that**: a `|Change| void` callback is a *void* channel, so a handler could not await the read of the file it was just told about, and R9 forbids it from capturing a `File` to hold one open across events — `next()` returns into a scope that can already own a handle, and reads exactly like `std::fetch`'s `BodyReader` chunk loop. `Watcher` is a `resource` built on `File`'s model (it moves, no closure captures it, no `List` holds it, no public `stop()`), with one difference that is the point: **its teardown is genuinely synchronous** — stopping a poll is a `clearTimeout` — so it inherits none of `File`'s asynchronous-close exception, which was ruled for `File` alone. That destructor is load-bearing rather than tidy, and the pins prove it: a pending host timer holds the event loop open, so with `impl Watcher with Drop` removed the end-to-end probe runs to its last line and then hangs forever. Nine runtime pins against real file activity, thirteen on the semantics and the emission, and a `watch.vl` corpus golden holding the poll helper's bytes. (kolt.local 020, which the owner ruled owns the whole watch surface on 2026-08-28; proposal/filesystem.md §8 is now a cross-reference to it; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::http` answers conditional requests: `etag_of`, `etag_response`, `if_none_match_matches`.** The two-tier cache policy every static layer converges on was only half-spellable: `Request::header` (v0.37.0) could read `If-None-Match` and the response side could say `304`, but the validator itself and the RFC bookkeeping in between were every app's own project — the motivating exhibit is a real server that shipped its surrender as a comment. Now `etag_of(bytes)` mints a strong validator (the first 32 hex digits of the body's sha-256, quotes included; colliding with a body you did not choose is still a second preimage, at half the header weight of the full digest), and `etag_response(request, tag, bytes, content_type)` answers the conditional GET whole: `304` with the tag echoed and no body when `If-None-Match` already names it, the full `200` with `ETag` and `Content-Type` otherwise — returned as a still-open `ResponseBuilder`, so `Cache-Control` and friends chain after it and land on whichever arm answered, which is exactly what RFC 9110 asks of a 304. Matching is the RFC's: the single, list and `*` forms, weak comparison on both sides (a proxy-weakened `W/` tag still revalidates), and only `GET`/`HEAD` revalidate — on any other method a matching `If-None-Match` means `412`, a state-changing route's protocol, so there the header is left unconsulted and the bytes go out. `if_none_match_matches(header, etag)` is the comparison half on its own, for a handler building both arms itself. User-land composable only, deliberately: `serve_build`'s default caching policy stays behind the fullstack-dx §5.10 fence (kolt.local 025b, the owner's call), and nothing here touches `serve_build`'s headers. Verified against a real node server end to end — match, mismatch, absent, list, `*`, weak, and the POST gate — each pin proven red first. (kolt.local 025c)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**Styles gained the relation axis — `within(attr, value, inner)`, `children(inner)`, `divide(inner)`.** The axis answers "whose element does this slot dress?" (kolt.local 009+014, proposal/ui-styling.md §0bis.6): `within` guards the element's own slots on an ancestor's attribute (the theme condition above, and any other ancestor state); `children` styles every direct child (`.sX > *`); `divide` every child but the first — rendered `.sX > :not(:first-child)`, the same element set as Tailwind's owl but carrying (0,2,0), so where both relations touch one property `divide` outranks `children` by specificity, never by the sheet's class-hash order. Child-side rules emit inside `@layer vilan`, and that is the whole cascade story: **a child's own `Style` always wins against a rule reaching in from an ancestor**, whatever the specificity — the relations set defaults a child may refuse, and symmetrically cannot override any unlayered CSS. One relation per slot, one contribution per (relation, property) slot, the same last-wins merge, and every pre-existing class name is byte-identical because the relation rides the condition grammar rather than widening the slot key. kolt-shaped theming — per-theme `declare` blocks with var()-backed colors plus a `within` guard — is pinned as the `theme.vl` corpus program, and `vilan fmt` sorts the new combinators on their axis (media, then the relation, then `attribute`, then the pseudo-classes).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A dependency that declares build hooks is now named out loud instead of passed over in silence — and `build-hooks = true` is the line that would grant it.** Only the addressed manifest ever contributed hooks, which is what keeps first-party build code a *first-party* trust tier; what it never did was **say so**. A path dependency declaring `run = ["echo hi"]` produced no output, no warning, and no note, and from the terminal "the policy refused it" and "the toolchain never looked" are the same thing: one reader debugs the dependency, their shell and their PATH before suspecting a rule nobody told them about, and the other never learns that a package in their graph is asking to execute code on their machine. Every dependency in the resolved graph whose own manifest declares a hook now gets one dim `note:` line, once per build, naming it — recorded during resolution, the one pass that already reads every dependency manifest, and deduplicated by package so two workspace members depending on one library say it once. A **note, never a warning**, in either case, because the refusal is a normal outcome and not an error to be dismissed; the exit code is unchanged. Alongside it, tier 2's opt-in gets its spelling: `build-hooks = true` on a dependency's own declaration, beside where the dependency comes from — per dependency (there is no project-wide switch to reconstruct the default it replaces), recorded in the manifest (a word in the diff that a review sees and a revert undoes), and absent means no, with `false` accepted as the same answer written down deliberately. It sits on the declaration rather than in a grant table of its own for two reasons that are properties: you cannot grant what you do not declare, and removing the dependency removes the grant. **Nothing honors it yet, deliberately** — every dependency hook is refused, opted in or not, and the opted-in case gets its own line saying so. The threshold was moved here rather than left to a registry because third-party code already reaches the machine on an ordinary `vilan build` (a git dependency is fetched and compiled), so the only thing holding the line is one function reading one manifest; the point of shipping the key now is that the syntax is fixed and reviewable *before* anything can cross it, and a registry inherits a spelling instead of minting one under pressure. (proposal/build-hooks.md §4.3 / S2 and proposal/build-trust.md §3–§4, the owner's Q4 and Q6 rulings of 2026-08-28)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan run` now sweeps the stale style sidecar, as `vilan build` always did.** Delete the last style from a program and build it, and `<entry>.css` goes away; do the same and `vilan run` it, and the file stayed on disk with the old bytes — round after round under `--watch`, which is the resurrection shape (a served page keeps injecting a stylesheet the source no longer produces) that `sweep_stale_sidecar` exists to prevent. The cause was placement rather than logic: the sweep hung off `write_chunks`, which `build` calls and which `run` and the single-file watch round do not. It now lives in `write_assets` — the flush itself, the one place that knows what this round emitted, and where G6's per-kind prune already sat — so every path that flushes assets prunes through both mechanisms together and `run` answers exactly as `build` does; `write_chunks` keeps its own call for the HMR dev loop, which writes its sidecar straight into `dist/` rather than through the flush. The sweep still names exactly one file, `<entry>.css`, so an unrelated user file beside the entry is not in its reach — pinned, along with the `run` round and a bounded single-file watch round. (backlog G8)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A reconnect whose re-attach is refused now closes the connection, instead of leaving every mirror bound to a dead channel while the socket says it is fine.** When a dropped socket comes back, the generated client re-verifies the contract and calls `__attach` to mint fresh channel ids for its mirrors. The contract half was handled loudly — a server that redeployed a different surface takes the connection to the terminal `Closed` rather than feeding typed mirrors a shape they were not built for — but the `__attach` half's failure arm was empty: `Err(let _failed) => {}`. The rebind loop was skipped in silence, so `connection_state()` reported `Connected` while every `RemoteSource` still pointed at the *previous* connection's channel ids. Nothing on that socket could ever update again, and nothing said so — no signal, no error, no log — so an app's `or`/state machinery had nothing to act on: the one state it can see said the connection was healthy. A refused `__attach` now takes exactly the drift branch's treatment, `Closed` plus `close()`, on the same reasoning: a mirror that cannot be rebound is a desync, not a delay. An UNREACHABLE server during the re-attach is still left alone, deliberately and as before — that socket is already dying and the next drop cycle redials it. What changes for an app that meets this: rpc calls that used to keep working over the live-but-wedged socket now fail fast with `Transport("not connected")`, which is the honest report, since the mirrors beside them were already dead.
