<!-- family: tooling -->
**A `Workspace` staging a dependency edge named `std` or `pkg` is refused at the analyzer's own door.** The manifest layer has refused the names since v0.36.0 (L12), but a `Workspace` built programmatically — wasm, embedders, tests — could still stage one, and the shape had two answers for one name: the analyzer's `resolve_import_root` checks dependency edges first, so a dependency named `std` silently replaced the entire standard library, while the IDE's completion (and the macro world's scope resolution) answered the stdlib. `analyze` — the funnel every entry path passes through — now reports such an edge with the manifest's own reserved-name refusal and drops it rather than failing the program: `std::` and `pkg::` keep meaning the roots they always name, and the resolvers agree by construction because the shape no longer exists past the door. `macro_std` stays a legal edge name at this layer — the macro world itself stages the macro standard library as one; user manifests remain refused up front by L12 (E88, filed from L12's report Q3).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan build` warns when a superseded generation of an artifact survives beside the new one.** A project last built before v0.33.0's artifact rename (process legs `.js` → `.mjs`) rebuilds clean on the current toolchain — and the old `dist/server.js` keeps sitting beside the new `dist/server.mjs`, so a script, Dockerfile, or process manager still saying `node dist/server.js` keeps launching the superseded application silently, exactly the drift the gotchas page warns about with nothing in the toolchain to catch it. The build now checks, after writing each bundle, for a same-stem sibling under the other script classification's extension — within one build the `dist/<name>.*` namespace belongs to one leg, so a surviving `.js` beside a `.mjs` (or a stranded `.mjs` after a leg retargeted to the browser) can only be an earlier generation — and warns, naming both files and the fix. Warned, never swept: no record proves the build wrote the old file (pre-rename builds recorded nothing, which is also why a record-scoped sweep would ship blind to every dist/ already carrying the residue), and the output directory is not exclusively the build's, so user files are never touched. The lone-package form (`src/main.js` beside `src/main.mjs`) gets the same check at its beside-the-source write (E92; a whole-build emission record for the renamed-leg and dropped-leg shapes stays open in the tracker).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The docs gate's heading-id twin can no longer drift from `std::markdown`'s rule.** The test-only reimplementation the keyword-hover deep links are checked against (`book_sync`'s `normalize_id`) lowercased ASCII-only and skipped the trim mdBook performs after dropping tags — both empirically wrong against mdBook v0.5.4 (`École Été` → `École-Été` where the renderer says `école-été`; an anchor-fronted heading kept the anchor's trailing space as a leading `-`), and both latent because no book heading exercises them today, which is exactly how a checking surface rots. The twin now trims after the tag drop and lowercases with the full Unicode fold, both shapes are pinned, and a differential gate holds the twin to the same `markdown_anchors.golden` (456 ids scraped from a real mdBook v0.5.4 build) that `std::markdown`'s `heading_id` already answers to — with both implementations reproducing one golden, neither can drift from the other over the book's ids without its own gate going red (B137, `proposal/markdown.md` §10.1).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan fmt` collapses a one-member brace import to its unbraced spelling.** `import a::{ b }` and `import a::b` spell the same import, and Format Document left the braced one standing — the shape hand-written code falls into whenever a set shrinks to one survivor. The unbraced form is now canonical, recursively (`a::{ b::{ c } }` reaches `a::b::c`) and for `use` too — the canonical form the tracker item asked to have defined. Two exceptions keep their braces: a lone `self` (`Option::{ self }` publishes `Option`; there is no unbraced spelling) and a set holding a comment (anchored to the split form). Organize Imports rides the same printer, so pruning a set down to one member renders the unbraced spelling as well, and the add-import quickfix never builds a one-member set — no editor path regenerates the braces. The shared sort key sees through the braces too (`unwrap_singleton_set`): the braced spelling ranks exactly where its collapsed form lands, so a reprint can never order a run against the safety net's canonicalization (the silent-bail interaction the corpus caught). The three corpus programs carrying the shape reflowed (`loops.vl`, `async-await.vl`, `async-promise-all.vl`); every emitted-JS golden is byte-identical — imports are spelling, not codegen. kolt (`proposals/projects/kolt.local` tracker item 005).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The docs state the build-hook trust model.** `[build] run` hooks have always executed through the platform shell with the developer's own privileges — deliberately, the cargo-vs-make decision, reasoned out in the code — but no user-facing page said so. The strongest sentence in the whole corpus described capability ("pipes, globs and `&&` work") and never authority, while the spec's `[build]` bullet introduced `run` one sentence after "Build options never change program semantics, only the emitted text" (true of `preset`/`indent`/`spaces`, false of `run`, with nothing separating them), and the CLI appendix's one-rule-up-front scoped the reader's threat model to the network on the very page that documents `vilan build`. The book (`guide/dev-loop.md`), the spec (§11.4), the tour, and the CLI appendix now say it plainly: a hook runs **with your privileges** — your files, your environment, your keys — silently and unsandboxed, exactly as if you had typed the command, which is the trust `cargo build` and `npm run` already take; and only the manifest being built declares hooks, so a dependency's are never run. The `[build] run` hover says it too, and the CLI's already-unconditional `Running <command>` echo is documented for the first time. No behavior changes, and **no prompt is added**: the first-run consent gate was proposed and declined, and the hook's own code comment now records the decline so it is not rediscovered as an oversight (backlog E96, ruled 2026-08-26; `proposal/build-trust.md` carries both tiers, including that dependency-authored build code — the day dependencies can carry hooks at all — does not run by default and needs an explicit per-dependency opt-in recorded in the manifest).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Two closed items were under-pinned and six open ones had no pin at all — a sweep to make the test file say what the tracker says.** The standing rule is that "fixed" and "closed" require a pinned test *per case, not per example*, and that a known-but-unfixed bug is pinned as an `#[ignore]`d test that is un-ignored when it is fixed. An audit found gaps in both directions, and this closes them. On the closed side, **B139** was recorded as two halves fixed with only the DEPTH half pinned. Its TIME half — the memoized return chain — was evidenced by a prose measurement (390,984 inference entries down to 14,730) plus a green suite, which is precisely the evidence the rule refuses. It now has a COUNT pin in the shape B140 established one item later: `inference_entry_count`, a monotonic thread-local probe beside `atom_parse_count` and `buffer_parse_count`, asserted against a ceiling LINEAR in the chain's length. A wall clock was the wrong instrument for the same reason it was there: a ceiling generous enough to be stable across machines separates linear from exponential but *not* from quadratic, and quadratic is what this bug actually was, not some distant worse case. The constant term is measured in-run rather than written down, because it is std's own inference cost and std grows; only the marginal cost per link is a claim about this code. Disabling the memo read takes a 400-link chain from 4,202 entries to 245,200 and reddens every depth in the sweep. B139's second claim, that the fix is linear in *both* source orders, had been plant-tested caller-first only; a callee-first plant joins it — in its own test, not sharing one — and the flattening claim (502 frames collapsing to 6) is now pinned behaviourally, since a callee-first 1200-link chain must not reach the 500-link bound at all. **B140** had both its plants inside a single test function, so an arithmetic failure short-circuited the list shape and it was never reported; they are two tests now, which is what "per case" means.

One claim could not be pinned as asked, and the negative result is worth more than a pin that pretends otherwise. B139's `substitution_context.is_empty()` guard on the recorded-answer read is the correctness guard the tombstone singles out, on the reasoning that an answer shaped by a caller's generic bindings is that caller's and not the function's. The hazard is real and measurable: 1,157 records are written under a non-empty substitution context over the inference suite, and one generic enum's slot receives six different instantiations in a single run, so the map genuinely does collect caller-shaped answers. But **deleting the guard changes no observable behaviour today** — all 2,596 inference tests, the docs gate, and the byte-identical corpus are unaffected, because a wrongly-served answer is re-substituted downstream before it can reach codegen. Twelve candidate programs were built to find a wrong-typed one and none discriminates. So the pin asserts the guard *itself*, through a probe that counts what the guard forbids and must read zero; deleting the guard turns it red immediately (4 serves against an expected 0). It is a weaker claim than "a wrong type is served", and it is the true one.

On the open side, six defects that the tracker calls open had nothing in the suite naming them, so nothing would notice when they were fixed. Each is now an `#[ignore]`d test whose reason string names the item, and each was run un-ignored to confirm it fails for the stated reason rather than a harness mistake: **B142** (the parser has no depth bound — the existing 5000-deep pin uses a method chain, which is flat to the parser, so nesting the source syntactically instead aborts with a stack overflow and no diagnostic at all); **E94** (`asset::emit`'s kind reaches `with_extension` unsanitized, so `..` or a separator directs a write out of `dist/`, pinned under one rule with a green negative so the fence cannot be over-broad); **kolt.local 001** (the context-blind completion classifier, whose faults cut both ways — a `.` starting the next line offers nothing, a cursor in a string body offers the whole scope including functions); **kolt.local 007** (CSS hot-reload's added- and removed-stylesheet transitions); and **L15** (`install.sh` prints "skipping checksum verification" and installs unverified bytes when no sha256 tool is on PATH, where it must refuse). Two findings came out of the pinning rather than the audit. kolt.local 001's second listed face, `a.|.b`, does **not** reproduce — a call receiver, a trailing dot, a field or method after the cursor, and a partial name before it all resolve correctly — so it is pinned green rather than dropped, keeping the face covered by name; while a *third* shape of its first face turned up, a space between receiver and dot, which offers 80 candidates where three members belong. And **K18**, the playground's origin-blind `message` listener, has no honest home here at all: it is `vilan-website` code, that repo has no test harness of any kind, and forcing a pin into this one would have been a pin in name only.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Member completion offers the methods a type inherits as trait defaults — `xs.iter().` used to answer `next` and nothing else, one method out of fifteen.** The gatherer walked `program.implementations`, matched the impls whose subject was the receiver's type, and offered what those impl blocks themselves DECLARE. A default-bodied trait method is declared on the trait, not on the impl, so nothing ever reached one: `impl ListIterator<type T> with Iterator<T>` writes out exactly `next`, and `map`, `filter`, `take`, `skip`, `enumerate`, `zip`, `chain`, `to_list`, `fold`, `for_each`, `count`, `any`, `all` and `rev` all live on `trait Iterator<T>` as defaults, invisible on every list in the language. This was never iterator-specific, which is the reason the traversal is fixed rather than the symptom: every default-bodied trait method was invisible on every implementing type, so `PartialEq`'s `ne`, `PartialOrd`'s `lt`/`le`/`gt`/`ge` and `Ord`'s `min`/`max`/`clamp` were missing too — a type implementing `Ord` offered none of the three comparisons a reader would go looking for — along with any user trait carrying a default body.

The admission rule is the analyzer's own, so the popup and the call site agree on what a type provides. For each impl of the receiver's type, each trait that impl provides and each of that trait's supertraits contributes its default-bodied instance methods, which is how an `Ord` implementor reaches `PartialOrd`'s comparisons and not only `Ord`'s own three. A member with no default body is never inherited, because conformance forces the impl to declare it and the declaring pass has already found it there. A `[trait_only]` default stays off the concrete surface, as it does at a call site. And one name is offered ONCE: a declaration wins its name outright, which is what makes an impl that overrides a default offer the override rather than both spellings of it.

Hover and go-to-definition were checked for the same blind spot in the same pass and do not have it, for a structural reason rather than a lucky one. They read the target the analyzer already resolved AT THE CALL SITE — whichever resolution tier answered, the wiring records the member's own declaration id behind the call, and for an inherited default that id is the trait's. So hovering one shows the trait's signature and jumping lands on the trait's default body, both correct, and both now pinned so the finding stays a finding. Completion is the one surface that cannot borrow that answer, because it has to speak for a member nobody has typed yet, and re-deriving the member surface is exactly where the tier was missing. Docs `appendix/editor.md`. (kolt.local 033)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Completion asks once where the cursor actually is — so a `.` that starts the next line completes, and a string body completes nothing.** The engine read the two bytes immediately before the cursor and dispatched on them, which left it blind along two axes at once; each face reported against it was one of those axes. Trivia was the first: `p.` was a member position but `p .` was not, so a space before the dot fell through to the bare scope position and offered the whole enclosing scope — measured at eighty candidates where three members belonged — while a `.` that started the next line, the ordinary way a long method chain is written, resolved no receiver at all and offered nothing whatsoever. The difference between code and text was the second: a string body was not distinguished from an expression, so typing a caption offered every function in scope.

Rather than a third branch on the same byte pair, there is now one cursor-context model that every completion path consults, computed once and dispatched on: no code (a string body or a comment), a macro name, an element head, an import path, a member position, a `::` path, or a plain expression. Two mechanisms make it answer the faces above. The member test reads TOKEN space, so the lexer's own notion of what is significant decides where the receiver and the dot are — `p.`, `p .`, a dot alone on the next line, and a dot with a `//` note between it and its receiver are one position, the receiver is the token before the dot rather than the byte before it, and what FOLLOWS the cursor never enters the question, so completing between two links of a chain works too. The string test reads the same tokens and takes the NARROWEST one containing the cursor, which is what keeps an interpolation hole a code position: an i-string's wrapper tokens all carry the whole literal's span while a hole's tokens carry their own, so `i"now {ma|}"` still offers the scope where `i"ma| now"` offers nothing. A comment leaves no token at all, being trivia, so that one case scans bytes — and the token spans are still what rule out a `//` sitting inside a string, which opens no comment.

The boundaries around it are held rather than redrawn. The element-head work's two deliberate refusals stand: an attribute NAME is still not offered, because the desugar is name-blind on purpose and there is no table to complete from, and a head item's argument, a `.` outside any markup, and an element's child position are all still ordinary expression ground rather than head ground. A cursor ON a delimiter is code and not text, so `|"a"` and `"a"|` both complete. The item's second face — completion between two dots, `a.|.b` — does not reproduce and never did: six shapes were probed and all resolve correctly, so it stays pinned green rather than shipped as a passing pin for an open bug. The three faces that do reproduce are pinned by name and their `#[ignore]` markers are gone, alongside a context matrix that pins each class of the taxonomy in its own test, so the next regression is caught by the class it belongs to rather than by whichever face happens to get reported. Docs `appendix/editor.md`. (kolt.local 001)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**CSS hot-reload was decided by one bit, so adding a stylesheet did nothing and deleting one brought it back.** The watch round asked `old.css != leg.css` and called every answer a `css` hot-swap. That bit conflates three different transitions, and only the middle one is a hot-swap: a sheet whose TEXT changed is a sheet the page already loads, and replacing its text is a complete description of the round. A sheet that **appeared** or **vanished** changes which stylesheets the page has at all, which is a change to the browser's output, not an edit to a loaded sheet — and calling it a hot-swap produced two silent failures and one that was worse than silent. A first-ever stylesheet was announced to a page with no `<link>` to supersede: the shim walked the page's links, matched none, injected nothing, and made no request at all — a completely successful-looking round in which nothing happened, and, since a client-only round never restarts the server that rendered the page, nothing ever would. A deleted stylesheet was announced too, and **that push landed**: the dist writer only ever wrote `dist/<leg>.css` inside `if let Some(css)`, and the one thing that deleted anything swept `<leg>.<arm>.js` and `<leg>.chunks.json` only, so last round's sidecar sat there, the dev channel served it with a healthy 200, and the browser re-injected the stylesheet the developer had just removed. Deleting styles reasserted them, with no 404, no warning and no failed round anywhere in sight.

A third face was hiding behind the rank rule. When a bundle and its sidecar both change, the round pushes `swap`, on a reason recorded in S1: "a reload subsumes the stylesheet refresh". That reason died when S2 made a swap a MODULE swap instead of a document reload — nothing about re-evaluating a bundle refreshes a stylesheet — so every round that touched code and styles together, which is most rounds in an app whose styles live in its source, refreshed the code and dropped the styles on the floor. That is the reported "CSS reload sometimes does not apply at all", and no amount of fixing the presence transitions would have touched it.

One corrected classification covers all three: the css axis is decided by TRANSITION rather than by a difference. `Some` → `Some'` stays a `css` push naming the changed sidecars. `None` → `Some` and `Some` → `None` become `swap`, carrying the version bump every other browser-output change carries — and a `swap` now DECLARES the round's complete stylesheet set on the wire (`"sheets":["client.css"]`, or `[]` when the round emits none), which is what lets the browser reconcile rather than guess. The shim's apply layer became that reconciliation, keyed by sidecar name rather than by `<link>` element: a declared sheet with links to supersede is superseded exactly as before, a declared sheet with no link is this leg's own and joins `<head>` on its own, and this leg's sidecar missing from the declaration is the round's statement that it emits none, so the page's copy is withdrawn. Superseding also stopped moving a sheet to the end of `<head>`: the injected `<style>` now takes the `<link>`'s place in the cascade instead of quietly re-deciding every tie. On disk, `sweep_stale_sidecar` extends the doctrine the chunk sweep already enforces — the leg's dist namespace belongs to its LAST build — to the style sidecar, so a build that emits no stylesheet leaves none behind and the manifest's `"styles": null` and the directory finally agree. That half applies to `vilan build` too, where the same file used to outlive the source that emitted it.

Two invariants were load-bearing and are unchanged, deliberately. Asset matching still holds: a named event touches only the `<link>` whose file it names, a nameless one refreshes every stylesheet the page links, each by its own basename — and a page can now be told, and pinned, that a nameless event cannot reach a sheet with no `<link>`, which is why the CLI always names its sidecar. And the **never-reload discipline is untouched**: a 404 or a network failure still warns and leaves the stylesheet exactly as it was. A failed fetch is ambiguous and is never read as a removal; only the round's own declaration withdraws a sheet, which is precisely why the swap carries the set instead of the shim probing for it. Another browser leg's sidecar is left alone entirely — every leg's page receives the same broadcast, and injecting the admin leg's stylesheet into the client leg's page would be a new bug. The recorded limitations stay: an app that inlines its CSS gets a full swap, and a sidecar swap still replaces the whole stylesheet rather than a rule.

Pinned as the matrix the item asked for rather than as three patches: the classifier's transitions as unit cells (including the empty one, the node-leg axis, and the wire's `sheets` shape), and the apply layer's **(new / changed / removed) × (named / nameless) × (link present / absent)** as 46 named cells across two documents in `hmr_css_matrix.rs`, driving the real shipped shim. Two end-to-end pins hold the wiring against a running dev channel: a first-ever stylesheet reaching a page a boot-time-rendered server built before it existed, and a removed one leaving no sidecar on disk and declaring none on the wire. Verified live as well as in the suite — a real `run --watch`, a real SSE connection, the real bundle: adding a stylesheet to a page with no `<link>` landed it as a `<style>`, changing it updated that same element in place, and deleting it took the element back out and the file off disk, with the app's own hand-written sheet untouched and no reload at any point. Docs `guide/dev-loop.md`. (kolt.local 007)
