The neighbouring rule, which makes a resource field readable only by loan and rejects moving one out of a live aggregate, is about *reading* a field. Writing over one fell between them, so nothing covered it — on an owned struct, on a tuple element, on a fixed-array element, and through a `&mut` equally.

The rule now reads over the whole place: the component's own type is what decides, asked of the projection rather than of whatever binding it hangs off. That is deliberate and it is the point — `slot.held` and `view.held` are the same expression, and an answer that consulted the root would make the two spellings behave differently. Writing an `i32` field of a struct that holds a resource somewhere else still destroys nothing.

No liveness question is asked, and none is needed: a resource field cannot be moved out of a live aggregate, so a component always holds a live value, and a write to a component of something already moved out is a use-after-move error either way. The destructor runs *before* the write, because it reads the very slot the write is about to replace.

No emitted program changed except the resource fixture, which gained the new shapes.

---

**A trait written where a type belongs is now an error, and it is reported at the annotation.** `let x: Display = bag;` compiled. So did `fun make(): Display`, `struct Holder { item: Display }`, a method's `v: Display` parameter, and `List<Display>`. Only *using* the value failed, and only sometimes — four of six positions took a bare trait silently. The spec has said since it was written that a trait is a bound and not a type; the compiler agrees now, in every value position, whether or not the declaration is ever called.

The reason this is worth a language change rather than a better message is what the acceptance was hiding. A resource behind a bare trait annotation **lost its destructor**. `let handle: Named = Handle { id = 1 }` ran the program, printed nothing, and never closed the handle — where `let handle: Handle = …` closes it — because the resource analysis is asked whether a type carries a resource by containment and answers "no, definitively" for a trait. Nothing could see through the annotation, so the single-owner rule stopped applying too: the same value could be moved twice, compile, and run. One changed word in a type annotation, no diagnostic, live data loss.

Two more things fall out of the same fix. A trait annotation meeting a bounded generic — `let x: Display = bag; use_it(x)` — used to abort the build with an *internal* error that asked you to report your own program as a compiler bug, with the caret pointing inside the standard library; there were three ways in (a binding, a field, a return) and all three are ordinary declaration errors now. And `List<Display>` used to type-check and then quietly narrow to `List<Bag>` at the first element, so a list holding two structurally different values compiled and ran.

The message names the trait, the generic to write instead (`<T: Display>`), and — inside a trait's own declaration — `Self`, which is what a trait naming itself in a return position always meant. The note points at the trait, which may live in another module.

A trait's name stays legal everywhere it names a bound or a namespace rather than a value's type: `<T: Display>`, a supertrait, an `impl` subject (`impl Iterator<type T>`, which blankets over a bound), and the head of a qualified path (`Display::show(x)`). A generic parameter defaulted to the trait — `trait PartialEq<B = Self>`, which is how every operator trait is written — is a parameter, not the trait, and is untouched.

Five declarations in the standard library needed the new spelling, and they are the five that always meant `Self`: `Iterable::iter`, `Wire::rebuild`, and `FromJson`'s two. No call site changed and no emitted output moved — none of the five was ever called through its bare-trait type.

Known, and filed rather than quietly left: two `impl … with` blocks of the same trait for the same type still resolve by declaration order, leaving the second silently dead. It is a plain bug with or without any of the above, and it has its own entry — closed above, in this same release.

---

**Generic code compiles smaller: a generic function specialized twice at the same type is now emitted once.** Writing `List<i32>` in two places used to be enough to get two copies of everything generic that touched it — byte-identical functions, under two different names, both shipped. Across the test corpus that was 19 duplicate functions and about 1.7% of all emitted JavaScript; one program shed 30% of its output, dropping from 46 emitted functions to 34.

The compiler does not intern types — resolving a type mutates it in place, so each written type gets its own identity and one `i32` has many. The table that remembers "this generic has already been specialized for these arguments" was keyed on those identities one level down, so `List<i32>` and `List<i32>` looked like different keys whenever their two `i32`s were different objects, which is most of the time. It now keys on the *shape* of the type — what it is, recursively — so equal types share one specialization and unequal ones still get their own.

Nothing about behaviour changes; every program in the corpus runs identically before and after. Five goldens moved, all of them shrinking, and each movement is a duplicate disappearing.

---

**A pattern over a `borrows` call copies and reads like every other pattern.** `if h.slot() is (let xs, let n)` — where `slot` hands back `&mut self.cells` — bound `xs` to the receiver's own list rather than a copy of it, and re-read `n` out of the receiver's storage at every mention. Pushing to `h.cells.0` inside the leg grew `xs`; writing `h.cells.1` changed `n` under it. Neither of the two rules that govern a pattern capture fired, because the subject collected no candidates at all: the pass admitted a place and a `*view` and nothing else, and this subject is a *call*.

It is the same hole one spelling over. A call that returns a view produces no storage of its own — it names the receiver's, exactly as `h.cells` does — so its captures bind pieces of something with another owner, which is the whole of the question. The pass asks that question of the arguments the call projects now, which is where the receiver is visible; a call returning an *owned* value stays outside the rule, because its result has no second owner and its captures were always right to bind it directly.

The measurement is what fixed the shape of the rule. Admitting `borrows` calls newly reaches `Option<&mut T>` returns, whose `Some(let v)` capture *is* a view — and because references are transparent in the type system, `&mut Inner` looks like an ordinary aggregate to every test in the pass, so the copy rule fired on it and `v.n = 77` started writing a copy. A view aliases on purpose; it never copies. With that clause the corpus does not move at all, and without it two goldens do, one of them wrongly.

The rule reaches the `&` direction too, which is why it is not simply "does this call return `&mut`". A readonly projection cannot be written *through*, but the receiver it projects can be written under its own name while the leg is live, and the capture aliases the receiver's storage either way. Chained projections work for the mirror reason — `o.inner_mut().peek()` is readonly, but what it was projected from is not, so the storage it names is writable after all.

---

**Writing a resource through a `&mut` view destroys what it replaces.** `fun clear(&mut self) { self = Holder::Empty }` on a `Holder::Full(Guard)` used to destroy nothing at all. The guard was still there, in a slot nothing could reach any more, and its destructor never ran — no diagnostic, no output, a silent leak.

Assigning onto an owned binding has always done the right thing: `holder = Holder::Empty` drops the guard it displaces, which is the language's overwrite rule. The rule was written about a binding that *owns* a resource, and the machinery implemented it literally — it tracks the bindings a body owns, a loan owns nothing, so a write through `&mut self` matched nothing and planned nothing. The scope-end teardown then read the *new* tag and correctly found nothing to destroy.

The two spellings write the same storage under different names, so they now do the same thing. The width of the variant was never the mechanism: a same-width `Full(g1)` → `Full(g2)` leaked exactly as hard as a shrinking one, and so did a `&mut` parameter, a `&mut` local, a struct pointee with no enum tag anywhere, and a re-borrow two calls deep. All of them destroy their outgoing value now, running the same per-type destructor in the same order the owned spelling runs it — and the drop is emitted *before* the write truncates the payload out from under it.

The loan asks no "is it still alive" question, and does not need one. It cannot move the pointee out — matching a loan inspects without consuming, and a field is not movable out of a live aggregate — and a binding its owner already moved out of is dead, so lending it is use-after-move, which was already an error. The owned spelling keeps its own liveness check for the case only it can reach.

The same sentence fixed a second leak in the other direction. Because `&mut Holder` *is* `Holder` to the type system, `let v = &mut holder` minted a resource-typed local, and the teardown planner enrolled it as an owner — so the borrowed value was destroyed twice, once at the view's scope end and once at the owner's. A loan owns nothing: it neither escapes destroying what it overwrites nor destroys what it merely borrows.

Found while proving it and filed rather than ridden in on: overwriting a resource-holding *component* — `slot.held = Holder::Empty` — still destroys nothing. That is a different rule (the one about fields, not the one about bindings) over a different set of programs, and it gets its own pass.

---

**Writing a whole value through a `&mut` view replaces it. It used to merge.** `fun replace(v: &mut List<i32>) { v = [9] }` called on `[1, 2, 3]` left the caller's list three elements long — `len()` still said `3`, and `2` and `3` were still in it, behind the `9`. Nothing in the program said "merge", and nothing reported anything.

The write has to keep the pointee's identity — that is how it reaches the caller at all — so it copies the new value's slots into the existing storage rather than rebinding a local. The copying was `Object.assign`, which is a *merge*: it writes the slots the source has and leaves every slot past them exactly where it was. For any aggregate whose width is fixed on both sides — a struct, a tuple, a `Map` — merge and replace are the same operation, which is why this went unnoticed. For the two whose width can shrink they are not, and the tail survived.

The other shrinking shape is an enum reassigned to a smaller variant. `self = Feed::Done` over a `Feed::Ready(List<str>, i32)` wrote the tag and left the list and the index sitting in the slots behind it: unreachable through the enum's own API, since every read goes through the tag, but present in the value — and where the payload is a **resource**, a live object with a destructor that nothing can reach any more. The write truncates now, so a shortened value is the value you wrote and nothing else.

One thing this did *not* fix was recorded rather than quietly left: overwriting a variant through a view did not run the old payload's destructor, because the overwrite rule is written about a binding that *owns* a resource and a loan owns nothing. Truncating the slot did not change that either way; it only meant the leaked object was no longer also reachable from the enum. That was a memory-model question rather than a codegen one, and it is answered above, in this same release.

Two corpus goldens moved, both by exactly the new write and the small helper behind it; nothing else in the corpus changed.

---

**An error in the web playground names the file it is in, even when the program also has a syntax error.** The playground gets one flat list of errors — the entry file's own lex and parse failures first, then everything the analyzer found — but the file each error belongs to is recorded only for the analyzer's half. The playground indexed that record with the *flat* position, so every attribution after a recovered syntax error was off by the number of syntax errors ahead of it, and the visitor's own syntax error could come back labeled with a standard-library file. The language server has always subtracted the prefix; the playground does now too.

---

**Internal:** the `const` pass's two report lists (calls that cross into compile-time-only territory, and compile-time-only values that escape as values) now sort on their whole entry rather than on the span's start alone, so the `dedup` beside them — which compares the whole entry — cannot be defeated by two distinct entries sharing a start. Latent: no diagnostic in the tree changed.

---

**A `next` with no return annotation is read from its body instead of being waved through.** `fun next(&mut self) { self.count += 1; }` compiled, and the loop it drove threw `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined` at runtime — the lowering reads an `Option` tag off whatever `next` hands back, and this one hands back nothing.

v0.32.0 checked the *annotation* and deliberately left the unannotated half alone, because `IteratorFromFn::next` in std is written `fun next(&mut self) { (self.fn)() }` and had to stay legal. But "unannotated" was never what makes that one legal — its body yields an `Option<T>`, which is exactly what reading the body says. So there is one rule for both spellings, the one the protocol always stated (`next(&mut self): Option<T>`), and the annotation only decides where the answer is read from. `from_fn` still works, and so does the same shape written by hand.

The rule is the shape rather than the emptiness, which closes the quieter half of the family at the same time: an unannotated `next` that yields a perfectly good `i32` used to run the loop zero times and exit 0, because `number[0]` is `undefined` and `undefined !== 0` breaks immediately. Both are compile errors now, and the two diagnostics stay distinguishable — one sends you to the annotation, the other to the body it was read from. The check follows an inherited trait default too, since the loop drives whatever body it resolved to and where that body came from cannot change what the lowering reads. Five pins, planted red on three axes — including the reverse plant, "unannotated is an error", which takes `from_fn` down and is what the carve-out pin exists to catch.

**A `next` inherited from a trait default drives a `for` loop.** `impl Down with Countdown<i32> { fun tick(..) .. }`, where the trait supplies `fun next(&mut self) { self.tick() }` as a default, refused to iterate: "cannot iterate `Down`: it has no `next(&mut self): Option<T>`". `down.next()` resolved perfectly well and returned exactly what the loop wanted.

The loop was asking a different question from every other caller. Method resolution has a tier for inherited defaults; the `for` lowering asked only for members an impl block *declares*, and `impl Empty with Fixed<i32> {}` declares none. Since v0.32.0 that came out as a clean refusal rather than the silent walk of the struct's field array it used to be, which made it a good error and still the wrong answer — a protocol resolved on a method *name* cannot mean one thing at a call and another at a loop. The loop now consults the same tier, in the same order, and dispatches to the concrete receiver through the same channel the call does.

Precedence is untouched and pinned: a `next` declared on the type beats an inherited one, unconditionally. Two *different* traits offering same-named defaults is now an ambiguity the loop reports — it used to say the type had no `next` at all, sending you to write one you already had two of. Because a `for` has no spelling that names a provider (the call form's `Trait::next(receiver)` has no loop equivalent), the message steers to the edit that works: declare `next` on the type, the tier that beats both. The tier's own boundaries came along unchanged — an inherited `next` that returns something other than an `Option` is the same compile error a declared one is, and a `[trait_only]` default still does not reach the concrete surface.

Two more shapes needed real work rather than the lookup alone. The element type is written in the *trait's* parameter, so it instantiates in two steps — the providing impl's generics from the receiver, then the trait's parameters from the arguments that impl wrote — and one step is not enough when the impl is itself generic (`impl Bag<type T> with Feed<T>` maps the trait's `T` onto a binder that is still abstract; `pair.1` was "cannot access field '1' on type T"). And `next_mut`, the `for e in &mut it` form, is a separate member reached the same way, so it has its own pin with the writes landing back in the receiver. Eight pins, planted red on three axes; corpus goldens byte-identical, since the shape was a compile error before.

**A closure passed to a generic method now knows what its parameter is.** `holder.apply(Route::Away(7), |current| match current { Other::First => 0, Other::Second(let id) => id })` compiled — matching one enum's variants against another enum's value — and printed `7`, having pulled `Other::Second`'s payload out of a `Route::Away`. The free-function twin of the same call rejected it, which is what made the shape look like a pattern-checker gap rather than what it was.

Nothing was wrong with the pattern checker: `current` reached the closure body still typed as the method's abstract `T`, so there was no enum for the arms to disagree with. The reason it stayed abstract is that an unannotated closure parameter's type is a **one-shot slot** — it is filled only while it is still unknown, so whoever writes it first writes it forever — and both call paths were willing to write it from a substitution they already knew to be incomplete. The method path bound the callee's generics from the other arguments, typed the closure arguments, and only *then* decided to defer because an argument's type had not landed yet; on that attempt `T` was unbound, `render: |T| i32` typed `current` as `T`, and the retry that finally knew `T = Route` found the slot taken. Which is why `apply(Route::Home, ..)` was fine and `apply(Route::Away(7), ..)` was not: a bare variant types immediately, a variant *call* takes a round trip.

Both paths now bind from the non-closure arguments and defer **before** typing any closure. The free-function path walks its parameters positionally, which is the same two-phase order for free — until the closure stands first (`fun apply<T>(render: |T| i32, value: T)`), where it had the identical bug and now hoists the same binding pass. Every callee shape was affected and each has its own pin: a method's own generic, a static's, a trait default's inherited through an impl that declares nothing, an impl binder's, and closures nested inside closure arguments. So is the closure's **return** type, which travels through the same substitution: `step: |T| T` under `T = Route` accepts a step returning a `Route` and rejects one returning an `Other`.

What must keep working does. `std::ui::View::swap(route, |current| match current { .. })` — how every vilan app dispatches pages, and the reason v0.32.0 refused to make a match against a bare type parameter a blanket error — now gets the *real* check instead of no check. Corpus goldens are byte-identical: the hoist is applied only in the argument order that needs it, because re-inferring an argument earlier mints its type id earlier and monomorphization keys on type ids (applied unconditionally it left three goldens behaviour-identical but re-keyed, one of them emitting a duplicate `Signal::new` instance). Six pins, all planted red.

---

**`if t is (let a, let b) { t.1 = 99; print(b) }` prints `3` now. It printed `99`.** The capture read the value the *same arm* had just written — the bug v0.32.0 fixed for `&mut self`, reaching an ordinary owned local through the one write form that release left out.

The reasoning that stopped it there was right as far as it went. `is` tests and guarded match legs compile a capture to an accessor into the subject — `$a[1]` — substituted wherever the capture is named, which is faithful only if the subject cannot change under it. Assigning a whole binding cannot reach it: `t = (1, 2)` installs a fresh value and everything holding the old one keeps the old one. But that is *one* write form. `t.1 = 99` writes a **component**, which mutates the very storage the accessor points at — and so do `h.pair.1 = 9`, `xs[0] = 9`, a write through a `&mut` taken of the place, and a `&mut self` method called on it. Seven distinct spellings printed the post-write value; all seven print the matched one now.

A capture is read once, when the pattern matches, whenever the storage its subject names can be mutated in place — through a view, as before, or through any of those forms on an owned place. Assigning the whole binding still is not one of them, deliberately: it genuinely leaves a snapshot, and treating it as a write would have cost the sharing that keeps read-only walkers from deep-copying at every level.

That cost was measured rather than assumed. The blunt fix — read *every* pattern capture at the match — is also correct, and was built and rejected on evidence: it moves six corpus goldens, and the diffs are not six added copies. One of them is the `&self` walker v0.32.0 pinned in bytes precisely to protect. Three more abandon the `else if` chain for a flat fallthrough sequence, because a guard that reads a capture now reads a declaration — restructuring matches that have nothing to do with any of this. The shipped rule changes **no** existing corpus byte; one golden moves additively, because the capture fixture gained the new shapes on purpose — the component-write function and the rebinding one sit side by side there, differing by the one line that is the rule.

The three payload shapes keep the doctrine v0.32.0 settled, and each is now pinned on the owned path against its viewed twin: a value materializes, an aggregate copies (it already did), and a resource materializes **bare** — no copy, because there is no copy spelling for a resource and inventing one would mint a second owner with a second destructor. Fifteen pins, planted red on four separate axes.

Found on the way and filed, not fixed: a method returning a `&mut` projection (`fun slot(&mut self): &mut T borrows self`) hands its pattern a subject the capture pass does not recognise as one at all, so *neither* rule fires there — the aggregate capture aliases the receiver outright, which is the original v0.23.6 bug surviving in a third spelling. Pinned `#[ignore]`d. Record: `proposal/capture-clones.md` §7.

---

**A failing `async fun main()` now reports its error and exits non-zero — the entry shim stopped dropping `main`'s promise.** An async `main` is emitted as a fire-and-forget IIFE, and nothing held onto what it returned. What a failing program did was therefore the *host's* policy rather than the language's: Node 15 and later rethrow an unhandled rejection and do exit non-zero, so this was less broken than it was recorded as being — but the program's own error came out buried under `UnhandledPromiseRejection` and an engine-internal stack trace, and a host configured any other way exits 0 with the failure discarded. A sync `main` that panics has always terminated with the message and a non-zero code. The two agree now.

This matters more than it would have on its own, because closing the top-level-await hole makes `async fun main()` the answer the language gives to "I need to await before my program does its work" — so it had better be an honest one.

The shim attaches a handler rather than awaiting, and that is the whole design: attaching does not delay anything, so a `main` that never settles — a server holding a listener — is untouched and keeps running, exactly as before. The handler exits rather than setting an exit code, for the mirror-image reason: a rejection while some other handle is still live would otherwise set a status and then hang forever. By the time the handler sees the error the unwind through `main` has already run its `finally` blocks, so nothing skips teardown. The browser leg is unchanged and gets no handler at all — there is no exit status to set, and no `process` to reference.

Six corpus goldens moved, all of them by exactly the four lines of the new tail and nothing else.

**A module-level `await` is refused in every spelling — it used to compile, and emit a top-level `await` nobody had signed off on.** The rule "a module-level binding cannot await" has shipped since v0.4.0, and it was enforced by a check that walked each initializer's *calls* and errored when one of them was async. That is a call-shaped check standing in for an await-shaped rule, and the gap between the two was reachable: `await` on a `Task`-valued binding, on a spawn (`await async work()`), on an `async { .. }` block, or on a `Task` returned by an ordinary sync function is an `await` whose operand is not a call at all. All four compiled clean, and all four emitted a genuine top-level `await` into the bundle.

That output was not merely unsanctioned, it was broken on two of the legs that had to run it. On Node the emitter parenthesizes the await operand, and `await (x)` is *valid CommonJS* — it parses as a call to a function named `await` — so Node's ESM syntax detection never fired and an extern-free program died at runtime with `ReferenceError: await is not defined`. Programs that happened to import a Node extern were rescued by accident, because the extern's `import` line is an unambiguous ESM marker. Under a watch round the same binding was wrapped in the hot-reload adopt thunk, which is built synchronous, producing `return await (pending);` inside a non-async arrow — a dev bundle that does not parse at all.

The check is now await-shaped: an `await` reachable anywhere in a module-level binding's own initializer is an error, whatever its operand and however deep in the expression it sits. The two checks are complementary and both are kept — the call check catches the implicit await and names the callee, the new one catches the explicit await and names the operand — and a binding reports one refusal, never a pair for the same line. Where the operand is a module-level binding the message says to hold it and move the `await` into `main`; otherwise it says to bind the `Task` first.

**Build artifacts for the process targets are named `.mjs`, so the runtime classifies them instead of guessing.** This is the other half of the same defect, and it is a *breaking change to artifact filenames* on the Node, Deno and Bun legs: `vilan build app.vl` writes `app.mjs`, and a multi-entry package writes `dist/<name>.mjs` per process entry. Scripts, Dockerfiles and process managers that name the old path need updating — `node dist/server.js` becomes `node dist/server.mjs`.

Vilan has always emitted ESM. A process runtime decides whether a file is ESM or CommonJS *before running it*, from the extension or the nearest `package.json`, and only falls back to sniffing the source — and the sniff could not see vilan's output, because `await (x)` is valid CommonJS. What actually rescued programs was incidental: the `import` line a Node extern happens to emit. Closing the top-level-await hole makes that shape unreachable from vilan source, so this is no longer a live crash — but the classification was never something to leave to a guess, and it is fixed by construction rather than by absence. The three temp scripts `run`, `test` and each watch round hand to Node are `.mjs` too; those no user sees.

**The browser leg keeps `.js`,** deliberately and pinned in both directions. A browser bundle is loaded by a `<script type="module">` tag, which declares the module at the load site, and browsers do not sniff — the extension carries no weight there, and renaming it would break every scaffolded `index.html` for nothing. The extension is derived in one place from the target platform, so the writers and every path that later launches or reports an artifact cannot disagree about a name; two of them previously rebuilt `dist/<name>.js` from their own format string.

The ~100 corpus goldens moved from `.js` to `.mjs` — **their bytes are unchanged**, which the byte-identical gate confirms; the rename is what a corpus program compiles *to*, since bare files build for the default Node target. One rule genuinely changed shape: two build units sharing a name used to be rejected because they would overwrite the same bundle, and on different platforms they now would not — but they still overwrite the `<name>.css` sidecar and the `<name>.chunks.json` manifest, so the name rule stands and only its message stopped naming one extension.

**The restriction is a design position now, and the spec says so.** It used to read as an implementation gap ("no top-level await in the emission model"). It is not one. Module initialization order is *derived* from what each binding reads, so a suspension there stalls bindings that never asked to wait, in an order nobody wrote. What top-level await is usually reached for, vilan already has and keeps better: module-level **spawn** starts the work at load without suspending — and independent spawns overlap, where awaited initializers would serialize — while `async fun main()` does the waiting somewhere a reader can see it. Initialization cycles stay a compile error with a witness chain, where the ESM model gives a silent deadlock.

Creating a closure or an `async { .. }` block in an initializer is still legal, and that boundary is deliberate and pinned in both directions: a closure's body is not the initializer, and nothing runs it at load. Eleven pins covering every row of the rule, planted red on two opposing axes (the call-shaped regression, and an over-widening that reaches into created closures). The sweep found no program in std, the corpus, the examples or the docs that the widened check flips — no corpus golden had ever contained the shape, so the byte-identical gate had never seen it. Record: `proposal/top-level-await.md`.

---

**A `for` over a type whose `next` two traits declare says so, instead of "it has no `next`".** A struct implementing two traits that each declare `fun next(&mut self): Option<T>` was refused with "cannot iterate `Twin`: it has no `next(&mut self): Option<T>`" — of a type that has two of them, sending the reader off to write a method that was already there twice.

The loop asked for the winning member through a lookup that answers "no such member" and "two traits provide it, name one" with the same `None`, so the ambiguity came out the exit meant for absence. The tier *below* it — a `next` inherited from a trait default, new in this same release — already reported the ambiguity properly, which left one program saying two different things about one problem depending on whether the two providers were defaults or declarations. The loop now asks the resolution question in the form that keeps the two failures apart and reports at whichever tier the competition is in, with one message at both tiers differing only in how the providers give the member.

The steer is the one that works for a loop: a call resolves this by naming a provider — `Trait::next(receiver)` — and a `for` has no such spelling, so the message says to declare `next` on the type itself, where an inherent member beats every trait-provided one. That is also the rule that keeps the report from firing when there already *is* an inherent one, and one trait providing the name beside another trait that does not is not a competition at all. `next_mut`, the `for x in &mut subject` form, is a separate member reached through the same tiering and gets the same report. Five pins, planted red on three axes; corpus goldens byte-identical, since every program this touches was a compile error before and still is.

## v0.32.0 — 2026-08-06

**A pattern capture taken from `&mut self` is what the pattern matched, not what the arm wrote afterwards.** `if self is Feed::Ready(let items, let at) { self = Feed::Ready(items, at + 1); items[at] }` returned `items[at + 1]` — the capture read the value the *same arm* had just written. Two `step()` calls over `Ready(["a","b","c"], 0)` printed `b`, `c` instead of `a`, `b`. Every writable view was affected: `&mut self`, an ordinary `&mut` parameter, a local bound to `&mut x`, and a `*view` dereference.

The captures were not aliasing a *value* so much as re-reading a *slot*. `is` tests and guarded match legs compile a capture to an accessor into the subject — `$a[2]` — substituted wherever the capture is named, which is faithful only if the subject cannot change under it. Against an owned local it cannot, and for a reason specific to how a place changes: assigning it **rebinds** it, installing a fresh aggregate and leaving the accessor holding the old one. Through a view, assigning writes **in place** — that is how the write reaches the caller at all — so the accessor was pointed at the very storage being overwritten. The aggregate captures were already safe (they copy, so they had been read eagerly since v0.23.6); an `i32` owes no copy, so it was the scalars that carried the bug, which is why an *index* is the natural way to hit it. Captures of a viewed subject are now read once, when the pattern matches.

The unguarded `match` leg on the identical program was always correct — it declares its captures rather than aliasing them — and that asymmetry is what identified the compilation path as the culprit rather than the view.

Two rules did not move, and both are pinned. A resource payload still takes **no copy**: there is no copy spelling for a resource and inventing one would mint a second owner with a second destructor, so its capture is a loan that now simply names the value the match found — the same thing the owned-local version of the program already did. And a `&` view keeps its accessors verbatim, because nothing can be written through one; widening the rule to every view would have taken back the sharing that keeps read-only recursive walkers from deep-copying at each level, and the corpus bytes now fail if it ever is. The `*view` spelling turned out to be missing the *original* B53 copy as well — `let (xs, n) = *view` aliased outright, so growing the element through the view grew the capture — and that is fixed and pinned in the same pass. Thirteen pins, planted red on three separate axes. One golden moved, additively, because the fixture gained the new shapes — no existing corpus byte changed, since no corpus program had this shape.

Left open and filed: an owned local has one write form that mutates in place too — `if t is (let a, let b) { t.1 = 99; print(b) }` prints `99`. Same seam, and a fix with a much larger blast radius. Record: `proposal/capture-clones.md` §6.

---

**The same broken program now reports the same errors, in the same order, every time you compile it.** It did not. Diagnostics came out in whatever order the checks that produced them happened to run, and dozens of those checks walk a hash table whose iteration order is randomly seeded per process — so a file with two dozen errors produced a different listing on every one of thirty runs. In the terminal that is untidy. In the browser it is not: the dev server's error overlay shows the first twenty and collapses the rest into "… and N more", so *which* errors you were shown changed run to run, and an error could vanish behind the fold and come back without you touching anything.

Two diagnostics were worse than untidy — they gave different *answers*. A failing generic bound points at where the bound was written ("the bound is declared here"), and it picked that declaration by taking the first match out of a hash table. One bound can honestly have several declarations: `impl Holder<type T>` inherits the identity of the `T` in `struct Holder<T: Greet>`, deliberately, so the binder means what writing the bound out would mean. So the note pointed at your struct on some runs and at your impl block on others — and where std declares the bound, at your file on some runs and at std's on others. It names the earliest declaration now, which is the one that actually writes the bound; an inheriting binder only adopted it. Separately, when an async closure reaches a `sync` callback only through a helper's adaptation, the compiler reports it once and points at the call that started it. If two call sites instantiate that helper differently, both reach the same violation, and which one you were sent to was a coin flip — a fifteen-to-fifteen split over thirty runs on the repro. It now names the earliest call.

The order is decided in one place rather than patched in a dozen: diagnostics leave the pipeline sorted by file, then by position in the file, then by what they render as, right at the last point either list can grow, so the terminal, the language server and the overlay all read the one list. Two shapes had to be respected and are worth recording. A diagnostic's file lives in a *parallel* vector indexed by position, so sorting one without the other would have silently reattributed every error to the wrong file. And a note is a field of its diagnostic rather than a separate entry paired by position, which is the only reason sorting is safe at all. Warnings got the same treatment; so did two order-dependent internals that had not yet reached anything visible — synthetic dereference ids were minted in hash order on every compile, and the synthetic destruction edges platform coloring walks were built from an unordered set.

Emitted JavaScript was never affected and still is not: the corpus goldens are byte-identical and both release-preset gates pass. Pinned by a harness that analyzes cold thirty times with the reuse cache cleared per attempt and compares the whole rendering — every span, message and note, in order — with each of the three fixes planted back and measured red.

---

**`enum Dup { A = 1, B = 1 }` is an error now — it used to be a wrong answer.** Both variants lowered to the same runtime value, so a `match` on `Dup::B` ran `Dup::A`'s arm: exhaustively matched, exit 0, wrong result, no diagnostic anywhere. Nothing checked discriminant uniqueness, and nothing checked much else about a discriminant either. `= 1.5` truncated to `1`. A magnitude past 64 bits became **`0`** — an ordinary discriminant a sibling may legitimately hold, which fed the overflow straight into the duplicate hole. `= 0xFF` also became `0`, and `= 1_000` became `1`, because the parser reduced the number token's whole part with `parse::<i64>().unwrap_or(0)` and threw the rest away. And a discriminant that could never reach the runtime at all — anywhere in an enum with a payload variant — parsed, was stored, and was silently discarded.

All of it is now checked at the declaration. A duplicate names both variants and the shared value, counting the values implicitly continued from the previous variant (`enum Walked { A = 1, B = 0, C }` collides on 1 without a second `=` in sight). A fraction or a trailer is rejected with the literal quoted back. An out-of-range magnitude says the range, in both directions, and the bounds themselves stay legal — the negative one reaches one further, because the minus applies to the magnitude rather than being parsed into it. Hex is read as hex. And the continuation itself is checked: a variant walking past the bound is an error, where a plain `+ 1` used to panic the debug compiler.

Two spec gaps closed alongside, because both are things you could only have learned by reading the compiler. The grammar wrote the discriminant production as `NUMBER` where the parser meant an integer. And the representation rule was written down nowhere: an enum lowers to its bare discriminant only when **every** variant is data-less **and** at least one has an explicit discriminant — a conjunction, so adding `= 0` to a single variant changes the whole enum's runtime shape, including how its values cross a host boundary. That is also why a discriminant beside a payload variant is now rejected rather than dropped: the tagged representation has nowhere to put it.

The sweep found no in-tree violations — std's `Ordering` is the one enum that uses the feature and it was always legal — so the rule rejects only programs that were already miscompiling. Thirteen pins, planted red on four separate axes. Corpus goldens unmoved. Record: `proposal/backed-enums.md` §1.7, which found all of this while surveying for string-backed enums; the messages state the rule as it stands rather than as a permanent one, so they do not foreclose that proposal.

**Two `fun which()` in one `impl` block are an error now — they used to be the second one.** The identical two declarations *one block apart* have been a hard error since v0.30.0, so this was the odd shape out: write them in separate blocks and the compiler stops you; write them adjacent, in the same block, and it compiles and quietly runs the second. Nothing about the rule differed. The duplicate check reads each impl's declarations back off the scope map, and a map holds one entry per name — so the second declaration overwrote the first during the walk, and by the time the check ran there was no pair left to compare.

A scope now keeps the ordered record of every declaration it saw, repeats included, beside the name-to-id index it always had — the same shape local `let` bindings already use to make shadowing work. The surface a type presents is *derived* from that record instead of standing in for it, so the check gets the pair, and the error is the same one the cross-block case has always produced, note and all. Methods, statics, the mixed static/method pair, and `external` declarations all behave alike, because receiver position was never part of a member's identity.

There is a second rule alongside it, deliberately: a name written twice in **one** block is a mistake whatever trait homes it. The cross-block rule exempts trait-provided names on purpose — that is what keeps two impls of one trait legal, including std's blanket `Into` beside your own — but that exemption exists to protect two *separate* blocks from each other, and there is no second block to protect inside one. Trait bodies are covered for the same reason. An inherent duplicate is still reported exactly once.

The sweep found no same-block duplicates anywhere in the tree, so nothing in-tree changed. Ten pins, planted red on three separate axes. Corpus goldens unmoved. Record: `proposal/method-resolution.md` §11.

**`Bag::default()` calls `Bag`'s own `default()`, not the one a trait provides.** It called whichever impl block happened to register first — so writing `impl Bag with Default` above `impl Bag` gave you the trait's answer, and swapping the two blocks gave you the type's own, with nothing to tell you which you had. v0.30.0 established that a type's own member outranks every trait-provided one and made `receiver.method()` obey it; the `Type::static()` path was still a flat scan in registration order, and never got the rule. It has it now, and the order the blocks are written in stops mattering.

Two traits providing the same static, with nothing of the type's own above them, is an error rather than a silent pick — the same ambiguity a method call already reports. The steer differs, and deliberately: for a method you can write `Trait::method(receiver)` to pick one, because the receiver names the type. A static has no receiver, so there is nothing for a `Trait::static()` path to select an impl through, and no such spelling exists. The message says so outright and names the fix that does work — declare the type's own, which outranks both — rather than pointing at a path that would not resolve.

A trait-provided static with no competition still resolves, which is the load-bearing half: unlike a method, it has no other spelling, so the trait tier has to stay reachable. `Iterator::from_fn` and every other static declared on a trait-subject impl are untouched.

The sweep found no type in the tree with the same static name from both an inherent block and a trait, so no in-tree call changed: corpus goldens byte-identical, all ten examples build. Nine pins, planted red on four separate axes, plus the v0.31.0 pin that recorded the inversion in a comment and now asserts the value. Record: `proposal/method-resolution.md` §12.

---
---

**`for x in set` walks the set — from inside `Set`'s own methods, from a parameter, from a call result.** Only from a `let` binding or a field did it ever work. Everywhere else the loop ran exactly once, whatever the set held, and said nothing: a three-element set counted 1, a hand-written `union` of two sets came back with one element. Nothing errored, nothing warned, and the number was plausible enough to be believed.

The loop's native lowering — a `Set` is a struct over a native map, so the loop has to walk the map's stored values rather than the struct itself — was chosen by asking the *iterable expression* for its type at emission time. That question has no answer for an expression that produces no type of its own, and a bare reference to a parameter is one: `self`, `other`, a plain `s: Set<i32>` argument, and equally a call result or a `*view` all came back untyped, so the lowering quietly did not fire and the loop fell through to iterating the struct's single backing field. The one form that always worked, a `let` binding, is the one form whose type is recorded.

The type is now taken from the analyzer, which computed it for this very loop when it decided the loop's element type and is the only place it is known in full. Every shape of iterable therefore gets the same answer — including `for x in self`, which std's own `Set` methods have been routing around via `self.table.values()` by convention since the type existed. That convention is now a style choice rather than a requirement; the existing methods keep it (rewriting them would be churn), and writing the direct form in your own `impl Set<type T: Hashable>` block is correct. Eight pins across the iterable shapes, the seven `Set` ones planted red and restored; `List` and `str` are natively iterable, were never affected, and are pinned as the unmoved half. No emitted byte moved anywhere in the corpus.

The sweep behind that fix turned up a neighbouring hole two lanes converged on from opposite sides: `for x in map` compiled, at every call site, and iterated once — `Map` has no loop form at all. The for-loop rule shipped above closes it: the loop now refuses an un-iterable struct and names `map.entries()`, `map.keys()` and `map.values()`. The pin one lane left waiting for that refusal is un-ignored in the same release it was written.

## v0.31.0 — 2026-08-06

**Writing `impl List<type T>` in your own file no longer makes the compiler complain about std's `List`.** Roughly half the time — the same file, the same compiler, a different answer per run — a user impl block on a std container produced "the type of 'result' is never fully determined", pointing at a `mut result = List::new()` line inside `list.vl` that you did not write and cannot annotate. The other half of the runs were clean, which is the tell: the diagnostic was never about your program.

The residual-generic check asks whether a binding's leftover type parameter is declared in the binding's own file — legitimate inside a generic function's body, a real leak when it is some callee's parameter arriving from elsewhere. It answered that by recording one declaring file per parameter. But a parameter can honestly be declared in several: `impl Subject<type T>` deliberately *inherits* the subject type's own parameter identity, so that writing the binder means exactly what writing the subject's bound out means — and your `impl List<type T>` therefore **is** `list.vl`'s `T`. Recording one file for it collapsed a set into a coin flip, and the coin was a randomly seeded hash table's iteration order, so which file "declared" `T` changed run to run. When your file won, every `List<T>` inside `list.vl` read as a foreign leak.

The check now keeps the set of declaring files and asks whether the binding's own file is among them, which is the question the rule always meant to ask and does not depend on iteration order. The leak it exists to catch is untouched — an unannotated `Map::new()` or `Set::new()` still requires its annotation, including in a file that also writes `impl Map<type K, type V>`, because a static's own parameters are not the struct's. Only the first compile in a process could ever show this: every later one is served from the reuse cache, which always held the clean world. Pinned at 30 cold compiles with the cache cleared per attempt, in both directions.

**`for pair in [(1, "a")].iter()` gives you the pair.** It gave you "cannot access field '0' on type T" — the loop binding came out as the iterator's own generic parameter instead of the type it was instantiated at. The same iterator pulled by hand, `if it.next() is Some(let pair)`, always worked, and so did `for pair in [(1, "a")]` straight over the `List`; only the loop over an iterator lost it.

It is not a tuple problem. The loop reads its element off the declared return type of the subject's `next` — `impl ListIterator<type T> { fun next(..): Option<T> }` — and took that `T` verbatim, without instantiating it against the receiver's arguments the way the trait-bounded arm beside it always did. A bare `T` admits nothing, so **every** element shape refused: a struct's field, a nested `List`'s `len()`, an `Option`'s `unwrap_or`, and calling a closure element at all ("cannot call this as a function: it is T"). The `&mut` lending form (`next_mut`) over a generic container had it too, as did an enum-shaped iterator.

`enumerate` and `zip` hid it for the whole adapter arc, and now it is clear why: their payloads are written structurally, `Option<(i32, T)>` and `Option<(T, U)>`, so the loop saw a tuple whose *parts* were abstract — which projects fine — rather than a whole that was. Only an iterator whose element **is** the parameter could reach the gap, and `List::iter` is the first one std ever had.

**A `for` over a struct that has no `next` is a compile error instead of printing the struct's fields.** It printed them. `struct Cursor { items: List<i32>, index: i32 }` in a `for` loop printed `[ 1, 2 ]`, then `0`, then exited 0 — the loop had nothing to drive, fell through to a native `for...of`, and JavaScript walked the value the struct *is* at runtime, which is its flat field array. Nothing was reported, so the program looked like it worked. An enum did the same over its `[tag, ..payload]` array, and a struct with a *field* named `next` did it while looking, at a glance, like it had implemented the protocol.

The check for this already existed one type-shape over. A `for` over a generic subject whose bounds provide no iterator has been an error since v0.29 — same reasoning, same nonsense fallback — and the concrete path simply never got it. It has it now, and the message names the method the loop wanted and what to do instead.

The rule the loop follows is stated on the [collections page](https://vilan-lang.org/docs/std/collections.html#what-a-for-can-iterate): a `for` iterates a type with `next` (or `next_mut`, for `for e in &mut it`), or one of the natively iterable values — `List`, a fixed array, a tuple, a `str`, a `Set`, and any host type an `external struct` names. Nothing else. Two consequences worth calling out. `for entry in map` was in the "nothing else" group all along and used to print the backing map object; it is now an error naming `entries()`, `keys()` and `values()`, which is how the docs have always shown a `Map` being walked. And the protocol still resolves on the method *name*, but the **shape is checked now too**: a `next` annotated with something other than an `Option` used to be driven anyway, and since the loop reads the `Option` tag off whatever came back, a `fun next(&mut self): i32` gave a loop that ran zero times and exited 0. An *unannotated* `next` is still judged by its body, because std's own `IteratorFromFn` is written that way.

Every `.vl` file in the repository was compiled against the new check — std, the corpus, every example, every docs fence, 225 files — and not one of them changes. Nine pins, seven of them red without the fix and the two positive controls red when the exemption set is emptied instead.

**`value is Colour::Red` where `value` is your function's own `T` is a compile error, because it was never checking anything.** The pattern resolver waved a bare type parameter through beside the genuinely unresolved types, on the reasoning that an unresolved type might still turn out to be this enum. A parameter never will — and the tag test was emitted regardless, so the pattern matched by coincidence of representation rather than by type. A `List<i32>` `[0, 7]` "matched" `Shape::Circle(let radius)` and bound `radius = 7`. A function declared `: i32` returned the string out of `Fruit::Apple("x")` through a `Shape::Circle` pattern. And a trait default written over its own `T` — one body, two impls — matched `Colour::Red` against a `Fruit::Apple(9)` and returned the *wrong branch*, silently, exit 0.

The same pattern against a concrete value has been two clean errors for a long time ("variant 'Colour::Red' does not belong to the matched enum", "cannot match an enum variant against type i32"). The check existed; a parameter simply never reached it.

**Which parameter it is decides whether the compiler can say anything, and that split is the shape of the fix.** A parameter declared by something *enclosing* the pattern — the function's own `<T>`, a trait's parameter inside a default — is abstract there by construction: the body is checked once for every instantiation at once, so no later pass can make it concrete and no answer the compiler gives can be right for all of them. That is the error. A parameter that arrived from *elsewhere* means only "not substituted yet", and leaving it alone was not a judgement call: a closure passed to a **method's** own generic reaches its body with the parameter still abstract, which is `View::swap`'s exact shape, so a blanket rule takes `swap(route, |current| match current { .. })` — the routing guide, and the way every vilan app dispatches pages — down with it. Probed, not assumed: five diagnostics across two guide pages.

That leaves a real hole rather than hiding one, and it is worth stating plainly, because it is the sharper defect of the two: **a closure argument to a method's own generic is not checked at all.** Matching a completely unrelated enum's variants inside one compiles and binds their payloads out of your value. The free-function twin substitutes the parameter and gets the full check today, which is what says the method path is a gap and not a design. Pinned `#[ignore]`d against the day the parameter is instantiated there too.

Seven pins. Four red without the check, and the three that guard against over-reach — the concrete diagnostics, `Some(let v)` on an `Option<T>` inside a generic function, an enum matching its own variants in its own `impl`, a literal pattern against a `T` — red under the over-eager version that refuses a parameterized enum.

---

**`fun show(v: Display)` now tells you to write `fun show<T: Display>(v: T)`.** Calling it with a value that plainly implements the trait failed with "Expected Display, but got Bag instead" — a message that accuses the impl, when the impl is fine and the signature is not. Traits in vilan are **bounds**, not types; the compiler has said so from inside a function body for a while ("a trait is not a value type — vilan has no trait objects"), and the spec lists using one as a type among its rejection cases. The call site simply had no version of that rule. It has one now, with a note at the parameter declaration where the fix goes, and it renders across modules. An argument that does *not* implement the trait keeps the plain mismatch, because there the missing impl is the likelier mistake.

The narrow cause is worth recording: a call is the only position that unifies **parameter-first** (deliberately, so generic bindings key on the callee), so it is the only position that ever asks the compiler to match a trait against a concrete type in that direction — and that direction had no rule. Every other position asks it the other way round and quietly accepts, which is why a bare trait still compiles as a `let` annotation, a field, a return type and a method's parameter. Those are pinned in both directions rather than left undescribed; making them agree means deciding whether a trait may name a type at all, which is trait objects' question to answer.

Making the parameter *work* was tried and rejected on evidence, not taste: accepting the value lets it flow onward, and the moment it reaches a bounded generic the compiler has no concrete implementation to select and reports an internal error asking you to report the program. Eleven pins, planted red on three axes. Record: `proposal/method-resolution.md` §10.

**Two impls declaring the same `fun new()` for one type are an error, like two methods already were.** v0.30.0 made a duplicate *method* a definition-site error and left statics a silent pick, because the check filtered on the receiver — which turned out to be the wrong axis entirely. An impl's members are one map keyed by name, so a type has one surface and receiver position was never part of a member's identity; the filter was doing double duty as "methods only" and "same namespace only", and only the second was ever meant. Every inherent declaration now competes, whatever its receiver, with the same cross-file note naming the other declaration and its module.

That also closes the mixed pair, which was the worst-behaved of the three. A static `fun tag()` in one impl and a method `fun tag(self)` in another both compiled, and the static was simply unreachable: `Bag::tag()` resolved the method and then failed on arity, reporting a wrong argument count for a declaration that could never have been called. It is now reported where it is written.

The sweep this needed found **no live collisions at all** — std, all 111 corpus programs, every example and every compiled docs fence — so the rule's entire blast radius is the shapes it was built to catch, and no in-tree code changed. Nine pins plus a cross-file one, each planted red on three separate axes (receiver filter restored, trait-homing removed, subject comparison removed) and restored. The trait-homing guard is what keeps a trait's own static — `Default::default`, `Wire::rebuild` — out of the inherent tier; removing it turns std's `Duration::describe` red, which is how it was proven load-bearing rather than argued.

**`let f = helper; f(1)` calls `helper`.** Naming a function and calling the name later failed with "cannot call this as a function: it is fn helper(i32): i32" — of something spelled `fun`, that the caller had just written a call on. The annotated form (`let f: |i32| i32 = helper`) always worked, and so did passing the name into a closure-typed parameter, returning it, and storing it in a field; only the unannotated binding did not, and `proposal/fn-coercion.md` §4 had recorded the opposite outcome ("calling such a binding works as before"), so nothing was ever deliberately refused here. The call resolver dispatched on what the subject *was* — a binding, not a declaration — and never looked at its type. It looks now, and resolves through the declaration, so arity, argument types, the call's own result type and the emitted code all come from the same path a direct call uses. Emission needed nothing: a function reference already emits as its own name, which is why the annotated form worked all along.

Which functions have a value form is one rule, not two. The four that do not — generic, `async`, methods, and `external` — are exactly the four the fn-to-closure coercion already excluded, each deferred with its own reason, and they stay excluded here rather than being widened as a side effect. That matters concretely: left open, a generic one **miscompiled** (the binding emits the declaration's name while monomorphization mints instance names from a separate pool, so the call reached a name that specialization never produced) and an `async` one handed back an unawaited promise. Each refusal now says which rule it hit and what to write instead, which is what the tour already promised it would. Thirteen pins, planted red on three separate axes and restored. Corpus goldens unmoved.

---

**`vilan bindgen` can now bind a constructor, which took `lib.dom.d.ts` from 65.8% of its declarations to 92.3%.** v1 bound methods and properties at 99.8% and could not reach a single object in the first place: 824 of the file's declarations are a `declare var`, and no `[extern(…)]` form reads a bare global as a value. But 641 of those 824 are not a global read at all — they are a host *class*, spelled as a value because TypeScript states an instance side (the `interface`) and a static side (a `declare var` whose object type carries `new(…)`) separately. bindgen reads the pair back together and emits `[extern(new, "HTMLCanvasElement")]`, the extern form that exists precisely for it. `HTMLCanvasElement::new()` exists; so do the statics beside it, as dotted globals — `Response::json(…)` from `declare var Response`'s static object, without shadowing the instance `json()` it collides with. The `Object()` bag constructor an interface used to get is *replaced* where a real constructor exists, because `Object()` hands back a plain `{}`, which an `HTMLCanvasElement` is not.

The recognizer is a syntactic match, and it is narrow on purpose. A construct signature is the whole signal: `prototype` need not be present (it is decoration, and is never itself bound — reading `X.prototype` yields the shared prototype, never an instance), and the global's name need not match the type's — `declare var Image: { new(…): HTMLImageElement }` binds on `HTMLImageElement`, as `new_image`, beside that type's own `new`. Three near-misses are named rather than guessed at: an object-typed global with **no** construct signature is not a class and has no type for its members to hang off; a constructor object whose type this file never declares has nothing for the binding to return; and the `interface FooConstructor { new(): Foo }` spelling (`lib.es5.d.ts`'s) is recognized and deliberately refused, because folding it in while also emitting `FooConstructor` would say the same thing twice.

**`--only <Type>` emits one type and everything its signatures reach.** Repeatable, composing into one closure. `extends` flattening multiplies — Vilan has no struct inheritance, so every base member is copied into each derived type — and pointed at the whole of `lib.dom.d.ts` that is 492,986 lines of Vilan. `--only` cuts it to what a consumer names: base types, member types, parameter and return types, generic arguments, a closure parameter's own type, a type's host constructor. Reachability is over what *survives* the mapping table, not over the TypeScript text — an open union widens to `any` and pulls in none of its members, `T | null` binds as `T` and pulls in `T` — which is the rule that keeps a DOM-scale closure finite. An `extends` base is followed for the members flattened out of it without emitting the base itself. Cycles cost one visit, not a hang. An `--only` name the file does not declare is an error, so a typo fails instead of quietly writing less than was asked for.

**And the honest number, because it refutes the expectation it was built against.** `--only HTMLCanvasElement` against the real `lib.dom.d.ts` removes 80% of the output — 2,415 declarations to 1,001, 492,986 lines to 96,316 — and every declaration it keeps is genuinely reachable: the filtered file introduces *zero* unresolved references the unfiltered one does not already have. It is still not a file anyone reviews. The browser's element types are one strongly-connected component: a `Node` has an `ownerDocument` that is a `Document`, a `UIEvent` has a `view` that is a `Window`, and seeding from `Element`, `Node`, `HTMLElement`, or `MouseEvent` all land on the same ~900 declarations. The filter is not the limitation; the type graph is. For the third-party library bindgen actually targets, the closure is a handful of types.

**A canvas that draws through bindings nobody typed.** `vilan/examples/canvas/` is the demonstration, compiled by the suite like every other example: `canvas.vl` is 345 lines of generated output from `canvas.d.ts` beside it, and `board.vl` fills, strokes, arcs, writes text, and repaints on click entirely through it. It adds exactly one binding by hand — `[extern("document.getElementById")]` — which is the residue stated plainly: `declare var document: Document` is a value, and 183 of `lib.dom.d.ts`'s globals are in that position. A handful of entry points, not a surface. `std::browser::dom` is untouched, and stays hand-curated.

**Found while building it, and pinned rather than fixed here: two functions of one name *in one `impl` block* are not a diagnostic.** Two `fun which(self)` in a single block compile clean and the *last* one wins, silently — a scope-map overwrite that happens before the duplicate check ever runs, so the cross-block error the method-resolution rule ships ("'which' is already defined for 'Bag'") never sees them. Across two blocks the error fires as designed, `external` or not — probed both ways at the merge. bindgen emits each type's constructor, instance members, and constructor-object statics into a single `impl`, so it manages that one shared name table itself rather than trusting a check that is not there. `crates/vilan-core/tests/bindgen.rs::a_duplicate_function_name_is_silently_shadowed_rather_than_rejected` goes red the day the language rejects it.

---

**`Map`/`Set` close out I4's one remaining row: pairing entries, checking a value, and the three set operations.** `map.entries(): List<(K, V)>` is the paired counterpart `keys()`/`values()` were missing — `for entry in map.entries() { entry.0; entry.1 }` walks both together instead of zipping two separate snapshots by hand, and a repeated key keeps its original position (only its value updates, matching `insert`). `map.contains_value(v)` (needing `V: PartialEq`, a bound the rest of `Map` does not carry) is the value-side twin of `contains_key`. `set.union`/`.intersection`/`.difference` are the textbook set operations, each returning a new `Set` and leaving both receivers untouched. `std-surface.md` §3 left this row deliberately unranked (zero corpus demand) rather than undesigned; `map`/`filter`/`for_each` on either type stay out — the audit never settled what shape they would return, and `entries()` already gives a composable route (`map.entries().iter()...`) with nothing left to invent. `str` carries no gap at all per the audit's §1.3, ranked or not.

**A real bug the union/intersection/difference implementation surfaced, worked around rather than fixed here:** `for value in self` inside one of `Set`'s own methods does not get the native "iterate the backing map's values" lowering that a concrete `Set` expression gets at an ordinary call site — it walks the struct's one-element backing array instead and silently produces nonsense (a first draft of `union` reported length 1 for a 4-element result). Every existing `Set` method already routes through `self.table.values()` instead of `for x in self` for what turns out to be exactly this reason; the new methods now do too, and the underlying `is_set_typed` gap is left for its own fix.
## v0.30.0 — 2026-08-06

**Which method a `value.method()` call runs is now a rule, not an accident of where you typed the impl block.** Resolution scanned every impl of the receiver's type and took the first hit, in registration order — so an inherent method and a trait's could swap places by moving two blocks in one file, or by renaming a module so it sorted differently, with exit 0 both times and nothing said. The rule now: an **inherent** method — one declared by an impl of the type whose `with` clause does not declare that name — always beats a trait's, whatever the text order and whichever module loaded first. Otherwise the method a trait provides, whether the impl declares it or inherits the trait's default.

Folklore had this backwards where it mattered most. `iterator.vl` and `list.vl` are both std tier-0 modules tie-broken by module *name*, so `"iterator" < "list"`: a lazy adapter sharing a name with `List`'s eager `map`/`filter`/`fold` would have registered — and won — *before* `List`'s own inherent method. That is now impossible to reach by accident.

**Two declarations at the same level are an error instead of a silent pick.** Two *inherent* declarations of one name for one subject are rejected where they are written, before any call resolves them, with a note at the other declaration — Rust's E0592 shape. Two *traits* providing one name with no inherent method above them make each call ambiguous, and the message names both homes and both fixes with your own receiver already substituted: `'pick' is ambiguous on 'Bag': both 'A' and 'B' provide it; call 'A::pick(bag)' or 'B::pick(bag)' to pick one`. The same rule reaches the two places the identical bug was also hiding: two traits whose *defaults* share a name, and a `T: A + B` bound whose two arms both supply it (that one used to `break` on the first match with no check at all). Reaching one declaration twice — `Ord` and its supertrait `PartialEq` both offering `eq` — is one candidate, not an ambiguity.

**`Trait::method(receiver, args…)` is the disambiguator, and it needed no new syntax.** The grammar already parsed the qualified-path call; it just resolved through the same order-dependent scan spelled differently, and naming a *trait* at the head failed outright. It now means what it says: the named trait's version, whether the impl declares it or inherits the trait's default, and on a trait-bounded generic receiver as well as a concrete one. Its sibling `Type::method(receiver, args…)` is tightened to match — the type's own member or an error naming the trait that does have it — so there is no second, unreformed door into the ambiguity the rest of this closes.

**The one place this bit, it was already biting.** A sweep of std, every example, and the whole 111-program corpus found exactly one live collision: `vilan/test/gap-b.vl` redeclared `Option<(T, U)>::unzip`, which `std::option` already declares — dead code in a CI-gated file, undetected only because the two bodies happen to compute the same thing. It is now a compile error, and the file's copy is renamed so the gap it exists to pin stays pinned. Building every corpus program and every example with the old scan and the new resolver side by side found **no other site whose resolution changes** — the rule's entire blast radius is the bug it was built to catch. Twenty pins, each planted red and restored. Record: `proposal/method-resolution.md`.

---

**`(make(), 6).1` gives you the 6.** Building a tuple out of a value you did not first name lost that value's slots. `let pair = (4, 5); (pair, 6)` was fine; `(make(), 6)` — the same tuple, straight from the call — silently built the wrong thing, and every read past that element came back `undefined`. It compiled clean, it type-checked clean, and it has been wrong since tuples started storing flat: v0.28.0 and every release before it. No `..` was involved.

Tuples store **flat**, so an element that is itself a tuple has to splice its slots into the one being built rather than nest inside it — that is what makes `.1` resolve to the slot the type says it does. The compiler decided whether to splice by looking up the element's type in the cache the type solver fills, and that cache only holds a type where one is *produced*: a binding, a literal, a field or index read, a `match`. Everything else is typed on demand and the answer thrown away. So a **call**, an `if`, an `else if` chain, a `{ block }`, a method call, an associated call, a call through a closure value, an `await`, a `*view` read, a `const` element — and, least expected of all, a plain **parameter** — all read as untyped, all nested, and all broke the layout. Now the tuple's own type rule keeps the type it computes for each element, so coverage follows from the rule rather than from which forms happened to leave a trace. Sixteen pins, one per form plus the mixed, nested and spread-alongside cases, each planted red and restored.

An element whose type is still a generic parameter deliberately stays nested, and that is not a gap. A generic body is compiled once and emitted for every instantiation, and the flat offsets baked into that single pass count a generic element as one slot; splicing it at the instantiations that bind it to a tuple would move every offset past it. Pinned in both directions.

The spread form was never affected and still is not: `(..make(), 6)` has always been correct, because a `..` splices for having been *written* as one and asks no type question at all. Record: `proposal/variadic-generics.md` §T.8.

**A `fun` written inside another function is emitted once.** It came out twice — nested where you wrote it and again at module level, identical bodies, the inner one shadowing the outer. Nothing ran wrong, because the two were the same function, but the second copy was output no reader of the emitted JavaScript could account for and no change to which one a call resolves to would have stayed harmless. Emission is driven by demand — a call emits its callee — and the body walk was emitting the declaration a second time just for being written. It no longer does, which also means a nested `fun` nobody calls now emits nothing, like any other unreached function. The filed report said an `impl` method's body; it happened in a plain function's body too, since the double visit had nothing to do with `impl`.

**A tuple's arity is part of its type again.** `let t: (i32, str, bool) = (1, "x")` compiled clean, and so did passing a two-tuple where a three-tuple was declared, returning one, assigning one, putting both in a list literal, binding both to one generic, and writing an impl whose method returns a tuple of a different arity than the trait declares. The unifier compared tuples by walking the pair until the shorter one ran out — so it agreed on the common prefix and never noticed the rest — where the array unification beside it has always required the same length and the closure unification the same parameter count. It requires the same arity now, in both the unifier and the trait-conformance comparison that had the same hole. Nine pins, each planted red.

---

**`vilan bindgen leaflet.d.ts --platform browser` writes the bindings you would have written by hand.** A `.d.ts` describes a JavaScript library's surface precisely enough to generate the `external struct` + `[extern(…)]` `external fun` dialect `std` is written in, and now it does: interfaces, classes, functions, properties, methods, generics, `extends` chains, closures, and closed string sets all map. `getElementById` becomes `get_element_by_id` and the extern keeps the exact JS spelling; a `readonly` property gets a getter and no setter; a callback the host awaits is typed `async |T| U` because adaptation never crosses a host boundary, while a plain `(e: Event) => void` needs no annotation at all — the divergence rule already absorbs it. It is **not a build step**: nothing in `build`/`check`/`run` reaches it, and the `.vl` it writes is a file you review, edit, and commit, the way you own `vilan init`'s scaffold.

The parser is our own, and that is the one place this departs from the paper. `proposal/bindgen.md` §2 recommended `oxc`; running the gate the proposal itself said to run — *"whoever takes this item up should run `cargo about generate` against a real `Cargo.lock` with oxc added and read its own output"* — it fails. `dragonbox_ecma`, reached non-optionally through `oxc_syntax`, is `Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception OR BSL-1.0`, and neither branch is on `about.toml`'s deliberately closed list. Two more costs came with it: **44** new crates against the ~12 the proposal predicted (a 32% larger lockfile), and — because the machinery lives in `vilan-core` — an entire JavaScript parser linked into the size-tuned wasm playground artifact. A `.d.ts` is a declaration-only grammar with no expressions, no statements, and no bodies, so it is written directly instead: **zero new dependencies, no license-policy change, `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt` untouched.**

**Nothing a `.d.ts` says is ever dropped silently.** Every construct that cannot be expressed becomes a `// TODO(bindgen)` naming it and saying why — overloads (the first signature wins, the rest are quoted verbatim), namespaces, `declare module`, conditional and mapped types, `keyof`, intersections, open unions, index signatures. A generated file with TODOs is reviewable; one with silent gaps is a landmine.

**Four rows of the ratified mapping table were wrong, and all four for one reason.** The owner flagged one as conditional — `{ [index: number]: T }` → `List<T>`, *"confirm whether `List` tolerates sparse keys before shipping that mapping"* — and running it found the rest. **A vilan aggregate has a vilan-owned runtime representation that a host does not speak.** A `struct` is a positional array (`struct P { x: f64 }` is `[x]`, `p.x` is `p[0]`), so a host object read through one yields `undefined`, silently. An `enum` is `[tag, …payload]`, so a TypeScript discriminated union — a tagged *object* — matches nothing and crashes. `Map<str, T>` is a struct over a hashed native map, not a plain object. And `List<T>` is a real JS array, which an array-*like* is not: `for`-in over `{0: "a", length: 1}` throws `TypeError: … is not iterable`, and a real array with holes hands `undefined` to a `T`-typed slot. So the three aggregate rows are diagnosed rather than mapped, and §3.8's "`external struct` always" recommendation stops being an ergonomic preference and becomes a correctness requirement. A tuple is the exception that proves it: `(A, B)` *is* a JS array, so `[A, B]` maps across exactly.

**The fourth is `Option`, and it is the one that would have hurt.** §3.2 maps every absence — `T | null`, `T | undefined`, `x?: T` — onto `Option<T>`. `Option` is a tagged array too: `Some(v)` is `[0, v]`, `None` is `[1]`. Reading, a host returning `"hello"` is tested as `value[0] === 0`, which is `"h" === 0`, so a **present value arrives as `None`**. Writing, `None` reaches the host as the array `[1]` — and for an optional `boolean` argument, `[1]` is truthy, so `arc(…)` quietly draws counterclockwise. `std` does use `Option` across `external` boundaries, but only ones it owns: compiler intrinsics and its own `__`-prefixed runtime helpers, which know the representation. A library's `.d.ts` does not. So nothing bindgen emits is ever an `Option`: a nullable type binds as the bare type with a `///` note, and an optional **parameter** becomes one binding per call arity — `getContext(id)` and `getContext(id, options)` are two real host calls, so they become two real bindings of the same symbol, which is exactly what `std` does by hand where `append` and `append_text` both bind `appendChild`.
